g\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\cap G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbb{C}).

Now we pick a lift
\widetilde{g}\in G_{\mathrm{sc}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
of
g\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. Since
G_{\mathrm{sc}}(\mathbb{R})
(which is connected by Cartan’s theorem) maps onto
G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
, we have
\widetilde{g}\in G_{\mathrm{sc}}(\mathbb{R})\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbb{C})
. The cocycle

\Gamma\ni\tau\mapsto\widetilde{g}^{-1}{\vphantom{\tau}}{}^{\tau}(\widetilde{g})

is then valued in
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
(since
g^{-1}{\vphantom{\tau}}{}^{\tau}g\in{\mathcal{I}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
), and represents a class in
\Sha_{G_{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{I}})
lifting
\beta
.
∎

Remark 1.2.7
.

In the setting of the above lemma, we in fact have
\Sha_{G_{\mathrm{sc}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{I}})=\Sha^{\infty}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{I}})
, because
\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},G_{\mathrm{sc}})=0
.

1.2.8
.

Let
I,G
be connected reductive groups over
\mathbb{Q}
, and let
(f,{\mathcal{W}})
be an inner transfer datum from
I
to
G
. Assume that
I
and
G
have the same absolute rank. For each
g\in{\mathcal{W}}
, we know that
\mathcal{I}_{g}
contains a maximal torus in
G
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, and in particular
\mathcal{I}_{g}
contains
Z_{G}
. Let
Z
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
Z_{G}
. Note that
f^{-1}(Z)
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
I
, and
f
induces a
\mathbb{Q}
-isomorphism
f^{-1}(Z)Z
. Let

\bar{I}:=I/f^{-1}(Z)
and
\bar{G}:=G/Z
. Then
(f,{\mathcal{W}})
induces an inner transfer datum
(\bar{f},\overline{{\mathcal{W}}})
from
\bar{I}
to
\bar{G}
. We use
(f,{\mathcal{W}})
to define
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I)
, and use
(\bar{f},\overline{{\mathcal{W}}})
to define
\Sha_{\bar{G}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\bar{I})

Corollary 1.2.9
.

In the setting of §
1.2.8
, the natural map
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I)\to\Sha_{\bar{G}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\bar{I})
is surjective.

Proof.

By picking an arbitrary element
g\in{\mathcal{W}}
and replacing
I
by
\mathcal{I}_{g}
, we reduce to the following situation:

•

I
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
G
containing a maximal torus in
G
,

•

f
is the inclusion,

•

{\mathcal{W}}
contains
1
.

Since
G_{\mathrm{sc}}=(\bar{G})_{\mathrm{sc}}
, and since the inverse image of
I
in
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
is equal to the inverse image of
\bar{I}
in
(\bar{G})_{\mathrm{sc}}
, the corollary immediately follows from Lemma
1.2.6
.
∎

Lemma 1.2.10
.

Let
F
be a field of characteristic zero. Let
I\rightarrow S
be a surjective homomorphism from a connected reductive group
I
to a torus
S
over
F
. Let
I^{\prime}
be the kernel.

(i)

We have
Z_{I}\cap I^{\prime}=Z_{I^{\prime}}
, and we have short exact sequences

1\to Z_{I^{\prime}}\to I^{\prime}\to I^{\ad}\to 1

and

1\to Z_{I^{\prime}}\to Z_{I}\to S\to 1.

(ii)

The maps

f_{1}:I(F)\rightarrow I^{\ad}(F)\xrightarrow{\delta^{1}}\coh^{1}(F,Z_{I^{\prime}})

and

f_{2}:I(F)\rightarrow S(F)\xrightarrow{\delta^{2}}\coh^{1}(F,Z_{I^{\prime}})

differ by a sign. Here the maps
\delta^{1},\delta^{2}
are the boundary maps induced by the short exact sequences in (i). In particular, every element of the image of
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
has trivial image in
\coh^{1}(F,I^{\prime})
and trivial image in
\coh^{1}(F,Z_{I})
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from the fact that
I^{\prime}
contains
I_{\mathrm{der}}
. For part (ii), let
i\in I(F)
and write
i=i_{0}i_{1}=i_{1}i_{0}
with
i_{0}\in I^{\prime}(\overline{F})
and
i_{1}\in Z_{I}(\overline{F}).

Then
f_{2}(i)
is represented by the cocycle

(i_{1}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}i_{1})_{\rho}
in
Z^{1}(F,Z_{I^{\prime}})
. Since
{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}i=i
, this cocycle equals

(i_{0}{\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}i_{0}^{-1})_{\rho}
, which represents
-f_{1}(i)
. ∎

Corollary 1.2.11
.

In Lemma
1.2.10
, take
F=\mathbb{Q}
, and take
I\to S
to be the natural map
I\to I^{\ab}
, so that
I^{\prime}=I_{\mathrm{der}}
. Then the image of
I(\mathbb{Q})_{+}
in
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{I_{\mathrm{der}}})
, under either of the maps
f_{1}
or
f_{2}
, is contained in
\Sha^{\infty}_{I_{\mathrm{der}}}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{I_{\mathrm{der}}})
.

Proof.

Using the notation of Lemma
1.2.10
, the image of
I^{\ad}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
under the boundary map
\delta^{1}:I^{\ad}(\mathbb{R})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{R},Z_{I_{\mathrm{der}}})
is trivial, because the image of
I_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})\to I^{\ad}(\mathbb{R})
contains
I^{\ad}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
. The corollary then follows from Lemma
1.2.10
.
∎

1.3.
The Kottwitz homomorphism

1.3.1
.

Let
F
be a field of characteristic
0
,
\overline{F}
an algebraic closure, and
\Gamma_{F}={\rm Gal}(\overline{F}/F).
Let
G
be a reductive group over
F.
Recall that, when
F
is complete, discretely valued, with algebraically closed residue field, the
Kottwitz homomorphism
is a homomorphism
G(F)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}

which is functorial in
G,
and for
G=\mathbb G_{m}
is the valuation map
F^{\times}\to\mathbb{Z}
. The original construction in
[
Kot97
, §7]
relies on Steinberg’s theorem for
F
.

Here we generalize the construction of the Kottwitz homomorphism. We shall obtain a homomorphism
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}:G(R)\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
, where
R
is any
F
-algebra (with
F
arbitrary) equipped with a discrete valuation
v
. This will allow us to show that the Kottwitz homomorphism
is constant in certain families.

1.3.2
.

Let
\mathsf{S}
be the big fpqc site of
\Spec F
. Let
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{S}hv(\mathsf{S})
be the category of abelian sheaves on
\mathsf{S}
. We shall view
{\mathrm{Mult}}(F)
and
{\mathrm{Tori}}(F)
(see §
1.1.1
) as full subcategories of
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{S}hv(\mathsf{S})
. Let
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{S})
be the derived category of
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{S}hv(\mathsf{S})
, and let
{\mathcal{D}}^{[-1,0]}(\mathsf{S})
be the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{S})
consisting of those
L\in\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathsf{S})
such that
\coh^{i}(L)=0
unless
i\in\left\{-1,0\right\}
.

We shall need the formalism of Picard stacks
4
4

4

We omit the adjective “strictly commutative”, as that will always be understood.
on
\mathsf{S}
, as in
[
AG73
, Exposé XVIII, §1.4]
. Following
loc. cit.
, let
\mathcal{C}h^{\flat}(\mathsf{S})
be the category whose objects are the small Picard stacks on
\mathsf{S}
, and whose morphisms are the isomorphism classes of additive functors between Picard stacks. By
[
AG73
, Exposé XVIII, Prop. 1.4.15]
, we have an equivalence of categories

\ch:{\mathcal{D}}^{[-1,0]}(\mathsf{S})\to\mathcal{C}h^{\flat}(\mathsf{S}).

For a complex
C^{\bullet}=(C^{-1}\to C^{0})
in
{\mathrm{Mult}}(F)
(at degrees
-1,0
),
\ch(C^{\bullet})
is given by the quotient stack
[C^{-1}\backslash C^{0}]
.

1.3.3
.

As in Proposition
1.1.2
, we have an equivalence of categories

\mathrm{R}X_{*}:\mathcal{D}^{b}({\mathrm{Mult}}(F))\to\mathcal{D}^{b}({\Gamma_{F}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}}).

We fix once and for all a quasi-inverse
\mathcal{Y}
of
\mathrm{R}X_{*}
, and natural isomorphisms
\epsilon:\mathrm{R}X_{*}\circ\mathcal{Y}\to\id
and
\eta:\id\to\mathcal{Y}\circ\mathrm{R}X_{*}
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over
F.
Let
\mathscr{Z}_{G}
be as in Definition
1.1.3
. By Proposition
1.1.4
, we have a canonical isomorphism
\mathrm{R}X_{*}(\mathscr{Z}_{G})\cong\pi_{1}(G)
in
\mathcal{D}^{b}({\Gamma_{F}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}})
. Let
\mathrm{pr}:\pi_{1}(G)\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
be the canonical map, viewed as a morphism in
\mathcal{D}^{b}({\Gamma_{F}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}})
. Let

\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F}:=\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}})\in\mathcal{D}^{b}({\mathrm{Mult}}(F)),

and let

\kappa_{0}:\mathscr{Z}_{G}\longrightarrow\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F}

be given by the composite

\mathscr{Z}_{G}\xrightarrow{\eta}\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{R}X_{*}(\mathscr{Z}_{G}))\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}(\mathrm{pr})}\mathcal{Y}(\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}})=\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F}.

Thus we have a canonical isomorphism

\epsilon:\mathrm{R}X_{*}(\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F})\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}.

Lemma 1.3.4
.

In
\mathcal{D}^{b}({\mathrm{Mult}}(F))
,
\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F}
is isomorphic to a complex of the form

T^{-1}\longrightarrow T^{0},

where
T^{-1}
and
T^{0}
are split tori over
F
and located at degrees
-1
and
0
. In particular, the image of
\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F}
in
{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{S})
lies in
{\mathcal{D}}^{[-1,0]}(\mathsf{S})
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
is isomorphic to a complex
L^{-1}\to L^{0}
in
\mathcal{D}^{b}({\Gamma_{F}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}})
, where
L^{-1}
and
L^{0}
are finite free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules with the trivial
\Gamma
-action.
∎

1.3.5
.

We write
\mathscr{K}^{F}_{G}
for
\ch(\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F})\in\mathcal{C}h^{\flat}(\mathsf{S})
, and call it the
Kottwitz stack
of
G
over
F
. The inclusions induce a canonical equivalence between quotient stacks
[Z_{G_{\mathrm{sc}}}\backslash Z_{G}]\to[G_{\mathrm{sc}}\backslash G]
. Thus we obtain a functor between stacks on
\mathsf{S}
:

\kappa_{G}^{\can}:G\longrightarrow[G_{\mathrm{sc}}\backslash G][Z_{G_{\mathrm{sc}}}\backslash Z_{G}]\cong\ch(\mathscr{Z}_{G})\xrightarrow{\ch(\kappa_{0})}\ch(\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F})=\mathscr{K}^{F}_{G},

which is canonical up to isomorphism.

For any small Picard category
P
(strictly commutative, as always), we denote by
\pi_{0}(P)
the set of isomorphism classes of
P
, which is naturally an abelian group. Then
\kappa_{G}^{\can}
induces a morphism

\pi_{0}(\kappa_{G}^{\can}):G\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathscr{K}^{F}_{G}(\cdot))

of presheaves in groups
5
5

5

We caution the reader that the right hand side is not a sheaf.
on
\mathsf{S}
.

1.3.6
.

Now choose
T^{\bullet}=(T^{-1}\to T^{0})
as in Lemma
1.3.4
, and choose an isomorphism
f:T^{\bullet}\mathscr{Z}_{G}^{F}
in
\mathcal{D}^{b}({\mathrm{Mult}}(F))
. If
R
is any
F
-algebra with
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R)=\{1\}
, then we have canonical isomorphisms of abelian groups

(1.3.6.1)

\pi_{0}\big(\ch(T^{\bullet})(R)\big)\cong T^{0}(R)/T^{-1}(R)\\
\cong(X_{*}(T^{0})/X_{*}(T^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R^{\times}\cong\coh^{0}(\mathrm{R}X_{*}(T^{\bullet}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R^{\times},

since
T^{-1}
is a split torus. In this case, consider the composite isomorphism:

\gamma_{R}:\pi_{0}(\mathscr{K}^{F}_{G}(R))\xrightarrow{f^{-1}}\pi_{0}\big(\ch(T^{\bullet})(R)\big)\cong\coh^{0}(\mathrm{R}X_{*}(T^{\bullet}))\otimes R^{\times}\\
\xrightarrow{f}\coh^{0}(\mathrm{R}X_{*}(\mathscr{Z}^{F}_{G}))\otimes R^{\times}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes R^{\times}.

Then
\gamma_{R}
is independent of the choice of
(T^{\bullet},f)
, by the functoriality of (
1.3.6.1
) in
T^{\bullet}
.

1.3.7
.

If
R
is a commutative ring, by a
discrete valuation on
R
, we mean a function
v:R\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
satisfying
v(0)=\infty
,
v(1)=0
,
v(ab)=v(a)+v(b)
, and
v(a+b)\geq\min(v(a),v(b))
, for all
a,b\in R
. (Here
\infty>n,\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and we do not require
v(a)=\infty\Rightarrow a=0
.)

Now consider an
F
-algebra
R
satisfying
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R)=\left\{1\right\}
, and a discrete valuation
v
on
R
. Composing the canonical map
\pi_{0}(\kappa_{G}^{\can})
in §
1.3.5
with the canonical map
\gamma_{R}
in §
1.3.6
, we obtain the canonical map

\kappa_{G}^{R}:G(R)\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}(\kappa_{G}^{\can})}\pi_{0}(\mathscr{K}_{G}^{F}(R))\xrightarrow{\gamma_{R}}\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes R^{\times},

which is a group homomorphism.
On composing the above with
v:R^{\times}\to\mathbb{Z}
, we obtain the group homomorphism

(1.3.7.1)

\displaystyle\kappa_{G}^{R,v}:G(R)\xrightarrow{\kappa_{G}^{R}}\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes R^{\times}\xrightarrow{v}\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}.

We now extend the definition of
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
, dropping the hypothesis
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R)=\left\{1\right\}
.

Definition 1.3.8
.

Let
R
be an arbitrary
F
-algebra, and let
v
be a discrete valuation on
R
. The elements
r\in R
with
v(r)=\infty

form a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
, and
v
factors as
R\to\mathrm{Frac\,}(R/\mathfrak{p})\xrightarrow{\bar{v}}\mathbb{Z}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
. We define
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
to be the composition

G(R)\longrightarrow G(\mathrm{Frac\,}(R/\mathfrak{p}))\xrightarrow{\kappa_{G}^{\mathrm{Frac\,}(R/\mathfrak{p}),\bar{v}}}\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}.

We call
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
the
Kottwitz homomorphism
. We often simply write
\kappa_{G}^{v}
for
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
.

1.3.9
.

In Definition
1.3.8
, if
R
satisfies
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R)=\left\{1\right\}
, one checks that the definition of
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
agrees with the previous definition (
1.3.7.1
). Moreover, the generally defined
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
(without the hypothesis
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R)=\left\{1\right\}
) is functorial in the pair
(R,v)
in the following sense. Let
R^{\prime}
be another
F
-algebra equipped with a discrete valuation
v^{\prime}
. Suppose there is an
F
-algebra map
h:R\to R^{\prime}
such that
v
is the pull-back of
v^{\prime}
along
h
. Then
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
equals the composition
G(R)\xrightarrow{h}G(R^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\kappa_{G}^{R^{\prime},v^{\prime}}}\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
. For fixed
(R,v)
, the homomorphism
\kappa_{G}^{R,v}
is also functorial in the reductive group
G
over
F
, i.e., it is a natural transformation between the functors
G\mapsto G(R)
and
G\mapsto\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
. Using this, one easily checks that
\kappa_{G}^{F,v}
agrees with Kottwitz’s original construction in
[
Kot97
, §7]
, in the special case when
(F,v)
is a complete discretely valued field with algebraically closed residue field.

Proposition 1.3.10
.

Let
R
be an
F
-algebra, and let
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
be a collection of discrete valuations on
R
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb Z
. Suppose that

(i)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}v_{i}(u)=0
for all
u\in R^{\times}
, and

(ii)

the group
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R)
is trivial.

Then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\kappa_{G}^{v_{i}}(h)=0
for all
h\in G(R).

Proof.

By condition (ii), each
\kappa_{G}^{v_{i}}
factors as in (
1.3.7.1
). Thus the map
\sum_{i}a_{i}\kappa_{G}^{v_{i}}
factors through the map
\id\otimes\sum a_{i}v_{i}:\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes R^{\times}\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
, which is zero by (i).
∎

Proposition 1.3.11
.

Let
R\supset F
be a domain, and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}

a collection of discrete valuations on
R
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb Z
. Suppose that
R=R^{\circ}[1/f_{j}]_{j=1}^{m},
where
R^{\circ}\subset R
is a subring and
f_{j}\in R^{\circ}
are non-zero prime elements, satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

The ring
R^{\circ}
is a noetherian locally factorial domain.

(ii)

For
i=1,\dots,n
, we have
v_{i}(R^{\circ})\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
.

(iii)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}v_{i}(f_{j})=0
for each
j=1,\ldots,m.

Then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\kappa^{v_{i}}_{G}(h)=0
for all
h\in G(R)
.

Proof.

Let
R^{\circ\prime}
be the ring obtained from
R^{\circ}
by inverting all elements
f

such that
v_{i}(f)=0
for all
i.
The conditions of the proposition continue to hold if we replace
R^{\circ}
(resp.
R
) by
R^{\circ\prime}
(resp.
R^{\circ\prime}[1/f_{j}]_{j=1}^{m}
), and omit from the list of
f_{j}
those elements such that
v_{i}(f_{j})=0
for all
i
(as they become units in
R^{\circ\prime}
). By the functoriality of the Kottwitz map as discussed in §
1.3.9
, we reduce to the case where
R^{\circ}=R^{\circ\prime}.
Then
R^{\circ}
is semi-local, as each proper ideal is contained in one of the prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\left\{x\in R^{\circ}\mid v_{i}(x)>0\right\},i=1,\dots,n
.

Since
R^{\circ}
is noetherian and locally factorial, the restriction map
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R^{\circ})\to{\rm Pic}(\Spec R)
is surjective (see
[
Gro67
, Cor. 21.6.11]
). Since
R^{\circ}
is semi-local, we have
{\rm Pic}(\Spec R^{\circ})={\rm Pic}(\Spec R)=\left\{1\right\}
.

Now since the
f_{j}
are prime in
R^{\circ}
, any unit in
R
has the form
u=wf_{1}^{e_{1}}\dots f_{m}^{e_{m}}
where

w\in R^{\circ\times},e_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}.
Since
v_{i}(w)=0
for all
i
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}v_{i}(u)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}v_{i}(f_{j})=0.

The proposition now follows from Proposition
1.3.10
.
∎

Corollary 1.3.12
.

Suppose that
F
is equipped with a discrete valuation
v_{F}:F\rightarrow\mathbb Z\cup\{\infty\}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{F},
and that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
extending
G.
Assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
has connected
fibers. Then
\kappa_{G}^{v_{F}}:G(F)\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
maps
{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\subset G(F)
to
\left\{0\right\}
.

Proof.

Let
R^{\circ}
be the affine ring of
{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
\pi_{F}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
be a uniformizer.
Our conditions imply that
\pi_{F}
is a prime element of the noetherian domain
R^{\circ},
and hence
R^{\circ}_{(\pi_{F})}
is a DVR. Let
v_{0}
be the pull-back to
R^{\circ}
of the canonical discrete valuation on
R^{\circ}_{(\pi_{F})}
. Let
g\in{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{O}_{F}).
We also consider the valuation
v_{g}
given by

R^{\circ}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{O}_{F}\xrightarrow{v_{F}}\mathbb{Z}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
. In the following we show that
\kappa_{G}^{v_{F}}(g)=0
.

Let
R=R^{\circ}[1/\pi_{F}]
.
Note that
v_{0}(\pi_{F})=v_{g}(\pi_{F})=1
. In particular,
v_{0}
and
v_{g}
extend to
R
. Since
R^{\circ}
and
v_{0},v_{g}
satisfy conditions (i) (ii) in Proposition
1.3.11
, and since
v_{0}(\pi_{F})-v_{g}(\pi_{F})=0
, we may apply that proposition to conclude that

\kappa^{v_{0}}_{G}(h)-\kappa_{G}^{v_{g}}(h)=0
for all
h\in G(R)
.
Applying this to
h=g_{u}
, where
g_{u}
is the universal point in
{\mathcal{G}}(R^{\circ})\subset G(R)
, we get

\kappa^{v_{0}}_{G}(g_{u})=\kappa_{G}^{v_{g}}(g_{u})=\kappa_{G}^{v_{F}}(g),

where the second equality follows from functoriality (§
1.3.9
).
This shows that
\kappa_{G}^{v_{F}}(g)
does not depend on
g\in G(R).

Hence it must be
0,
its value on the identity.
∎

Remark 1.3.13
.

In Corollary
1.3.12
, if
{\mathcal{G}}
is a parahoric group scheme (
[
HR08
]
), and if the discretely valued field
(F,v_{F})
is strictly henselian, then the conclusion follows from
[
HR08
, Prop. 3]
.

1.3.14
.

Keep the setting and notation of Corollary
1.3.12
. Assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is reductive,
and
F
is complete. Let
\pi_{F}\in F
be a uniformizer. Let
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathcal{G}}
be a maximal split torus. Then we have the Cartan–Iwahori–Matsumoto decomposition

G(F)=\bigcup_{\mu\in X_{*}({\mathcal{S}})}G(\mathcal{O}_{F})\mu(\pi_{F})G(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

(The union is not disjoint.) The decomposition in this generality is proved in
[
AHH19
, Thm. 1.3, Rmk. 3.5]
.

Corollary 1.3.15
.

Keep the setting of §
1.3.14
. If
g\in G(F)
belongs to the double coset indexed by
\mu\in X_{*}({\mathcal{S}}),
then
\kappa_{G}^{v_{F}}(g)=[\mu]
, where
[\mu]
is the image of
\mu
under the natural map
X_{*}({\mathcal{S}})=\pi_{1}({\mathcal{S}}_{F})\to\pi_{1}(G)\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{F}}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
1.3.12
, it suffices to show that
\kappa_{G}^{v_{F}}(\mu(\pi_{F}))=[\mu].
But this follows from the functoriality of the Kottwitz map in the group
G
. ∎

1.4.
Decent elements and twisting

Throughout this subsection, we fix a prime
p
, and denote by
\sigma
the arithmetic
p
-Frobenius in
\Aut(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

1.4.1
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. For
b\in G(\breve{\mathbb}{Q}_{p})
, we write
\nu_{b}
for the Newton cocharacter of
b
, which is a fractional cocharacter of
G_{\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
; see
[
Kot85
, §4]
and
[
RZ96
, §1.7]
. Following
[
RZ96
, Def. 1.8]
, we say that
b
is
decent
, if there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
n\nu_{b}
is a cocharacter of
G_{\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, and such that

(1.4.1.1)

\displaystyle b\sigma(b)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(b)=(n\nu_{b})(p).

In this case, we also say that
b
is
n
-decent
.

For any
n
-decent
b\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, it is shown in
[
RZ96
, Cor. 1.9]
that
b\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
, and that
\nu_{b}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}
. In particular, if
b
is
n
-decent, then it is also
n^{\prime}
-decent for
n|n^{\prime}
. Clearly the condition that an element of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
is
n
-decent is invariant under
\sigma
-conjugation by
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
. Conversely, if
b,b^{\prime}
are
n
-decent and if
b^{\prime}=gb\sigma(g)^{-1}
for some
g\in G(\breve{\mathbb}{Q}_{p})
, then necessarily
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
; see
[
RZ96
, Cor. 1.10]
.

1.4.2
.

We denote by
\B(G)
the set of
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. For
b\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we denote its class in
\B(G)
by
[b]
. We recall Kottwitz’s classification of elements of
\B(G)
. Let
\mathcal{N}(G)
denote the set of
\sigma
-stable
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-conjugacy classes of fractional cocharacters of
G_{\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
. The association
b\mapsto\nu_{b}
descends to the
Newton map

\bar{\nu}:\B(G)\to\mathcal{N}(G)
. As in §
1.3
, we have the Kottwitz homomorphism
w_{G}:G(\breve{\mathbb}{Q}_{p})\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p,0}}
associated with the
p
-adic valuation on
\breve{\mathbb}{Q}_{p}
. By
[
Kot97
, §7]
,
w_{G}
is surjective, and descends to a map
\kappa_{G}:\B(G)\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p}},
called the
Kottwitz map
. By
[
Kot97
, §4.13]
, the map

(\bar{\nu},\kappa_{G}):\B(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}(G)\times\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p}}

is injective.

It is proved by Kottwitz
[
Kot85
, §4.3]
(cf.
[
RZ96
, §1.11]
) that every
\sigma
-conjugacy class in
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is represented by a decent element. Thus
\B(G)
is in natural bijection with the set of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
-orbits in the set of decent elements of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
, where
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
acts by
\sigma
-conjugation.

1.4.3
.

Let
b\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. The functor sending any
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra
R
to the group

J_{b}(R):=\left\{g\in G(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\mid gb=b\sigma(g)\right\}

is represented by a reductive group
J_{b}
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We shall also write
J_{b}^{G}
for
J_{b}
, to make the presence of
G
explicit. If
b
is decent (so
b\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
), then by
[
RZ96
, Cor. 1.14]
, there is a canonical
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}
-isomorphism from
J_{b,\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}
to the centralizer
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}},\nu_{b}}
of
\nu_{b}
in
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}
. (In this case, for any
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}
-algebra
R
we have
J_{b}(R)\subset G(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
, and the embedding
J_{b,\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}\to G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}
is induced by the natural map
R\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}\to R
.)
In this case the action of
\sigma
on
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}},\nu_{b}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
with respect to the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-form
J_{b}
is given by
g\mapsto b\sigma(g)b^{-1}
, where
\sigma(\cdot)
is defined with respect to the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-form
G
.

If
b
is decent and if

(1.4.3.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}:=\left\{c\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})\mid c\nu_{b}c^{-1}\text{ is defined over }\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right\}\neq\emptyset,

then the canonical embedding
J_{b,\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}\to G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}
form an inner transfer datum from
J_{b}
to
G
(Definition
1.2.4
). We thus obtain a canonical map

(1.4.3.2)

\displaystyle\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b})\cong\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b})\to\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},G).

Note that for
b
decent, (
1.4.3.1
) holds in the following two cases:

(i)

G
is quasi-split over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

(ii)

b
is basic in
G
, i.e.,
\nu_{b}
is central.

Indeed, the
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
-conjugacy class of
\nu_{b}
is always stable under
{\rm Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. In case (i), this conjugacy class must contain a fractional cocharacter defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. In case (ii),
\nu_{b}
itself is already defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

We remark that in case (ii), the canonical inner transfer datum from
J_{b}
to
G
equips
J_{b}
with the structure of an inner form of
G
. The isomorphism class of this inner form of
G
(see Definition
1.2.2
) depends only on
[b]\in\B(G)
, not on the decent representative
b
.

1.4.4
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
b\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
be a decent element, and fix an element
\beta\in\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b})
. By Steinberg’s theorem,
\beta
is represented by a cocycle
(a_{\rho})_{\rho}\in Z^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}))
. Under the canonical isomorphism
J_{b,\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}},\nu_{b}}
, we view
a_{\sigma}\in J_{b}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
as an element of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
, and define
b^{\prime}:=a_{\sigma}b\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
. It is easy to see that the
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
b^{\prime}
in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}})
depends only on
b,\beta
, not on
(a_{\rho})_{\rho}
. We shall say that (the
\sigma
-conjugacy class of)
b^{\prime}
is the twist of
b
by
\beta
.

Proposition 1.4.5
.

In the setting of §
1.4.4
, we have
\nu_{b^{\prime}}=\nu_{b}
, and
b^{\prime}
is decent. Moreover, if (
1.4.3.1
) holds for
b
(e.g., if
G
is quasi-split), then
\kappa_{G}([b^{\prime}])-\kappa_{G}([b])
is equal to the image of
\beta
under

\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b})\to\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},G)\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p},\tors},

where the first map is (
1.4.3.2
), and the second isomorphism is as in Proposition
1.1.9
.

Proof.

Choose
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
to be divisible enough such that

(a_{\rho})_{\rho}\in Z^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})),

and such that
b
is
n
-decent. Using that
(a_{\rho})_{\rho}
is a cocycle, one shows by induction that

a_{\sigma^{i}}=b^{\prime}\sigma(b^{\prime})\cdots\sigma^{i-1}(b^{\prime})\sigma^{i-1}(b^{-1})\cdots\sigma(b^{-1})b^{-1},

for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Since
a_{\sigma^{n}}=1
, we have

b^{\prime}\sigma(b^{\prime})\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(b^{\prime})=b\sigma(b)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(b).

Since
b
is
n
-decent, the right hand side is equal to
p^{n\nu_{b}}
. By the characterization of
\nu_{b^{\prime}}
(see
[
Kot85
, §4]
), we conclude that
\nu_{b^{\prime}}=\nu_{b}
, and that
b^{\prime}
is
n
-decent.

We now prove the statement about
\kappa_{G}([b^{\prime}])-\kappa_{G}([b])
. Since (
1.4.3.1
) holds, we can replace
b
by a
\sigma
-conjugate in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
and assume that
\nu_{b}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Then we can replace
G
by
G_{\nu_{b}}
and reduce to the case where
\nu_{b}
is central. To finish the proof we only need to show that the image of
\beta
under the composite isomorphism
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b})\cong\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},J_{b})\cong\pi_{1}(J_{b})_{\Gamma_{p},\tors}
is equal to
\kappa_{J_{b}}(a_{\sigma}).
This follows from
[
Kot85
, Rmk. 5.7]
applied to
J_{b}
(cf.
[
RV14
, Rmk. 2.2 (iv)]
). ∎

1.5.
Shimura varieties and their cohomology

Definition 1.5.1
.

Let
H
be a locally profinite group admitting a countable neighborhood basis of the identity. Let
B
be a locally noetherian scheme. Let
S
be a
B
-scheme equipped with a right
H
-action via
B
-automorphisms. We say that the action is
admissible
if there exists a class
\mathscr{K}
of compact open subgroups of
H
satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

The class
\mathscr{K}
contains all sufficiently small compact open subgroups of
H
(i.e., all open subgroups of a fixed compact open subgroup).

(ii)

For
K\in\mathscr{K}
, the categorical quotient
S/K
in the category of
B
-schemes exists, and is smooth, separated, and of finite type over
B
.

(iii)

For
K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathscr{K}
of
H
such that
K_{1}\subset K_{2}
, the natural map
S/K_{1}\to S/K_{2}
is finite étale.

(iv)

The maps
S\to S/K
identify
S
with
\varprojlim_{K\in\mathscr{K}}S/K
.

1.5.2
.

Let
H
,
B
,
S
and
\mathscr{K}
be as in Definition
1.5.1
. For
K\in\mathscr{K}
, we write
S_{K}
for
S/K
.
Let
\ell
be a prime number invertible on
B
. The construction in
[
HT01
, §III.3]
can be generalized to define
\ell
-adic sheaves on
S_{K}
and the Hecke action on the cohomology. We explain this in the following.

Let
K,U\in\mathscr{K},
with
U
normal in
K
. The group
K/U
acts on
S_{U}
via
S_{K}
-automorphisms. Since
S_{K}=S_{U}/K
and since the map
S_{U}\to S_{K}
is finite étale, we know that
S_{U}\to S_{K}
is a Galois étale cover, and moreover
{\rm Gal}(S_{U}/S_{K})
is identified with the maximal quotient of
K/U
that acts faithfully on
S_{U}
, cf.
[
Gro03
, Exposé V, Prop. 3.1]
.

For each
K\in\mathscr{K}
, we define the profinite group

(1.5.2.1)

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}(S/S_{K}):=\varprojlim_{U\mathrel{\unlhd}K\text{ open}}{\rm Gal}(S_{U}/S_{K}).

Since there exists neighborhood basis of
1
in
H
consisting of countably many open normal subgroups
U_{i}
of
K
with

\cdots\subset U_{i}\subset U_{i-1}\subset\cdots U_{1}\subset K,

we have a presentation

(1.5.2.2)

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}(S/S_{K})\cong\varprojlim_{i}{\rm Gal}(S_{U_{i}}/S_{K}).

Thus we are in a special case of the general setting at the beginning of
[
HT01
, §III.2]
, with our
S_{K}
playing the role of
X
, and our
{\rm Gal}(S/S_{K})
playing the role of
\Gamma
. By the construction in
loc. cit.
, every continuous
{\rm Gal}(S/S_{K})
-representation
\rho
on a finite-dimensional
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector space gives rise to a lisse
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaf
{\mathscr{L}}_{\rho}
on
S_{K}
.

Note that for each
K\in\mathscr{K}
, the natural homomorphism
K\to{\rm Gal}(S/S_{K})
is surjective, which can be seen from (
1.5.2.2
) and the similar presentation
K\cong\varprojlim_{i}K/U_{i}
(using that the indexing set is countable). Now let
\xi
be a continuous representation of
H
on a finite-dimensional
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector space
W
. We make the following assumption on
\xi
:

•

For all sufficiently small
K\in\mathscr{K}
, the restriction
\xi|_{K}
factors through
{\rm Gal}(S/S_{K})
.

Given such a
\xi
, we may and shall shrink
\mathscr{K}
and assume that the above condition holds for all
K\in\mathscr{K}
. Then for each
K\in\mathscr{K}
we apply the previous construction to the representation of
{\rm Gal}(S/S_{K})
on
W
induced by
\xi
, and obtain a lisse
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaf on
S_{K}
, denoted by
{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K}
.

Consider
K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathscr{K}
and
g\in H
such that
g^{-1}K_{1}g\subset K_{2}
. The action of
g
on
S
induces a map
g:S_{K_{1}}\to S_{K_{2}}
. As on p. 96 of
[
HT01
]
, the actions of
g
on
S
and on
W
together define a morphism

\overrightarrow{g}^{*}:g^{*}{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K_{2}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K_{1}}

between
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaves on
S_{K_{1}}
. For any geometric point
x
of
B
, this induces a map

[g]_{K_{2},K_{1}}:\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{K_{2},x},{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K_{2}})\longrightarrow\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{K_{1},x},{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K_{1}}).

Define

\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{x},\xi):=\varinjlim_{K\in\mathscr{K}}\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{K,x},{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K}),

where the transition maps are given by
[1]_{K_{2},K_{1}}
for
K_{1}\subset K_{2}
. The maps
[g]_{K_{2},K_{1}}
for varying
g,K_{1},K_{2}
give rise to a left
H
-action on
\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{x},\xi)
, called the
Hecke action
. As on p. 97 of
[
HT01
]
,
\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{x},\xi)
is an admissible
H
-module over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
. Indeed, using the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence and the fact that the cohomology of a finite group acting on a
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector space vanishes in positive degrees, we see that for each
K\in\mathscr{K}
the natural map
\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{K,x},{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K})\to\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{x},\xi)
is injective, and its image is the subspace of
K
-invariants. Since
\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{K,x},{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K})
is finite-dimensional, we know that
\coh^{i}_{c}(S_{x},\xi)
is an admissible
H
-module.

1.5.3
.

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum with reflex field
E=E(G,X)\subset\mathbb{C}
. By the theory of canonical models due to Shimura
[
Shi63
,
Shi64
,
Shi65
,
Shi66
,
Shi67a
,
Shi67b
,
Shi70a
,
Shi70b
]
, Deligne
[
Del71
,
Del79
]
, Milne
[
Mil83
]
(cf.
[
Mil90a
]
), and Borovoi
[
Bor84
]
, we have a canonical
E
-scheme
\Sh=\Sh(G,X)
equipped with a right
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-action that is admissible in the sense of Definition
1.5.1
. The class
\mathscr{K}
as in Definition
1.5.1
can be taken to be the class of neat compact open subgroups of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
(as defined in
[
Pin90
, §0.6]
). For each
K\in\mathscr{K}
, we denote
\Sh/K
by
\Sh_{K}=\Sh_{K}(G,X)
. This is a smooth, quasi-projective
E
-scheme, whose analytification over
\mathbb{C}
is identified with the hermitian locally symmetric variety
G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash X\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K
.

If
G=T
is a torus, then
X
consists of a single
\mathbb{R}
-homomorphism
h:\mathbb S\to T_{\mathbb{R}}
, and
\Sh_{K}(\overline{E})
is identified with the finite set
\Sh_{K}(\mathbb{C})=T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K
. The action of
{\rm Gal}(\overline{E}/E)
on this finite set is given by the
reciprocity law
, which we now recall in order to fix the sign convention.
Let
\mu=\mu_{h}
6
6

6

The convention used here is the same as in
[
Del79
]
. If
T_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{R}}
and
h
is given by
h(z)=z^{p}\bar{z}^{q}
, then
\mu_{h}
is given by
\mu_{h}(z)=z^{-p}
.
, which is a cocharacter of
T
defined over
E
. Consider the composite homomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic groups

\displaystyle r(\mu)^{\alg}:\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}\mu}\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}T\xrightarrow{\N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}}T.

This induces a group homomorphism

\pi_{0}(E^{\times}\backslash\mathbb A_{E}^{\times})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A})).

Now the left hand side is identified with
{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}/E)
under the global Artin map (normalized geometrically, i.e., uniformizers correspond to geometric Frobenius elements at the finite places), while the right hand side admits a natural map to
T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K
(cf.
[
Del79
, §2.2.3]
). We thus obtain a group homomorphism

r:{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}/E)\longrightarrow T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K.

For
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(\overline{E}/E)
and
x\in\Sh_{K}(\overline{E})\cong T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K
, we have the
reciprocity law

\sigma(x)=r(\sigma)\cdot x.

This uniquely determines the
E
-scheme structure of
\Sh_{K}
. Note that the above reciprocity law differs from
[
Del79
]
in the sign of
\mu
. Thus the
E
-scheme which we call the canonical model for
(T,\left\{h\right\})
would be called the canonical model for
(T,\left\{h^{-1}\right\})
according to
loc. cit.
Our sign convention is the same as that used by Pink
[
Pin90
,
Pin92a
]
and Morel
[
Mor10
]
.

For general Shimura data, the canonical models are uniquely characterized by functoriality and the case of tori. According to our sign convention, the Siegel modular varieties classifying polarized abelian varieties are canonical models for the Siegel Shimura data as specified in
[
Kis10
, §2.1.5]
. (This is proved in
[
Del71
, Thm. 4.21]
.) The discrepancy between this fact and Deligne’s sign conventions in
[
Del79
]
was observed in
[
Mil90b
]
.

Definition 1.5.4
.

Let
T
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-torus. We denote by
T_{a}
be the maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-anisotropic subtorus of
T
(see

[
Spr98
, Prop. 13.2.4]
). We denote by
T_{ac}
the smallest
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
T_{a}
whose base change to
\mathbb{R}
contains the maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split subtorus of
T_{a,\mathbb{R}}
. (Clearly
T_{ac}
exists and is a torus.) We call
T_{ac}
the
anti-cuspidal part
of
T
. We say that
T
is
cuspidal
, if
T
has equal
\mathbb{Q}
-split rank and
\mathbb{R}
-split rank (cf.
[
Mor10
, Def. 3.1.1]
).

Lemma 1.5.5
.

Let
T
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-torus. The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

T
is cuspidal.

(ii)

T
is isogenous over
\mathbb{Q}
to the product of a split
\mathbb{Q}
-torus and a
\mathbb{Q}
-torus that is anisotropic over
\mathbb{R}
.

(iii)

T_{a}
is
\mathbb{R}
-anisotropic.

(iv)

T_{ac}
is trivial.

(v)

T(\mathbb{Q})
is discrete in
T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

(vi)

All arithmetic subgroups of
T(\mathbb{Q})
are finite.

(vii)

T
satisfies the following
Serre condition
(cf.
[
Kis17
, §3.5.6]
). Fix a complex conjugation
\iota\in{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
. For all
\tau\in{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
and all
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
, we have

(\tau-1)(\iota+1)\mu=(\iota+1)(\tau-1)\mu=0.

In general,
T_{ac}
is the smallest
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
S
of
T
such that
T/S
is cuspidal.

Proof.

The equivalence of (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv) follows from
[
Spr98
, Prop. 13.2.4]
. The equivalence of (i), (v), and (vi) is shown in
[
Gro99
, Prop. 1.4]
. We now show that (vii) is equivalent to the other conditions. Note that (vii) is invariant under isogeny over
\mathbb{Q}
, and is satisfied when
T
is is either split over
\mathbb{Q}
or anisotropic over
\mathbb{R}
. Hence (ii) implies (vii). Conversely, if (vii) holds, then every
\mu\in X_{*}(T)^{\iota=1}
is fixed by
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
, since
(\iota+1)\mu=2\mu
is fixed by
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
. This implies (i).

The last assertion in the lemma is clear since (i) and (iii) are equivalent.
∎

1.5.6
.

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum with reflex field
E
. Write
Z
for
Z_{G}
, and write
Z_{ac}
for
(Z^{0})_{ac}
. For each compact open subgroup
K\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we write
Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
for
Z(\mathbb{Q})\cap K
, and write
Z_{K}
for
Z(\mathbb{A}_{f})\cap K
. Here both intersections are inside
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

Lemma 1.5.7
.

Let
K\subset G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a neat compact open subgroup. Then
Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
is contained in
Z_{ac}(\mathbb{Q})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.5.5
,
Z^{0}/Z_{ac}
, all congruence subgroups of
(Z^{0}/Z_{ac})(\mathbb{Q})
are finite. Thus the same is true for all congruence subgroups of
(Z/Z_{ac})(\mathbb{Q})
. It follows that the image of
Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
in
(Z/Z_{ac})(\mathbb{Q})
is finite. But this image is also neat inside
(Z/Z_{ac})(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, so it is trivial.
∎

1.5.8
.

Fix a prime number
\ell
. Let
\xi
be an irreducible algebraic representation of
G
over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
.
We set
G^{c}:=G/Z_{ac}
, and assume that
\xi
factors through
G^{c}
.
7
7

7

In
[
Mil90a
, §III]
,
G^{c}
is defined to be
G/Z_{s}
, where
Z_{s}
is the maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-subtorus of
Z^{0}
that is
\mathbb{Q}
-anisotropic and
\mathbb{R}
-split. Note that it is assumed in
[
Mil90a
, §II, (2.1.4)]
that
Z^{0}
splits over a CM field. Under this assumption,
Z_{s}
is equal to
Z_{ac}
. In general, the two can be different.
In the following we construct
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaves on
\Sh_{K}
associated with
\xi
, for all sufficiently small
K
, by applying the general formalism in §
1.5.2
. This construction is well known. See for instance
[
Kot92b
, §6]
,
[
Pin92a
, §5]
,
[
HT01
, §3.2]
, and
[
LS18
, §3]
, which give this construction at different levels of generality. Note that in all but the last reference, the Shimura varieties being considered satisfy
G=G^{c}
.

Let
K
and
U
be neat compact open subgroups of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, with
U
normal in
K
. Since each neat congruence subgroup
\Lambda
of
G(\mathbb{Q})
acts on
X
with kernel
\Lambda\cap Z(\mathbb{Q})
, we have

(1.5.8.1)

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}(\Sh_{U}/\Sh_{K})=K/(Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}U).

Write
Z(\mathbb{Q})^{-}
for the closure of
Z(\mathbb{Q})
in
Z(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, and write
Z(\mathbb{Q})^{-}_{K}
for the intersection
Z(\mathbb{Q})^{-}\cap K
inside
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Note that
Z(\mathbb{Q})^{-}_{K}
is also the closure of
Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
inside
K
, since
K
is open and closed in
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Define
{\rm Gal}(\Sh/\Sh_{K})
as in (
1.5.2.1
). By (
1.5.8.1
), we have

(1.5.8.2)

\displaystyle{\rm Gal}(\Sh/\Sh_{K})\cong K/Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}^{-},

(cf.
[
Del79
, §2.1.9]
). By Lemma
1.5.7
, the natural map
K\to G^{c}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
factors through
{\rm Gal}(\Sh/\Sh_{K})
.
8
8

8

In fact, the induced map
{\rm Gal}(\Sh/\Sh_{K})\cong K/Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}^{-}\to G^{c}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
is never injective, if
Z_{ac}
is non-trivial. This follows from the fact that
Z_{ac}(\mathbb{Q})(K\cap Z_{ac}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))
has finite index in
Z_{ac}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
(
[
Bor63
, Thm. 5.1]
), and the fact that
Z_{ac}(\mathbb{Q})^{-}
has infinite index in
Z_{ac}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
(
[
PR94
, Prop. 7.13(2)]
). In
[
Mil90a
, §III, Rmk. 6.1]
it is incorrectly stated that
{\rm Gal}(\Sh/\Sh_{K})
is isomorphic to the image of
K
in
G^{c}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, cf. §3 of the updated online version of
[
LS18
]
and its erratum.

Via the projection
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})\to G(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
, we obtain a continuous representation of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
on a finite-dimensional
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector space induced by
\xi
. This continuous representation satisfies the assumption in §
1.5.2
, namely its restriction to
K
factors through
{\rm Gal}(\Sh/\Sh_{K})
for all sufficiently small (in fact, all neat)
K
. By the construction in §
1.5.2
, we obtain a lisse
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-sheaf
{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K}
on
\Sh_{K}
for all neat
K
, and obtain the admissible
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-module

\coh^{i}_{c}(\Sh_{\overline{E}},\xi):=\varinjlim_{K}\coh^{i}_{c}(\Sh_{K,\overline{E}},{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi,K}).

We have a natural continuous
{\rm Gal}(\overline{E}/E)
-action on
\coh^{i}_{c}(\Sh_{\overline{E}},\xi)
that commutes with the
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-action. Our main interest is to understand the virtual
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})\times{\rm Gal}(\overline{E}/E)
-module

\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\coh^{i}_{c}(\Sh_{\overline{E}},\xi).

1.6.
Kottwitz parameters

1.6.1
.

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum, and let
p
be a prime number.
In this subsection we define
Kottwitz parameters
with respect to
(G,X)
and
p
, generalizing the considerations in
[
Kot90
, §2]
where
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is assumed to be simply connected.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{C}
be the reflex field of
(G,X)
. From the fixed embeddings
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, we obtain a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
E
over
p
. Let
p^{r}
be the cardinality of the residue field of
\mathfrak{p}
. Fix a positive multiple
n
of
r
.

The Hodge cocharacters attached to
h\in X
all have the same image in
\pi_{1}(G)
, which we denote by
[\mu]_{X}\in\pi_{1}(G)
.

We say that an element
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q})
is
semi-simple and
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic
, if
\epsilon\in T(\mathbb{R})
for some elliptic maximal torus
T
in
G_{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
G
is part of a Shimura datum,
G^{\ad}_{\mathbb{R}}
admits a Cartan involution. Therefore
G_{\mathbb{R}}
contains elliptic maximal tori, and our definition of
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic elements agrees with the more general definition in the literature, cf.
[
Kot86
, §9.1]
.

Definition 1.6.2
.

A
classical Kottwitz parameter of degree
n
(with respect to
(G,X)
and
p
) is a triple

(\gamma_{0},\gamma=(\gamma_{l})_{l\neq p},\delta)\in G(\mathbb{Q})\times G(\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\times G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}),

satisfying the following conditions.

CKP1:

The element
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q})
is semi-simple and
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic.

CKP2:

For each prime
l\neq p
,
\gamma_{l}
is stably conjugate to
\gamma_{0}
as elements of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{l})
.

CKP3:

The image of
\gamma_{0}
in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a degree
n
norm of
\delta
(see
[
Kot82
, §5]
).

CKP4:

The image of
\delta
under the Kottwitz map
\kappa_{G}:\B(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p}}
is equal to the image of
-[\mu]_{X}
.

We denote by
{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})
the set of classical Kottwitz parameters of degree
n
.

1.6.3
.

We define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
G(\mathbb{Q})\times G(\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\times G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
by declaring
(\gamma_{0},\gamma,\delta)\sim({\gamma_{0}}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

The elements
\gamma_{0}
and
{\gamma_{0}}^{\prime}
are stably conjugate in
G(\mathbb{Q})
.

•

The elements
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are conjugate in
G(\mathbb A_{f}^{p})
.

•

The elements
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
are
\sigma
-conjugate in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
.

The subset
{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})\subset G(\mathbb{Q})\times G(\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\times G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
is stable under
\sim
.

Definition 1.6.4
.

A
Kottwitz parameter
(with respect to
(G,X)
and
p
) is a tuple
(\gamma_{0},a,[b])
, consisting of:

•

a semi-simple and
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic element
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q})
,

•

an element
a\in\mathfrak{D}(G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0},G;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
,

•

a
\sigma
-conjugacy class
[b]
in
G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
(i.e.,
[b]\in\B(G_{\gamma_{0},\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{0})
),

satisfying the following condition.

KP0:

Let
[b]_{G}
be the image of
[b]
in
\B(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. Then the element
\kappa_{G}([b]_{G})\in\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p}}
is equal to the image of
-[\mu]_{X}
.

We denote by
{\mathfrak{KP}}
the set of all Kottwitz parameters.
For
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},a,[b])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}
, we write
I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})
for
G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
. When
{\mathfrak{c}}
is fixed in the context we also simply write
I_{0}
for
I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})
.

Definition 1.6.5
.

We say that
(\gamma_{0},a,[b])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}
is
p^{n}
-admissible
, if it satisfies the following condition.

KP1:

Let
b\in I_{0}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
be a representative of
[b]
.
There exists
c\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that, letting
\delta:=c^{-1}b\sigma(c)\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we have

(1.6.5.1)

\displaystyle c^{-1}\gamma_{0}c=\delta\cdot\sigma(\delta)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(\delta).

(Clearly this condition is independent of the representative
b
of
[b]
.) We denote by
{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})
the set of
p^{n}
-admissible Kottwitz parameters.

1.6.6
.

Next we deduce some consequences of the condition
KP1
. We shall work in a local setting as follows. Let
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})_{\mathrm{ss}}
and let
I_{0}:=(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
. Let
[b]\in\B(I_{0})
, and assume that
KP1
holds for
[b]
and
\gamma_{0}
.

Lemma 1.6.7
.

Keep the setting of §
1.6.6
. Choose
b
and
c
as in
KP1
with respect to
[b]
and
\gamma_{0}
. Let
\delta=c^{-1}b\sigma(c)
. Then we have
\delta\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
. The
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
\delta
in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
depends only on
[b]\in\B(I_{0})
, not on the choices of
b
and
c
. Moreover,
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a degree
n
norm of
\delta\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
.

Proof.

By (
1.6.5.1
) we have
(\delta\rtimes\sigma)^{n}=c^{-1}\gamma_{0}c\rtimes\sigma^{n}
. Since
b
and
1\rtimes\sigma
both commute with
\gamma_{0}
, we know that
\delta\rtimes\sigma=c^{-1}(b\rtimes\sigma)c
commutes with
c^{-1}\gamma_{0}c
. Hence
\delta\rtimes\sigma
commutes with
\sigma^{n}
, which means that
\delta\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
.

To prove the second statement, we first note that when
b
is fixed, any choice of
c
has to satisfy the equation

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}c=b\sigma(b)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(b)\sigma^{n}(c).

Hence two choices of
c
give rise to the same coset
cG(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
. It follows that the
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
\delta
in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
is independent of the choice of
c
. Now suppose we choose another representative
b^{\prime}\in I_{0}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
of
[b]\in\B(I_{0})
. Then
b^{\prime}=db\sigma(d)^{-1}
for some
d\in I_{0}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. Letting
c^{\prime}:=dc
, we have

c^{\prime-1}b^{\prime}\sigma(c^{\prime})=\delta,
and

c^{\prime-1}\gamma_{0}c^{\prime}=c^{-1}\gamma_{0}c=\delta\sigma(\delta)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(\delta).

Hence
b^{\prime}
still determines the same
\delta\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
up to
\sigma
-conjugacy.

Finally, we show that
\gamma_{0}
is a degree
n
norm of
\delta
. If
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is simply connected, then the statement follows from (
1.6.5.1
) and the definition of norm. In general, take a
z
-extension
1\to Z\to H\to G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to 1
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
\widetilde{c}\in H(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
be a lift of
c\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, and let
\widetilde{\delta}\in H(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
be a lift of
\delta\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
. Define
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\in H(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
by the equation

(1.6.7.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}^{-1}\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\widetilde{c}=\widetilde{\delta}\sigma(\widetilde{\delta})\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(\widetilde{\delta}).

Then
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
is a lift of
\gamma_{0}
. We claim that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\in H(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Once the claim is proved, we know that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
is a degree
n
norm of
\widetilde{\delta}
by (
1.6.7.1
), and it follows that
\gamma_{0}
is a degree
n
norm of
\delta
(see
[
Kot82
, §5]
).

It remains to prove the claim. Let
\widetilde{b}=\widetilde{c}\widetilde{\delta}\sigma(\widetilde{c})^{-1}\in H(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. By (
1.6.7.1
) we have
(\widetilde{\delta}\rtimes\sigma)^{n}=\widetilde{c}^{-1}\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\widetilde{c}\rtimes\sigma^{n}
. Since
\widetilde{\delta}\rtimes\sigma
commutes with
\sigma^{n}
(i.e.,
\widetilde{\delta}\in H(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
), it must commute with
\widetilde{c}^{-1}\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\widetilde{c}
. Hence
\widetilde{b}\rtimes\sigma=\widetilde{c}(\widetilde{\delta}\rtimes\sigma)\widetilde{c}^{-1}
commutes with
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
. On the other hand since
\widetilde{b}
is a lift of
b\in I_{0}
, we know that
\widetilde{b}
commutes with
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
by
[
Kot82
, Lem. 3.1 (1)]
. Therefore
\sigma
commutes with
\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}
, which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 1.6.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with connected fibers. Then every element of
{\mathcal{H}}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})/{\mathcal{H}}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
is fixed by some power of
\sigma
. The natural map
{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})/{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})\to{\mathcal{H}}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})/{\mathcal{H}}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
is a bijection.

Proof.

By
[
Bro13
, Lem. 3.2]
, there exists a closed embedding of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-groups
{\mathcal{H}}\to{\rm GL}_{n}
. Hence the first statement in the lemma reduces to the case where
{\mathcal{H}}={\rm GL}_{n}
, and it follows from the fact that
{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
is dense in
{\rm GL}_{n}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
under the
p
-adic topology. The second statement (for general
{\mathcal{H}}
) follows from the first statement and Greenberg’s theorem
[
Gre63
, Prop. 3]
asserting the surjectivity of the map
{\mathcal{H}}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})\to{\mathcal{H}}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p}),g\mapsto g\cdot\sigma^{n}(g)^{-1}
for arbitrary
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
∎

Lemma 1.6.9
.

Keep the setting of §
1.6.6
. Let
b\in I_{0}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
be a decent representative of
[b]\in\B(I_{0})
(see §
1.4.2
). Then there exists
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
b
is
t
-decent, and such that

(1.6.9.1)

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}^{t}=p^{nt\nu_{b}}k,

where
k
lies in a bounded subgroup of
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
(in the sense of
[
Tit79
, §2.2]
).

Proof.

Assume
b
is
t_{0}
-decent. Let
c
be as in
KP1
. Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a parahoric model of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and write
\bar{c}
for the image of
c
in
\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})/{\mathcal{G}}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
. By (
1.6.5.1
), we have
\gamma_{0}\cdot\bar{c}=(b\rtimes\sigma)^{n}\cdot\bar{c}.
Since
\gamma_{0}
commutes with
b
and
1\rtimes\sigma
, for any multiple
t
of
t_{0}
we have

\gamma_{0}^{t}\cdot\bar{c}=(b\rtimes\sigma)^{nt}\cdot\bar{c}=(p^{nt\nu_{b}}\rtimes\sigma^{nt})\cdot\bar{c}.

By Lemma
1.6.8
, when
t
is sufficiently divisible we have
\sigma^{nt}\bar{c}=\bar{c}
. Then
k:=p^{-nt\nu_{b}}\gamma_{0}^{t}
lies in
c{\mathcal{G}}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})c^{-1}
, which is a bounded subgroup of
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.∎

One can view (
1.6.9.1
) as a “polar decomposition” of
\gamma_{0}^{t}
. Such a decomposition satisfies a very strong sense of uniqueness, as specified in the following lemma.

Lemma 1.6.10
.

Let
F
be a complete discretely valued field. Let
H
be a linear algebraic group over
F
, and let
\epsilon
be a semi-simple element of
H(\overline{F})
. Then there exists at most one cocharacter
\nu
of
H_{\epsilon}
defined over
\overline{F}
such that for some uniformizer
\pi\in F^{\times}
,
\epsilon^{-1}\pi^{\nu}
lies in a
H(\overline{F})
-conjugate of a bounded subgroup of
H(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\rho:H\to{\rm GL}_{N}
be a faithful representation of
H
over
F
. If
k
is an element of a bounded subgroup of
H(F)
, then
\rho(k)
lies in a
{\rm GL}_{N}(F)
-conjugate of
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
(cf. the proof of
[
Kis10
, Lem. 2.3.1]
), and hence all eigenvalues of
\rho(k)
have valuation zero. (Here and below, by eigenvalues we always mean eigenvalues in
\overline{F}
.) To prove the lemma it suffices to prove that for each semi-simple
\epsilon\in{\rm GL}_{N}(\overline{F})
, there exists at most one cocharacter
\nu
of
{\rm GL}_{N,\epsilon,\overline{F}}
such that for some uniformizer
\pi\in F
all eigenvalues of
\epsilon^{-1}\pi^{\nu}
have valuation zero.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
\epsilon=\diag(\lambda_{1}I_{N_{1}},\cdots,\lambda_{r}I_{N_{r}})
with distinct
\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{r}\in\overline{F}^{\times}
. Then
{\rm GL}_{N,\epsilon}
is naturally identified with
{\rm GL}_{N_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\rm GL}_{N_{r}}
. Note that if a semi-simple element
k\in{\rm GL}_{N,\epsilon}(\overline{F})
is such that all its eigenvalues have valuation zero, then the projection of
k
in each
{\rm GL}_{N_{i}}(\overline{F})
satisfies the analogous condition. We have thus reduced to the case where
\epsilon
is central in
{\rm GL}_{N}(\overline{F})
. In this case, if

\nu
is a cocharacter of
{\rm GL}_{N,\overline{F}}
such that for some uniformizer
\pi
all eigenvalues of
\epsilon^{-1}\pi^{\nu}
have valuation zero, then
\nu
must be given by
z\mapsto\diag(z^{m},\cdots,z^{m})
where
m
is the valuation of the unique eigenvalue of
\epsilon
. This proves the uniqueness of
\nu
. ∎

Corollary 1.6.11
.

Keep the setting of §
1.6.6
. Then
[b]
is basic in
\B(I_{0})
. If
[b^{\prime}]\in\B(I_{0})
is another class satisfying
KP1
with respect to
\gamma_{0}
, then
\nu_{b}=\nu_{b^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
b
be a decent representative of
[b]
. By Lemma
1.6.9
and Lemma
1.6.10
(the latter applied to
F=\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
,
\pi=p
,
H=G_{\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
,
\epsilon=\gamma_{0}
), any element of
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
that centralizes
\gamma_{0}
has to centralize
\nu_{b}
. Therefore
\nu_{b}
factors through the center of
I_{0}
, and
[b]
is basic in
\B(I_{0})
. The second statement also follows from these two lemmas.
∎

Corollary 1.6.12
.

Let
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},a,[b])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}(p^{n})
. Then
[b]
is basic in
\B(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. If
(\gamma_{0},a^{\prime},[b^{\prime}])
is another element of
{\mathfrak{KP}}(p^{n})
, then
\nu_{b}=\nu_{b^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
1.6.11
.
∎

1.6.13
.

Our next goal is to define the notion of an isomorphism between Kottwitz parameters. We first consider a general construction. Let
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q})_{\mathrm{ss}}
, and let
I_{0}:=G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
. Let
u\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
be an element satisfying

\gamma_{0}^{\prime}:=u\gamma_{0}u^{-1}\in G(\mathbb{Q})

and

u^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}u\in I_{0},\quad\forall\rho\in\Gamma={\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}).

Let
I_{0}^{\prime}:=G_{\gamma_{0}^{\prime}}^{0}
.
We have a bijection

(1.6.13.1)

\displaystyle u_{*}:\mathfrak{D}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\mathfrak{D}(I_{0}^{\prime},G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p}),

sending the class of a cocycle
(a_{\rho})_{\rho}\in Z^{1}(\Gamma,I_{0}(\bar{\mathbb}{A}_{f}^{p}))
to the class of
(ua_{\rho}{\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}u^{-1})_{\rho}
.
Next note that the cocycle
(u^{-1}{\vphantom{u}}{}^{\rho}u)_{\rho}\in Z^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},I_{0})
becomes trivial in
\coh^{1}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p},I_{0})
by Steinberg’s theorem. Hence there exists
d\in I_{0}(\overline{\breve{\mathbb}{Q}}_{p})
such that

\displaystyle u^{-1}{\vphantom{u}}{}^{\rho}u=d^{-1}{\vphantom{d}}{}^{\rho}d,\quad\forall\rho\in\Gamma_{p,0}.

Then
u_{0}:=ud^{-1}
lies in
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. We have

\displaystyle u_{0}\gamma_{0}u_{0}^{-1}=\gamma^{\prime}_{0},

and

\displaystyle u_{0}^{-1}{\vphantom{u}}{}^{\sigma}u_{0}\in I_{0}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}).

By the previous two properties of
u_{0}
, we have a bijection

(1.6.13.2)

\displaystyle u_{*}:\B(I_{0,\mathbb{Q}_{p}})

\displaystyle\B(I^{\prime}_{0,\mathbb{Q}_{p}})

\displaystyle[b]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[u_{0}b\sigma(u_{0})^{-1}],

which depends only on
u
, not on the choice of
d
.

Definition 1.6.14
.

Let
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},a,[b]),{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}=(\gamma^{\prime}_{0},a^{\prime},[b^{\prime}])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}
. By an
isomorphism from
{\mathfrak{c}}
to
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
, we mean an element
u\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
, satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

We have
\Int(u)\gamma_{0}=\gamma^{\prime}_{0}
, and
u^{-1}{\vphantom{u}}{}^{\rho}u\in I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})
for all
\rho\in\Gamma
.

(ii)

The bijection
u_{*}:\mathfrak{D}(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}),G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\mathfrak{D}(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}),G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p})
as in (
1.6.13.1
) sends
a
to
a^{\prime}
.

(iii)

The bijection
u_{*}:\B(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\B(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
as in (
1.6.13.2
) sends
[b]
to
[b^{\prime}]
.

In such a situation we write
u:{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}.

1.6.15
.

If
u:{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
and
v:{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime\prime}
are two isomorphisms between Kottwitz parameters, then
vu\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
is an isomorphism
{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime\prime}
, and
u^{-1}\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
is the isomorphism
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}
inverse to
u
. Moreover, one checks that
p^{n}
-admissibility of Kottwitz parameters (Definition
1.6.5
) is preserved under isomorphisms.

We denote by
{\mathfrak{KP}}/{\cong}
the set of isomorphism classes of Kottwitz parameters, and by
{{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})}/{\cong}
the set of isomorphism classes of
p^{n}
-admissible Kottwitz parameters.

1.6.16
.

We define a natural map

(1.6.16.1)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})/{\sim}

as follows. Let
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},a,[b])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})
. The element
a\in\mathfrak{D}(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}),G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p})
determines a conjugacy class in
G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
which is stably conjugate to
\gamma_{0}
. Take
\gamma
to be an arbitrary element of this conjugacy class. By Lemma
1.6.7
,
[b]
determines a
\sigma
-conjugacy class in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
, of which
\gamma_{0}
is a degree
n
norm. Take
\delta
to be an arbitrary element of this
\sigma
-conjugacy class. Then
(\gamma_{0},\gamma,\delta)
is an element of
{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})
, and its equivalence class depends only on
{\mathfrak{c}}
. We define the map (
1.6.16.1
) by sending
{\mathfrak{c}}
to the equivalence class of
(\gamma_{0},\gamma,\delta)
.

One checks that the map (
1.6.16.1
) factors through
{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})/{\cong}
. Moreover, when
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is simply connected, the induced map
{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})/{\cong}~\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})/{\sim}
is a bijection. We will not need this fact in the paper, but we outline here how to recover
[b]\in\B(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
from
\delta
, which is perhaps the only non-obvious part of the argument. Since
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is simply connected,
I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})=G_{\gamma_{0}}
. Since
\gamma_{0}
is a degree
n
norm of
\delta
and since
\coh^{1}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p},I_{0})
is trivial by Steinberg’s theorem, there exists
c\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that (
1.6.5.1
) holds. Define
b:=c\delta\sigma(c)\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. Since
(\delta\rtimes\sigma)^{n}=c^{-1}\gamma_{0}c\rtimes\sigma^{n}
and since
\delta\rtimes\sigma
commutes with
\sigma^{n}
, we know that
\delta\rtimes\sigma
commutes with
c^{-1}\gamma_{0}c
, or equivalently that
b\rtimes\sigma
commutes with
\gamma_{0}
. Since
\gamma_{0}
is
\sigma
-invariant, we know that
b
commutes with
\gamma_{0}
, i.e.,
b\in I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. In this way we have recovered
[b]\in\B(I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
from
\delta
.

1.7.
The Kottwitz invariant

1.7.1
.

In this subsection we define an invariant attached to each Kottwitz parameter. We first construct the abelian group where the invariant lies in. We start with a general setting.

Let
G
be a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
, and let
I
be a reductive subgroup of
G
. We have an infinite commutative diagram with exact rows and columns

\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\to\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I\to G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i+1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\to\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\to\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb A,I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb A,G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb A,I\to G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i+1}_{\ab}(\mathbb A,I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\to\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\to\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q},G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i}_{\ab}(\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q},I\to G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i+1}_{\ab}(\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\to\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\to\coh^{i+1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i+1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i+1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I\to G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{i+2}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\to\cdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

We define
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q})
to be the cokernel of the map

\coh^{0}_{\ab}(\mathbb A,G)\longrightarrow\coh^{0}_{\ab}(\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q},I\to G)

given by the dashed arrow in the above diagram for
i=0
. We have a natural map
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb A)\to\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q})
defined by first lifting an element of
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb{A})
to
\coh^{0}_{\ab}(\mathbb{A},I\to G)
, and then mapping the lift to
\coh^{0}_{\ab}(\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q},I\to G)
and then to
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q})
. We know that the sequence

(1.7.1.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb{Q})\to\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb{A})\to\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q})

is exact; see
[
Lab99
, Prop. 1.8.4]
.

We write
K
for the kernel of
\pi_{1}(I)\to\pi_{1}(G)
. Recall that the functor
{\mathcal{A}}{Q}
is introduced in §
1.1.8
. As usual we write
\Gamma
for
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
.

Lemma 1.7.2
.

The natural inclusions

\displaystyle\ker\!\bigg(\mathcal{A}{Q}(K)\to\mathcal{A}{Q}(\pi_{1}(I))\bigg)\hookrightarrow\ker\!\bigg(K_{\Gamma,\tors}\to\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma,\tors}\bigg)\hookrightarrow\ker\!\bigg(K_{\Gamma}\to\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma}\bigg)

are equalities.

Proof.

As in the proof of
[
Mil92
, Prop. B.4]
, there exists a finite Galois extension
E/\mathbb{Q}
such that the actions of
\Gamma
on
K
,
\pi_{1}(I)
, and
\pi_{1}(G)
all factor through
{\rm Gal}(E/\mathbb{Q})
and such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}(K)

\displaystyle=\tatecoh^{-1}({\rm Gal}(E/\mathbb{Q}),K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\pi_{1}(I))

\displaystyle=\tatecoh^{-1}({\rm Gal}(E/\mathbb{Q}),\pi_{1}(I)).

Thus the first group in the lemma is equal to the kernel of

\tatecoh^{-1}({\rm Gal}(E/\mathbb{Q}),K)\longrightarrow\tatecoh^{-1}({\rm Gal}(E/\mathbb{Q}),\pi_{1}(I)).

From this, it is clear that the first and third groups in the lemma are both canonically identified with the cokernel of

\tatecoh^{-2}({\rm Gal}(E/\mathbb{Q}),\pi_{1}(I))\longrightarrow\tatecoh^{-2}({\rm Gal}(E/\mathbb{Q}),\pi_{1}(G)).

The lemma follows.
∎

Proposition 1.7.3
.

Assume that
I
contains a maximal torus in
G
. Then the abelian group
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q})
is canonically identified with

\frac{K_{\Gamma,\tors}}{\bigoplus_{v}\ker(K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\to\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v}})}.

Here
v
runs through all the places of
\mathbb{Q}
, and the quotient is with respect to the natural maps
K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\to K_{\Gamma,\tors}
. In particular,
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q})
is finite.

Proof.

Under our assumption, the map
\pi_{1}(I)\to\pi_{1}(G)
is surjective. Applying Proposition
1.1.9
(ii), we know that
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q})
is canonically identified with the cokernel of

\mathscr{P}(K):\ker\big(\mathcal{B}{Q}(K)\to\mathcal{B}{Q}(\pi_{1}(I))\big)\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}{Q}(K).

By Lemma
1.7.2
, we have

\ker\big(\mathcal{B}{Q}(K)\to\mathcal{B}{Q}(\pi_{1}(I))\big)=\bigoplus_{v}\ker(K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\to\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v}}).

The corollary follows.
∎

For any Hausdorff locally compact abelian group
H
, we denote by
H^{D}
the Pontryagin dual. The following result is well known to experts, cf.
[
Lab99
, p. 43, Remarque]
.

Corollary 1.7.4
.

Under the assumption in Proposition
1.7.3
, the abelian group
\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q})
is canonically identified with
\mathfrak{K}(I/\mathbb{Q})^{D}
, where
\mathfrak{K}(I/\mathbb{Q})
is defined in
[
Kot86
, §4.6]
.

Proof.

We shall freely use the definitions and results in
[
Kot86
]
. Recall that
\mathfrak{K}(I/\mathbb{Q})
is defined as the subgroup of
\pi_{0}([Z(\widehat{I})/Z(\widehat{G})]^{\Gamma})
consisting of those elements whose images in
\coh^{1}(F,Z(\widehat{G}))
are locally trivial.

Since
X^{*}(Z(\widehat{I}))\cong\pi_{1}(I)
and
X^{*}(Z(\widehat{G}))\cong\pi_{1}(G)
, we have
X^{*}(Z(\widehat{I})/Z(\widehat{G}))\cong K
. Hence

X^{*}\bigg(\pi_{0}([Z(\widehat{I})/Z(\widehat{G})]^{\Gamma})\bigg)\cong K_{\Gamma,\tors}.

Also, for each place
v
, we have

X^{*}\bigg(\pi_{0}(Z(\widehat{I})^{\Gamma_{v}})\bigg)\cong\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}

and

X^{*}\bigg(\pi_{0}([Z(\widehat{I})/Z(\widehat{G})]^{\Gamma_{v}})\bigg)\cong K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}.

By the exact sequence

\pi_{0}(Z(\widehat{I})^{\Gamma_{v}})\to\pi_{0}([Z(\widehat{I})/Z(\widehat{G})]^{\Gamma_{v}})\to\coh^{1}(F_{v},Z(\widehat{G})),

we can identify
\mathfrak{K}(I/\mathbb{Q})
with the set of
x\in(K_{\Gamma,\tors})^{D}
such that for each place
v
the composite map
x_{v}:K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\to K_{\Gamma,\tors}\xrightarrow{x}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
equals the composite map
K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\to\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\xrightarrow{y_{v}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, for some
y_{v}\in(\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v},\tors})^{D}
.
By the exact sequence

(\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v},\tors})^{D}\to(K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors})^{D}\to\bigg(\ker(K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\to\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v},\tors})\bigg)^{D},

the last condition on
x_{v}
is equivalent to requiring that
x
kills the image of

\ker(K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\to\pi_{1}(I)_{\Gamma_{v},\tors})

in
K_{\Gamma,\tors}
. Comparing this description of
\mathfrak{K}(I/\mathbb{Q})
and Proposition
1.7.3
, we see that
\mathfrak{K}(I/F)\cong\mathfrak{E}(I,G;\mathbb A_{F}/F)^{D}
.
∎

1.7.5
.

We now keep the setting of §
1.6.1
.
Let
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},a,[b])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}
, and write
I_{0}
for
I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})=G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
. We now construct an element

\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})\in\mathfrak{E}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q}),

called the
Kottwitz invariant
of
{\mathfrak{c}}
. This generalizes the construction in
[
Kot90
, §2]
.

We write
\beta^{p,\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
for the element
a\in\mathfrak{D}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p})
. As discussed in §
1.1.6
, the abelianization map induces an isomorphism
\mathfrak{D}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\cong\mathfrak{E}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A_{f}^{p})\subset\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p},I_{0})
. By Proposition
1.1.9
(i), we have a canonical isomorphism

\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p},I_{0})\cong\bigoplus_{v\neq p,\infty}\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}.

Hence we also view
\beta^{p,\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
as an element of

\bigoplus_{v\neq p,\infty}\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}.

For each place
v\notin\left\{p,\infty\right\}
, we write
\beta_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})\in\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}
for the component of
\beta^{p,\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
at
v
.
We pick a lift
\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})\in\pi_{1}(I_{0})
of
\beta_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
that maps to zero in
\pi_{1}(G)
. Such a lift exists, since
\beta_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
maps to zero in
\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{v}}
and since the map
\pi_{1}(I_{0})\to\pi_{1}(G)
is surjective.
Since
\beta_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})=0
for almost all
v
, we may and shall assume that
\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})=0
for almost all
v
.

Let
\beta_{p}({\mathfrak{c}}):=\kappa_{I_{0}}([b])\in\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{p}}
. We pick a lift
\widetilde{\beta}_{p}({\mathfrak{c}})\in\pi_{1}({I_{0}})
of
\beta_{p}({\mathfrak{c}})
that maps to
-[\mu]_{X}\in\pi_{1}(G)
. Such a lift exists by condition
KP0
in Definition
1.6.4
and by the surjectivity of the map
\pi_{1}(I_{0})\to\pi_{1}(G)
.

Now we take an elliptic maximal torus
T
in
G_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\gamma_{0}\in T(\mathbb{R})
. Then
T\subset I_{0,\mathbb{R}}
. Since
T
is elliptic, there exists
h\in X
that factors through
T
. Let
\beta_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
be the image of
\mu_{h}\in X_{*}(T)
in
\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
. By
[
Kot90
, Lem. 5.1]
, we know that the image of
\mu_{h}
in
X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
is independent of the choice of
h
. Moreover, since all elliptic maximal tori in
I_{0,\mathbb{R}}
are conjugate under
I_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, the element
\beta_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
is independent of the choice of
(T,h)
as above. (For more details see
[
Kot90
, p. 167]
.) We pick a lift
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})\in\pi_{1}(I_{0})
of
\beta_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
that maps to
[\mu]_{X}\in\pi_{1}(G)
.
Such a lift exists, since the image of
\beta_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
under
\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{\infty}}\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
equals the image of
[\mu]_{X}
, and since the map
\pi_{1}(I_{0})\to\pi_{1}(G)
is surjective.

Write

K
for
\ker(\pi_{1}(I_{0})\to\pi_{1}(G))
. By the above construction, we have an element

\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})\in K
for each place
v\notin\left\{p,\infty\right\}
, as well as an element
\widetilde{\beta}_{p}({\mathfrak{c}})+\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})\in K
.
We define

\widetilde{\beta}({\mathfrak{c}}):=\sum_{v}\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})\in K.

Here the summation is over all places
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
, and only finitely many terms are non-zero.

Note that
K_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
is torsion. Indeed, if we take an
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic maximal torus
T
in
I_{0,\mathbb{R}}
and let
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{S}
be the inverse images of
T
in
G_{\mathrm{sc},\mathbb{R}}
and
I_{0,\mathrm{sc},\mathbb{R}}
respectively, then
K\cong X_{*}(\widetilde{T})/X_{*}(\widetilde{S})
. Since
\widetilde{T}
is anisotropic over
\mathbb{R}
, we know that
X_{*}(\widetilde{T})_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
is torsion. It follows that
K_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
is torsion. In particular,
K_{\Gamma}
is torsion. Hence
\widetilde{\beta}({\mathfrak{c}})
gives rise to an element
\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})\in\mathfrak{E}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q})
, by Proposition
1.7.3
. Note that the ambiguity in
\widetilde{\beta}({\mathfrak{c}})
caused by the choices of
\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
always comes from
\bigoplus_{v}\ker(K\to\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{v}}).
Hence
\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})
is well defined.

1.7.6
.

It is convenient to have the definition of local components of Kottwitz invariants when
\gamma_{0}
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-rational for the stabilization of the trace formula.
Suppose
\gamma_{0}
is a semi-simple element of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
. We have
I_{0}=G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
over
\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}
, and
we define the pointed set
{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
to be the restricted product
\prod^{\prime}_{v}\mathfrak{D}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{v})
with respect to the trivial elements. (The cohomology of
\Gamma
acting on
I_{0}(\bar{\mathbb}{A}_{f}^{p})
no longer makes sense.) If we are given an element
a\in\mathfrak{D}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
, we will also write
\beta^{p,\infty}(\gamma_{0},a)
for
a
, generalizing the notation
\beta^{p,\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
in §
1.7.5
. Similarly, given
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})_{\mathrm{ss}}
and given
[b]\in\B((G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})_{\gamma_{0}}^{0})
satisfying
KP0
in Definition
1.6.4
, we have
\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0},[b])
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{p}(\gamma_{0},[b])
(the latter involving an extra choice), generalizing
\beta_{p}({\mathfrak{c}})
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{p}({\mathfrak{c}})
in §
1.7.5
. Finally, starting from a semi-simple
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic element
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{R})
, we can define
\beta_{\infty}(\gamma_{0})
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0})
(the latter involving an extra choice) in the same way as in §
1.7.5
, generalizing
\beta_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}})
.

1.7.7
.

Let us check that our definition of Kottwitz invariants coincides with Kottwitz’s definition in
[
Kot90
, §2]
, when
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is simply connected. This verification allows us to freely import results from
[
Kot90
]
during the stabilization process.

Under the identification
\pi_{1}(G)\cong X^{*}(Z(\widehat{G}))
, we view
[\mu]_{X}\in\pi_{1}(G)
as a character on
Z(\widehat{G})
. Under the current assumption on
G_{\mathrm{der}}
, recall that Kottwitz attaches an invariant
\alpha({\mathfrak{k}})\in{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{0}/\mathbb{Q})^{D}
to a classical Kottwitz parameter
{\mathfrak{k}}=(\gamma_{0},\gamma,\delta)
of degree
n
. The outline is as follows. Let
I_{0}:=G_{\gamma_{0}}
, which is connected by the assumption on
G_{\mathrm{der}}
. Kottwitz first defines
\alpha_{v}({\mathfrak{k}})\in\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{v}}\cong X^{*}(Z(\widehat{I}_{0})^{\Gamma_{v}})
at every place
v
. Then the character
\alpha_{v}({\mathfrak{k}})
on
Z(\widehat{I_{0}})^{\Gamma_{v}}
can be extended to a character
\beta^{\prime}_{v}({\mathfrak{k}})
on
Z(\widehat{I_{0}})^{\Gamma_{v}}Z(\widehat{G})
, uniquely by the requirement that
\beta^{\prime}_{v}({\mathfrak{k}})
is either trivial or equal to
-[\mu]
or

[\mu]
on
Z(\widehat{G}),
according as
v\notin\left\{p,\infty\right\}
or
v=p
or
v=\infty
, respectively.

9
9

9

We write
\beta^{\prime}
for Kottwitz’s
\beta
to avoid conflict with our own notation.
Thereby one obtains a character
\beta({\mathfrak{k}}):=\prod_{v}\beta^{\prime}_{v}({\mathfrak{k}})
on
\bigcap_{v}Z(\widehat{I_{0}})^{\Gamma_{v}}Z(\widehat{G})
. Since
\beta({\mathfrak{k}})
is trivial on
Z(\widehat{G})
by construction, it gives rise to a character
\alpha({\mathfrak{k}})
on
\left(\bigcap_{v}Z(\widehat{I_{0}})^{\Gamma_{v}}Z(\widehat{G})\right)/Z(\widehat{G})
. The last group is canonically isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{0}/\mathbb{Q})
if
\gamma_{0}
is elliptic in
G(\mathbb{Q})
, which is true since
\gamma_{0}
is
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic. We note that the canonical map from
K=\ker(\pi_{1}(I_{0})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(G))
to the character group of
\cap_{v}Z(\widehat{I_{0}})^{\Gamma_{v}}Z(\widehat{G})
is compatible with the canonical map from
K
to
{\mathfrak{E}}(I_{0},G,\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q})
via the isomorphisms

{\mathfrak{E}}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q})\cong{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{0}/\mathbb{Q})^{D}\cong\left(\frac{\cap_{v}Z(\widehat{I_{0}})^{\Gamma_{v}}Z(\widehat{G})}{Z(\widehat{G})}\right)^{D}

as can be seen from the proof of Corollary
1.7.4
.

When
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is simply connected, we remarked in §
1.6.16
that we have a bijection
{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})/{\cong}\to{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})/{\sim}
. Suppose
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},a,[b])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})
corresponds to
{\mathfrak{k}}=(\gamma_{0},\gamma,\delta)\in{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})
. We defined
\beta_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
in §
1.7.5
, for each place
v
. For
v\in\{p,\infty\}
, note that
\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
is a character on
Z(\widehat{I}_{0})
, extending the character
\beta_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
on
Z(\widehat{I}_{0})^{\Gamma_{v}}
. Inspecting the definition we see that

\beta_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})=\alpha_{v}({\mathfrak{k}}),\quad\forall v,

and

\left.\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})\right|_{Z(\widehat{I_{0}})^{\Gamma_{v}}Z(\widehat{G})}=\beta^{\prime}_{v}({\mathfrak{k}}),\quad\forall v.

Therefore the product
\prod_{v}\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
gives the element
\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})\in{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{0}/\mathbb{Q})^{D}
. Comparing with the definition of
\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})
in §
1.7.5
, we conclude that

\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})=\alpha({\mathfrak{k}}).

From now on we return to the general setting, i.e., we do not assume that
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is simply connected.

Proposition 1.7.8
.

Let
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},a,[b])
,
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}=(\gamma_{0},a^{\prime},[b])\in{\mathfrak{KP}}
. Write
I_{0}
for the group
I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}})=I_{0}({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})
. The difference

\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})-\alpha({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{E}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q})
is equal to the image of

a-a^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})

under the composite map

(1.7.8.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})\cong\mathfrak{E}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{E}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A)\to\mathfrak{E}(I_{0},G;\mathbb A/\mathbb{Q}).

Proof.

Write
K
for
\ker(\pi_{1}(I_{0})\to\pi_{1}(G))
. By Proposition
1.1.9
(ii), the diagram

\textstyle{\coh^{0}_{\ab}(\mathbb A,I_{0}\to G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A},I_{0})}
\textstyle{\coh^{0}_{\ab}(\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{Q},I_{0}\to G)}

is canonically isomorphic to the diagram

\textstyle{\tatecoh^{-1}(\Gamma_{\infty},K)\oplus\bigoplus_{v\neq\infty}K_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tatecoh^{-1}(\Gamma_{\infty},\pi_{1}(I_{0}))\oplus\bigoplus_{v\neq\infty}\pi_{1}(I_{0})_{\Gamma_{v},\tors}}
\textstyle{K_{\Gamma,\tors}}

For each place
v\notin\left\{p,\infty\right\}
, we choose
\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}})
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{v}({\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime})
in
K
as in §
