) is induced by the canonical isomorphism between any étale sheaf and its pull-back under
F
.

We now apply the Grothendieck–Lefschetz–Verdier trace formula together with Deligne’s conjecture to compute (
3.7.3.2
). The latter has been proved by Fujiwara
[
Fuj97
]
and Varshavsky
[
Var05
]
(cf. also
[
Pin92b
]
), and states that the local terms in the trace formula can be replaced by the naive local terms, under the assumption that
m
is sufficiently large (while fixing
K^{p}
and
g
).

Let
\mathcal{FIX}
be the set of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-valued fixed points of the correspondence (
3.7
). Using the bijection (
3.7.1.1
), we obtain a description of
\mathcal{FIX}
as follows. For each admissible morphism
\phi
, by Lemma
3.7.2
we know that the data

(3.7.3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y=X_{p}(\phi)\times X^{p}(\phi)^{\prime}/K^{p}_{g},\\
X=X_{p}(\phi)\times X^{p}(\phi)^{\prime}/K^{p},\\
I=I_{\phi}(\mathbb{Q}),\\
C=Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K},\\
a:Y\to X,~(y_{p},y^{p})\mapsto(y_{p},y^{p}g\mod K^{p}),\\
b:Y\to X,~(y_{p},y^{p})\mapsto(\Phi^{m}y_{p},y^{p}\mod K^{p}).\end{cases}

satisfy the hypotheses of
[
Mil92
, Lem. 5.3]
. By
loc. cit.
, (
3.7.1.1
) induces a bijection

(3.7.3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{FIX}\cong\coprod_{\phi}\coprod_{\epsilon}\mathscr{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g),

where
\phi
and
\epsilon
run through the same ranges as in (
3.7.3.1
).

It remains to calculate the naive local term at each point in
\mathcal{FIX}
. We need to show that if
x\in\mathcal{FIX}
corresponds to a point in
\mathscr{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)
under (
3.7.3.9
), then the naive local term at
x
is equal to
{\rm tr}\,\xi(\epsilon)
. Note that
x
only determines the conjugacy class of
\epsilon
in
I_{\phi}(\mathbb{Q})/Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
. By our assumption that
\xi
factors through
G^{c}
and by Lemma
1.5.7
, we know that
{\rm tr}\,\xi
is invariant under
Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
since
K
is neat. Hence
{\rm tr}\,\xi
defines a class function on
G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
that is translation-invariant under
Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
, and our desideratum makes sense.

Now let
\widetilde{x}
be a lift of
x
in
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. Since
x\in\mathcal{FIX}
, there exists
k_{\widetilde{x}}\in K^{p}
such that

\Phi^{m}\widetilde{x}=\widetilde{x}gk_{\widetilde{x}}.

Let
k_{\ell}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
be the component of
k_{\widetilde{x}}
at
\ell
, and let
g_{\ell}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
be the component of
g\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
at
\ell
. By the same argument as
[
Kot92b
, p. 433]
, the naive local term at
x
is given by

{\rm tr}\,\xi(k_{\ell}^{-1}g_{\ell}^{-1}).

If
x
corresponds to an element of
\mathscr{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)
under (
3.7.3.9
), then
k_{\ell}^{-1}g_{\ell}^{-1}
is conjugate to
\epsilon
in
G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
. Thus the naive local term at
x
is
{\rm tr}\,\xi(\epsilon)
, as desired.∎

Lemma 3.7.4
.

Let
(\phi,\epsilon)\in\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\sa}
, and let
{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon)
. The set
\mathcal{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)
defined in Lemma
3.7.3
is empty unless
(\phi,\epsilon)\in\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})
. In the latter case, we have
{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(p^{n})
by Proposition
3.5.8
, and we define
c_{1}({\mathfrak{c}},K^{p})O({\mathfrak{c}},m,f^{p}dg^{p})
as in §
1.8.2
and §
1.8.6
. We have

\#\mathcal{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)=\iota_{I_{\phi}}(\epsilon)^{-1}c_{1}({\mathfrak{c}},K^{p})O({\mathfrak{c}},m,f^{p}dg^{p}).

Proof.

We write
{\mathfrak{c}}=(\gamma_{0},\gamma,[b])
, where
\gamma_{0}=\epsilon
, and let
(\gamma_{0},\gamma,\delta)\in{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\clsc}(p^{n})
be the classical Kottwitz parameter associated with
{\mathfrak{c}}
(which is well defined up to equivalence). By construction,
(b,\epsilon)\in\cls_{p}(\phi,\epsilon)
, so
X_{p}(\phi,\epsilon,q^{m})
is identified with
X_{-\mu_{X}}(b,\epsilon,q^{m})
; see §
3.1.5
.
If
\mathcal{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)\neq\emptyset
, then
X_{p}(\phi,\epsilon,q^{m})\neq\emptyset
, and it follows that
(\phi,\epsilon)
is
q^{m}
-admissible.

Now assume that
(\phi,\epsilon)
is
q^{m}
-admissible. The computation of
\#\mathcal{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)
is essentially the same as the computation by Kottwitz in
[
Kot84a
, §1.4, §1.5]
. We explain how to make the transition from our setting to the setting of
loc. cit.

We write
Y^{p}
for the set
\{y^{p}\in X^{p}(\phi)^{\prime}/K^{p}_{g}\mid y^{p}\equiv\epsilon y^{p}g\mod K^{p}\}
, and write
Y_{p}
for the set
X_{p}(\phi,\epsilon,q^{m})
. Thus
\mathcal{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)=I_{\phi,\epsilon}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(Y_{p}\times Y^{p})
. Note that
\#\mathcal{O}(\phi,\epsilon,m,g)
is equal to the cardinality of
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(Y_{p}\times Y^{p})
multiplied by
\iota_{I_{\phi}}(\epsilon)^{-1}
. This is because, if an element
u\in I_{\phi,\epsilon}(\mathbb{Q})
has a fixed point in
Y_{p}\times Y^{p}
, then by Lemma
3.7.2
(applied to
g=1
), we must have
u\in Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}\subset I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
. Thus it remains to show that

(3.7.4.1)

\displaystyle\#\bigg(I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(Y_{p}\times Y^{p})\bigg)=c_{1}({\mathfrak{c}})O({\mathfrak{c}},m,f^{p}dg^{p}).

As explained in §
2.4.7
, we identify
X^{p}(\phi)^{\prime}=X^{p}(\phi)
with a right
G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
-coset inside
G(\bar{\mathbb}{A}_{f}^{p})
. Fix
y^{p}_{0}\in X^{p}(\phi)^{\prime}\subset G(\bar{\mathbb}{A}_{f}^{p})
. Inspecting definitions, we see that
\gamma\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
is conjugate to
(\Int(y^{p}_{0})^{-1}\circ\Int(\tau_{\phi}))(\epsilon)\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
inside
G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
. We may assume that they are equal. Now if we use the “base point”
y^{p}_{0}
to identify
X^{p}(\phi)^{\prime}
with
G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
, then we have a bijection

\displaystyle Y^{p}W^{p}:=\left\{y^{p}\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})/K^{p}_{g}\mid y^{p}\equiv\gamma y^{p}g\mod K^{p}\right\}.

Under this bijection, the action of
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
on
Y^{p}
corresponds to the action of
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
on
W^{p}
given by the composition of the
\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}
-isomorphism
\Int(y^{p}_{0})^{-1}\circ\Int(\tau_{\phi}):(I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0})_{\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}}\hookrightarrow(G_{\gamma}^{0})_{\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}}
(see §
3.2.14
) followed by left multiplication of
G_{\gamma}^{0}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
on
W^{p}
.

Let
\mu\in\bbmu_{X}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
. We have already seen that
Y_{p}
can be identified with
X_{-\mu}(b,\epsilon,q^{m})
as in §
3.1.5
. More precisely, write
I_{0}
for
G_{\epsilon}^{0}
, and suppose that
b=b_{\Int(g^{-1})\circ\phi_{I_{0}}(p)\circ\zeta_{p}}
for
g\in\mathcal{UR}(\phi(p)\circ\zeta_{p})\cap I_{0}(\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p})
. (Here we follow the notation in §
3.5.1
.)
Then
y_{p}\mapsto g^{-1}y_{p}
induces a bijection

Y_{p}X_{-\mu}(b,\epsilon,q^{m}),

see §
2.2.7
and §
3.1.5
. Moreover, under this bijection the action of
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
on
Y_{p}
corresponds to the action of
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
on
X_{-\mu}(b,\epsilon,q^{m})
given by the composition of the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-isomorphism
\Int(g^{-1}):(I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}J_{b}^{I_{0}}
followed by left multiplication of
J_{b}^{I_{0}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
on
X_{-\mu}(b,\epsilon,q^{m})
. Here, to see that
\Int(g^{-1}):(I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}J_{b}^{I_{0}}
is an isomorphism, use the identification
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}\cong I_{\phi_{I_{0}}}
as in §
3.2.3
and §
3.2.14
, and use the fact that
b
is basic in
I_{0}
by Corollary
1.6.12
.

If we fix
c\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that
\delta=c^{-1}b\sigma(c)
and such that (
1.6.5.1
) holds, then the map
x\mapsto c^{-1}x
induces a bijection

\displaystyle X_{-\mu}(b,\epsilon,q^{m})W_{p}:=\left\{y_{p}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})/{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p^{n}})\mid y_{p}^{-1}\delta\sigma(y_{p})\in{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p^{n}})p^{-\mu}{\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p^{n}})\right\}.

Under this bijection, the action of
J_{b}^{I_{0}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
on the left corresponds to the following action on
W_{p}
: We have an injective
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-homomorphism
J_{b}^{I_{0}}\to R_{\delta\rtimes\sigma}^{0}
induced by
\Int(c^{-1})
. (See §
1.8.2
for
R_{\delta\rtimes\theta}^{0}
.) This homomorphism is in fact an isomorphism, because both groups are connected, and their dimensions are equal to that of
I_{0}
. We thus identify
J_{b}^{I_{0}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
with
R^{0}_{\delta\rtimes\theta}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and let the latter group act on
W_{p}
by left multiplication.

In conclusion, we have bijections

(3.7.4.2)

\displaystyle I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(Y_{p}\times Y^{p})I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(X_{-\mu}(b,\epsilon,q^{m})\times W^{p})I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(W_{p}\times W^{p}),

where
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
acts on
X_{-\mu}(b,\epsilon,q^{m})
,
W^{p}
, and
W_{p}
in the way described above. By abuse of notation, we still write
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
for the image of
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
inside
J_{b}^{I_{0}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times G_{\gamma}^{0}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})\cong R_{\delta\rtimes\theta}^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times G_{\gamma}^{0}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
, under the embeddings described above. We also identify the last two product groups with
I({\mathfrak{c}})(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, canonically up to conjugation by
I({\mathfrak{c}})^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. We assume for a moment that
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})Z_{K}
is closed and has finite co-volume inside
I({\mathfrak{c}})(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Then the computation in
[
Kot84a
, §1.5]
21
21

21

In
loc. cit.
, it is assumed that
G_{\mathrm{der}}
is simply connected, so that
G_{\gamma}
and
R_{\delta\rtimes\sigma}
(which is denoted by
G_{\delta}^{\sigma}
) are connected. To transport the computation to the current situation, one simply replaces all appearances of
G_{\gamma}
and
R_{\delta\rtimes\sigma}
by their identity components.
shows that the cardinality of the third set in (
3.7.4.2
) is given by

\mathrm{vol}(I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})Z_{K}\backslash I({\mathfrak{c}})(\mathbb{A}_{f}))\cdot O({\mathfrak{c}},m,f^{p}dg^{p}).

Here,
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})Z_{K}
is equipped with the Haar measure giving volume
1
to its open subgroup
Z_{K}=Z_{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\cap K
.

To complete the proof, we need to verify our assumption on
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})Z_{K}
, and we need to identify
\mathrm{vol}(I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})Z_{K}\backslash I_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}))
with
c_{1}({\mathfrak{c}})
. For both purposes, it suffices to prove the following claim: The image of
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}(\mathbb{Q})
inside
I({\mathfrak{c}})(\mathbb{A}_{f})
is
I({\mathfrak{c}})^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-conjugate to
I({\mathfrak{c}})(\mathbb{Q})
. (Note that the Haar measure on
I({\mathfrak{c}})(\mathbb{A}_{f})
is invariant under conjugation by
I({\mathfrak{c}})^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.)

To prove the claim, note that we have a canonical inner twisting between the
\mathbb{Q}
-groups
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}
and
I_{0}
, as in §
3.2.14
. We have described an
\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}
-isomorphism
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}G_{\gamma}^{0}
, and a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-isomorphism
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}J_{b}^{I_{0}}
. One checks that these isomorphisms are isomorphisms between inner forms of
I_{0}
(in the sense of Definition
1.2.2
). Also,
I_{\phi,\epsilon}^{0}/Z_{G}
is anisotropic over
\mathbb{R}
, and up to isomorphism there is at most one inner form of
I_{0}
over
\mathbb{R}
which is anisotropic modulo
Z_{G}
. The claim then follows from the unique characterization of
I({\mathfrak{c}})
as an inner form of
I_{0}
over
\mathbb{Q}
(up to isomorphism), in Lemma
1.7.12
. ∎

3.7.5
.

We have a natural action

\displaystyle Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}\times[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]

\displaystyle(z,[\phi,\epsilon])

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\phi,z\epsilon].

We fix a set of representatives for the
Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}
-orbits in
[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]
and by abuse of notation denote this subset of
[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]
by

[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]/{Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}}.

By the first statement in Lemma
3.7.4
and by Corollary
3.2.17
, we may replace the two summations in (
3.7.3.1
) by the summation over
[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]/{Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}}
. Applying Lemma
3.7.4
to the summands, we obtain

(3.7.5.1)

T(\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{m},f^{p}dg^{p})\\
=\sum_{[\phi,\epsilon]\in[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]/Z(\mathbb{Q})_{K}}\iota_{I_{\phi}}(\epsilon)^{-1}c_{1}(\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon))~O\big(\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon),m,f^{p}dg^{p}\big){\rm tr}\,{\xi}(\epsilon),

for all sufficiently large
m
. Here, for
(\phi,\epsilon)\in\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})
, we know that the isomorphism class of
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon)
depends only on
[\phi,\epsilon]
, by Proposition
3.6.1
. Moreover, the value of

c_{1}(\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon))\cdot O\big(\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon),m,f^{p}dg^{p}\big)

depends on
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon)
only via its isomorphism class, which can be checked using the definitions.

Let
\Sigma_{K^{p}}
be as in §
1.8.7
, and for each
\gamma_{0}\in\Sigma_{K^{p}}
we write
{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0},q^{m})_{\alpha}
for the set of
{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0})\cap{\mathfrak{KP}}_{\adm}(q^{m})
satisfying
\alpha({\mathfrak{c}})=0
. (See §
1.8.7
for the notation
{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0})
.) By Proposition
1.7.10
,
{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0},q^{m})_{\alpha}
is stable under isomorphisms between Kottwitz parameters. Using Proposition
3.6.3
, we rewrite (
3.7.5.1
) as

(3.7.5.2)

\displaystyle T(\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{m},f^{p}dg^{p})=\sum_{\gamma_{0}\in\Sigma_{K^{p}}}{\rm tr}\,{\xi}(\gamma_{0})\sum_{{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0},q^{m})_{\alpha}/{\cong}}c_{1}({\mathfrak{c}})O({\mathfrak{c}},m,f^{p}dg^{p})\mathscr{A}({\mathfrak{c}}),

where

(3.7.5.3)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}({\mathfrak{c}}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\phi,\epsilon]\in[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})]\\
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon)\cong{\mathfrak{c}}\end{subarray}}\iota_{I_{\phi}}(\epsilon)^{-1}.

Lemma 3.7.6
.

Let
\gamma_{0}\in\Sigma_{K^{p}}
and let
{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0},q^{m})_{\alpha}
. Assume that
O({\mathfrak{c}},m,f^{p}dg^{p})
is non-zero. Let
k
be the number of elements of
{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0},q^{m})_{\alpha}
that are isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{c}}
. Then we have

\mathscr{A}({\mathfrak{c}})=k\cdot\bar{\iota}_{G}(\gamma_{0})^{-1}\cdot c_{2}(\gamma_{0}).

Here the notation is as in §
1.8.6
and §
1.8.7
.

Proof.

First note that
k
is finite, since it is at most
\left|G_{\gamma_{0}}/G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}\right|
. From the non-vanishing
O({\mathfrak{c}},m,f^{p}dg^{p})
(or rather just the non-vanishing of the twisted orbital integral at
p
), it follows that
{\mathfrak{c}}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
3.6.5
(for
n=mr
). By that proposition, there exists an element of
\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})
of the form
(\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})
such that
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})={\mathfrak{c}}
. By Proposition
3.2.20
, all
[\phi,\epsilon]
appearing in the summation in (
3.7.5.3
) necessarily lie inside
\mathcal{C}_{[\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}]}
. On the other hand, for each
[\phi,\epsilon]\in\mathcal{C}_{[\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}]}\cap[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\sa}]
, if
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon)\cong{\mathfrak{c}}
, then
[\phi,\epsilon]
is automatically
q^{m}
-admissible, by Proposition
3.5.8
. Hence we can rewrite (
3.7.5.3
) as

(3.7.6.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}({\mathfrak{c}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\phi,\epsilon]\in\mathcal{C}_{[\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}]}\cap[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\sa}]\\
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\phi,\epsilon)\cong{\mathfrak{c}}\end{subarray}}\iota_{I_{\phi}}(\epsilon)^{-1}.

Let
\widetilde{\Sha}_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
denote the inverse image of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
in
Z^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
.
By Definition
3.2.9
and Proposition
3.2.19
, we have a surjection

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sha}_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{[\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}]}\cap[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\sa}]

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\longmapsto[e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}].

which factors through a surjection

\displaystyle\eta_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}:\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{[\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}]}\cap[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\sa}].

Define
\widetilde{D}
to be the subset of
\widetilde{\Sha}_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
consisting of elements
e
such that

\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})\cong{\mathfrak{c}}.

Now let
{\mathfrak{c}}_{1},{\mathfrak{c}}_{2},\cdots,{\mathfrak{c}}_{k}
be all the distinct elements of
{\mathfrak{KP}}(\gamma_{0},q^{m})_{\alpha}
that are isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{c}}
. For each
e\in\widetilde{D}
, we have
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})={\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
for a unique
i\in\left\{1,\cdots,k\right\}
. We thus obtain a partition

\widetilde{D}=\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\widetilde{D}_{i}.

By Proposition
3.6.2
(iii), for each
1\leq i\leq k
, the set
\widetilde{D}_{i}
is the inverse image of a subset
D_{i}
of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
. We can thus rewrite (
3.7.6.1
) as

(3.7.6.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}({\mathfrak{c}})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\beta\in D_{i}}\frac{1}{\iota_{I_{e\phi_{0}}}(\gamma_{0})\cdot\#\eta_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{-1}(\eta_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}(\beta))}

where
e
is a cocycle representing
\beta
. (We will soon see that the fibers of
\eta_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}
are indeed finite.)

By §
3.2.14
, the
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-embedding
(I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\to(G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is an isomorphism and is an inner twisting between the
\mathbb{Q}
-groups
I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0}
and
G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
. Using this observation and Proposition
3.6.2
, it is easy to see that for each
1\leq i\leq k
, the set
D_{i}
is either empty, or a coset of
\Sha(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
inside
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
. We claim that it is never empty. Let
u_{i}\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
be an isomorphism
{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
. Then
u_{i}\in G_{\gamma_{0}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
, and
u_{i}{\vphantom{\tau}}^{\tau}u_{i}^{-1}\in G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}),\forall\tau\in\Gamma
. By Lemma
3.6.4
, we have
(\Int(u_{i})\circ\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})
. By Proposition
3.6.1
, we have
\mathbf{t}_{\underline{\sigma}}(\Int(u_{i})\circ\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})={\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
. It remains to check that
\Int(u_{i})\circ\phi_{0}
is of the form
e\phi_{0}
for some
e\in Z^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
. For this, we fix a lift
q_{\tau}\in\mathfrak{Q}
of each
\tau\in\Gamma
, and write
\phi_{0}(q_{\tau})=g_{\tau}\rtimes\tau
. Define
e_{\tau}:=u_{i}g_{\tau}{\vphantom{\tau}}^{\tau}u_{i}^{-1}g_{\tau}^{-1}
. Then
e=(e_{\tau})_{\tau}
is a cocycle in
Z^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0}})
, and
\Int(u_{i})\circ\phi_{0}=e\phi_{0}
. It remains to check that
e_{\tau}\in I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
for each
\tau
. Let
\pi_{0}:=(G_{\gamma_{0}}/G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0})(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
, which is an abelian group as explained in §
1.8.7
. We have seen that
u_{i}
and
{\vphantom{\tau}}{}^{\tau}u_{i}
map to the same element of
\pi_{0}
. Since
(\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})
is gg,
g_{\tau}
maps to the identity in
\pi_{0}
. Hence
e_{\tau}
maps to the identity in
\pi_{0}
, i.e.,
e_{\tau}\in G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})=I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
, as desired.

We have proved the claim. Hence for each
1\leq i\leq k
, we have

\left|D_{i}\right|=\left|\Sha(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})\right|.

Since
I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0}
is an inner form of
G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
over
\mathbb{Q}
, this number is equal to
c_{2}(\gamma_{0})=\left|\Sha(\mathbb{Q},G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0})\right|
.

To complete the proof of the lemma, it suffices to check that each summand in (
3.7.6.2
) is equal to
\bar{\iota}_{G}(\gamma_{0})^{-1}
.
Recall from §
3.2.7
that the composition of
\eta_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}
with the natural injection
\mathfrak{v}:[\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}]\to\langle\mathcal{LRP}\rangle
factors as

\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}})\xrightarrow{\iota_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}}\langle\mathcal{LRP}\rangle.

The map
\iota_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}
is injective. Hence for each
\beta\in\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})
, the set

\eta_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{-1}(\eta_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}(\beta))

is equal to the fiber of the map
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}})
containing
\beta
. This fiber can be identified with the kernel of
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}})
, where
e
is a cocycle representing
\beta
. By
[
Lab04
, Cor. III.1.3]
, the cardinality of this kernel is

\bar{\iota}_{I_{e\phi_{0}}}(\gamma_{0})\cdot(\iota_{I_{e\phi_{0}}}(\gamma_{0}))^{-1}.

From this, we see that if a summand in (
3.7.6.2
) is indexed by
\beta
, then this summand is equal to
\bar{\iota}_{I_{e\phi_{0}}}(\gamma_{0})^{-1}
for any cocycle
e
representing
\beta
. Since
\beta\in D_{i}
for some
i
and since
{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}
is
q^{m}
-admissible (as
{\mathfrak{c}}
is), we have
(e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{LRP}^{\mathrm{gg}}_{\adm}(q^{m})
by Proposition
3.5.8
. Thus by §
3.2.14
, we have canonical inner twistings
(I_{e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}(G_{\gamma_{0}})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
and
(I_{e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}(G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. In particular, we have an inner twisting between the commutative groups
I_{e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}/I_{e\phi_{0},\gamma_{0}}^{0}
and
G_{\gamma_{0}}/G_{\gamma_{0}}^{0}
(see §
1.8.7
), which must be an isomorphism over
\mathbb{Q}
. It follows that
\bar{\iota}_{I_{e\phi_{0}}}(\gamma_{0})=\bar{\iota}_{G}(\gamma_{0})
. The proof is complete.
∎

The proof of (
1.8.8.1
) is completed by combining (
3.7.5.2
) and Lemma
3.7.6
.

Part II
Shimura varieties of abelian type

4.
Results on crystalline representations

Throughout this section we fix a prime number
p
.

4.1.
Generalities on fiber functors

4.1.1
.

Let
G
be a flat, finite-type, affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p},
and

{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G
the category of representations of
G
on finite free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules. For any commutative ring
S
, we write
{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{S}
for the category of finite projective
S
-modules. Now let
R
be a faithfully flat
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra. By a
fiber functor over
R
, we mean a faithful exact
\otimes
-functor

\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\rightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
.
We denote by
\idfunc_{R}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\rightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
the functor which takes
L
to
L\otimes R.
Then
\idfunc_{R}
is a fiber functor, called
the standard fiber functor
.

If
\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
is a
\otimes
-functor and
S
is an
R
-algebra, we write
\omega_{S}
for the composition of
\otimes
-functors

{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\xrightarrow{\omega}{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}\xrightarrow{\cdot\otimes_{R}S}{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{S},

called the
base change
or
pull-back
of
\omega
over
S
.

For two
\otimes
-functors
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
, we write
\SIsom^{\otimes}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
for the
R
-scheme of
\otimes
-isomorphisms from
\omega_{1}
to
\omega_{2}
; see
[
Del90
, §1.11]
. Thus for any
R
-algebra
S
,
\SIsom^{\otimes}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})(S)
is the set of
\otimes
-isomorphisms
\omega_{1,S}\omega_{2,S}
. If
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}
, we write
\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}(\omega_{1})
for
\SIsom^{\otimes}(\omega_{1},\omega_{1})
.

The reconstruction theorem, which is well known over a field, is valid in our current setting, since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a Dedekind domain and
G
is affine flat. This theorem says that the natural morphism
G\to\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}(\idfunc_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-group schemes; see for instance
[
Mil12
, X, Thm. 1.2, Rmk. 1.6]
, or
[
Wed04
, Thm. 5.17]
.

Let
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G
be the category of
G
-representations on finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector spaces. By
[
Ser68
, §1.5]
, every representation in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G
contains a
G
-stable
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattice (since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is noetherian and
G
is affine flat). Using this fact, for each fiber functor
\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\rightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
we can define a
\otimes
-functor

\omega[1/p]:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\rightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R[1/p]}

as follows: If
V
is in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G,
then we write
V=\varinjlim_{i}V_{i}
as a direct limit of
G
-stable
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattices. We set
\omega[1/p](V)=\varinjlim_{i}\omega(V_{i}),
which is naturally isomorphic to
\omega(V_{i})\otimes_{R}R[1/p]
for any
i.
Note that
\omega[1/p]
is again a fiber functor, i.e., a faithful exact
\otimes
-functor.

Given the above construction, it is easy to see that the category of fiber functors
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
is equivalent to the category of fiber-wise faithful exact functors between fibered categories
\mathbf{Rep}~G\to\mathbf{Bun}_{\Spec R}
(fibered over the small Zariski site of
\Spec\mathbb{Z}_{p}
), as considered in
[
Bro13
]
.
Thus we shall freely import results from
loc. cit.
for the fiber functors in our sense. Also cf. the last remark in the introduction of
loc. cit
.

Let

\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
be a fiber functor. Then
\SIsom^{\otimes}(\idfunc_{R},\omega)
has a right
G_{R}
-action via the natural homomorphism
G_{R}\to\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}(\idfunc_{R})
(which we have seen is an isomorphism). By
[
Bro13
, Thm. 4.8]
,
\SIsom^{\otimes}(\idfunc_{R},\omega)
is in fact a right
G_{R}
-torsor over
R
(for the fppf topology). This result could be viewed as a generalization of the reconstruction theorem
recalled above. In the sequel, we denote the
G_{R}
-torsor
\SIsom^{\otimes}(\idfunc_{R},\omega)
by
P_{\omega}
.

4.1.2
.

Let
G
and
R
be as in §
4.1.1
. We introduce a short-hand notation. Let
\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
be a fiber functor, and let
S
be a
R[1/p]
-algebra. Then the base change
\omega_{S}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{S}
of
\omega
over
S
factors as

{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\xrightarrow{\cdot\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\xrightarrow{\omega[1/p]}{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R[1/p]}\xrightarrow{\cdot\otimes_{R[1/p]}S}{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{S}.

We denote the composite functor

(\cdot\otimes_{R[1/p]}S)\circ\omega[1/p]:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{S}

again by
\omega_{S}
.

Definition 4.1.3
.

For any finite free module
M
over any commutative ring
S
, we denote by
M^{\otimes}
the direct sum of all the
S
-modules
which can be formed from
M
using the operations of taking duals, tensor products,
symmetric powers, and exterior powers. Elements of
M^{\otimes}
are called
tensors over
M
.

4.1.4
.

Let
G
and
R
be as in §
4.1.1
. Let
\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
be a fiber functor. Then
\omega
is compatible with the operations considered in Definition
4.1.3
, by
[
Bro13
, Thm. 4.8, Rmk. 4.2]
. (The compatibility with taking duals follows from rigidity; see
[
DM82
, Prop. 1.9]
.) In this case, for each
L
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G
and each
G
-invariant element
s\in L^{\otimes}
, we may think of
s
as a morphism
\mathbf{1}\to L^{\otimes}
(where
\mathbf{1}
is the unit object) and apply
\omega
to it. We then get a morphism
\omega(s):\mathbf{1}\to\omega(L^{\otimes})\cong\omega(L)^{\otimes}
, or equivalently an element
\omega(s)\in\omega(L)^{\otimes}
. Here, it is understood that
\omega
has been extended to infinite direct sums of objects, when we apply it to
L^{\otimes}
.

Definition 4.1.5
.

Let
G
be a flat, finite-type, affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}.
We say that
G
is
definable by tensors
, if there exists
L
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G
, and a subset
(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}\subset L^{\otimes}
, such that the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-homomorphism
G\to{\rm GL}(L)
is a closed embedding whose image is the point-wise stabilizer of
(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}
. When this is the case we call
(L,(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}})
a
defining datum for
G
.

Remark 4.1.6
.

If
G
is a flat, finite-type, affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
such that
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is reductive, then
G
is definable by tensors, by combining
[
Bro13
, Lem. 3.2]
and
[
Kis10
, Prop. 1.3.2]
.

4.1.7
.

Let
G
be a flat, finite-type, affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
that is definable by tensors. Fix a defining datum
(L,(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}})
for
G
. Let
R
be a faithfully flat
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra. It will be useful to give a description of more explicit data giving rise to a fiber functor over
R
.

Let
D
be a finite free
R
-module equipped with a collection of tensors

(s_{\alpha,0})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}\subset D^{\otimes}
indexed by the set
\bm{\alpha}
, and suppose that there exists an
R
-module isomorphism

L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}RD
taking each
s_{\alpha}
to
s_{\alpha,0}.
(Obviously such
D
and
(s_{\alpha,0})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}
always exist.)

Lemma 4.1.8
.

Keep the setting of §
4.1.7
. There exists a fiber functor

\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}

equipped with an isomorphism
\iota:\omega(L)D
such that
\iota
maps
\omega(s_{\alpha})\in\omega(L)^{\otimes}
to
s_{\alpha,0}
for each
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
. The pair
(\omega,\iota)
is unique up to unique isomorphism in the following sense. Given two such pairs
(\omega,\iota),

(\omega^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}),
there is a unique
\otimes
-isomorphism
\omega\omega^{\prime}

which takes
\iota
to
\iota^{\prime}.

Proof.

To show the existence of
\omega,
we set
\omega(Q)=Q\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}R
for
Q
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G,

and we take
\iota
to be any isomorphism
L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}RD
taking
s_{\alpha}
to
s_{\alpha,0}.
(Such an isomorphism is assumed to exist in §
4.1.7
.)

For the uniqueness, consider two such pairs
(\omega,\iota),

(\omega^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}).
We may assume that
(\omega,\iota)
is as constructed above, so in particular
\omega=\idfunc_{R}
. Consider the
R
-scheme

\SIsom_{(s_{\alpha})}(\omega(L),\omega(L^{\prime}))
whose points valued in any
R
-algebra
S
classify
S
-linear isomorphisms
\omega(L)\otimes_{R}S\omega^{\prime}(L)\otimes_{R}S
taking

\omega(s_{\alpha})
to
\omega^{\prime}(s_{\alpha})
for each
\alpha
. Using the existence of
\iota
and
\iota^{\prime}
, one sees that
\SIsom_{(s_{\alpha})}(\omega(L),\omega(L^{\prime}))
is a trivial
G_{R}
-torsor. (Here
G_{R}
acts on
\omega(L)=L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}R
.)
There is a natural
G_{R}
-equivariant map

P_{\omega^{\prime}}=\SIsom^{\otimes}(\idfunc_{R},\omega^{\prime})\longrightarrow\SIsom_{(s_{\alpha})}(\omega(L),\omega(L^{\prime})),

which is necessarily an isomorphism since
P_{\omega^{\prime}}
is a
G_{R}
-torsor. It follows that
P_{\omega^{\prime}}
is a trivial
G_{R}
-torsor, and there exists an isomorphism of
\otimes
-functors
\omega\omega^{\prime}
which is unique up to multiplication by elements of
G(R).

In particular, there is a unique choice of such an isomorphism which takes
\iota
to
\iota^{\prime}.

∎

Remark 4.1.9
.

The proof of Lemma
4.1.8
also shows that if
(\omega,\iota)
is a pair as in that lemma, then
\omega
is
\otimes
-isomorphic to
\idfunc_{R}
.

Lemma 4.1.10
.

Keep the setting of §
4.1.7
, and let
(\omega,\iota)
be a pair as in Lemma
4.1.8
. Let
\omega^{\prime}
be a fiber functor
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R[1/p]}
, and let
\iota^{\prime}
be an isomorphism
\omega^{\prime}(L[1/p])D[1/p]
taking
\omega^{\prime}(s_{\alpha})\in\omega^{\prime}(L[1/p])^{\otimes}
to
s_{\alpha,0}\in D[1/p]^{\otimes}
for each
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
. Then there is a unique
\otimes
-isomorphism
\omega[1/p]\omega^{\prime}
which takes
\iota
to
\iota^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Remark
4.1.9
, we may assume that
\omega=\idfunc_{R}
without loss of generality. The proof of the lemma is then completely analogous to the proof of the uniqueness in Lemma
4.1.8
.
∎

Lemma 4.1.11
.

Let
G
be a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with connected fibers. (We do not need to assume that
G
is definable by tensors.) Let
R
be the ring of integers in either a finite unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
or
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}.
Let
\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
be a fiber functor. Then
P_{\omega}
is a trivial
G_{R}
-torsor. In particular, the set
P_{\omega}(R)
is a
G(R)
-torsor.

Proof.

Our assumptions, together with Lang’s theorem, imply that any
G_{R}
-torsor over
R
, such as
P_{\omega}
, is necessarily trivial.
∎

4.1.12
.

We continue to consider
G
and
R
as in §
4.1.1
. For each fiber functor
\omega:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\rightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{R}
we set

Y(\omega):=P_{\omega}(R[1/p])=\{\text{$\otimes$-isomorphisms }\idfunc_{R[1/p]}\omega_{R[1/p]}\}.

This is either empty or a right
G(R[1/p])
-torsor (i.e., for
\eta\in Y(\omega)
and
g\in G(R[1/p])
, we define
\eta\cdot g:=\eta\circ g
). When
P_{\omega}(R)
is non-empty and thus a (right)
G(R)
-torsor, there is a canonical isomorphism

Y(\omega)\cong P_{\omega}(R)\times^{G(R)}G(R[1/p]).

In this case we write
Y(\omega)^{\circ}
for
P_{\omega}(R)
when we view it as a subset of
Y(\omega)
. Its elements are called
integral points
of
Y(\omega)
.

4.2.
Integral
F
-isocrystals with
G
-structure

4.2.1
.

Let
K_{0}
be either a finite unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p},
or
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}
. Let
\sigma\in\Aut(K_{0})
be the arithmetic
p
-Frobenius. We will apply the considerations in §
4.1
to
F
-isocrystals over
K_{0}
with additional structures.

Recall that an
F
-isocrystal over
K_{0}
is a
finite-dimensional
K_{0}
-vector space
V,
equipped with an isomorphism
\varphi_{V}:\sigma^{*}VV
called the
Frobenius
. We denote by
{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}
the category of
F
-isocrystals over
K_{0}.

Let
{\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}
be the category of pairs
(M,\varphi_{M})
, where
M
is a finite free
\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
-module, and
(M[1/p],\varphi_{M})
is an
F
-isocrystal. We shall often write
\varphi
for
\varphi_{M}
when no confusion can arise. Morphisms in this category are by definition morphisms of
\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
-modules that respect the Frobenii (after inverting
p
). We call an object of
{\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}
an
integral
F
-isocrystal over
K_{0}
. Note that
{\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}
contains the category of the usual
F
-crystals, but has the advantage of containing duals of objects.
We write

v:{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}}

for the forgetful functor, taking each
(M,\varphi_{M})
to
M
. (Here
v
stands for “vergessen”, as in
[
RZ96
, §1]
.)

Let
G
be a flat, finite-type, affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. By an
integral
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure over
K_{0}
, we mean a faithful, exact,
\otimes
-functor

\Upsilon:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}.

Equivalently,
\Upsilon
is a
\otimes
-functor such that the composition
v\circ\Upsilon:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\rightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}}
is a fiber functor. Note that we do not require
v\circ\Upsilon
to be equal to
\idfunc_{K_{0}}
.

Similarly, by an
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure over
K_{0}
, we mean a faithful, exact,
\otimes
-functor

\Upsilon:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}.

If
\Upsilon
is an integral
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure, then the similar construction as in §
4.1.1
yields a natural
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure
\Upsilon[1/p]
.

We denote the categories of integral
F
-isocrystals (resp.
F
-isocrystals) with
G
-structure over
K_{0}

by
{G\text{-}\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}
(resp.
{G\text{-}\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}
). In these categories, morphisms are by definition
\otimes
-isomorphisms between
\otimes
-functors
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}
(resp. between
\otimes
-functors.

4.2.2
.

Let
G
be a flat, finite-type, affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
that is definable by tensors, and fix a defining datum
(L,(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}})
for
G
(see §
4.1.7
). Let
(D,\varphi_{D})
be an object in
{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}
, equipped with a collection of
\varphi_{D}
-invariant tensors
(s_{\alpha,0})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}}\subset D^{\otimes}
, such that there
exists an
\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
-module isomorphism
L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}D
taking each
s_{\alpha}
to
s_{\alpha,0}
. (Obviously such a tuple
(D,\varphi_{D},(s_{\alpha,0})_{\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}})
always exists.)

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Keep the setting of §
4.2.2
. There exists an integral
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure
\Upsilon

equipped with an isomorphism
\iota:\Upsilon(L)(D,\varphi_{D})
in
{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}
such that
\iota
maps
\Upsilon(s_{\alpha})\in\Upsilon(L)^{\otimes}
to
s_{\alpha,0}
for each
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
. The pair
(\Upsilon,\iota)
is unique up to unique isomorphism in the following sense.
Given two such pairs
(\Upsilon,\iota),

(\Upsilon^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}),
there is a unique isomorphism
\Upsilon\Upsilon^{\prime}
in the category
{G\text{-}\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}
which takes
\iota
to
\iota^{\prime}.

Proof.

Fix an
\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
-module isomorphism
\iota:L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}\simeq D
taking each
s_{\alpha}
to
s_{\alpha,0}.

The composite map

L\otimes K_{0}\underset{\sigma^{*}\iota}{}\sigma^{*}D[1/p]\xrightarrow{\varphi_{D}}D[1/p]\underset{\iota^{-1}}{}L\otimes K_{0}

fixes
(s_{\alpha}),
and hence has the form
\delta\sigma
for some
\delta\in G(K_{0}).
For
Q
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G

we set
\Upsilon(Q)=Q\otimes\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
, and set
\varphi_{Q}:=\delta\sigma:\sigma^{*}Q[1/p]Q
. This shows the existence of
(\Upsilon,\iota).

To show uniqueness, let
(\Upsilon,\iota),

(\Upsilon^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}),
be two pairs as in the lemma. Let
\omega=v\circ\Upsilon
and
\omega^{\prime}=v\circ\Upsilon^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
4.1.8
, there is a unique isomorphism
\eta:\omega\omega^{\prime}
of fiber functors over
\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
taking
\iota
to
\iota^{\prime}.
In particular the isomorphism
\eta(L):\omega(L)\omega^{\prime}(L)
is compatible with the Frobenii on
\omega(L)[1/p]
and on
\omega^{\prime}(L)[1/p]
.
Since for any
Q
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G
, the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-representation
Q\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
of
G
is a subquotient of
(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})^{\otimes},
this implies that the isomorphism
\eta(Q):\omega(Q)\omega^{\prime}(Q)
is compatible with
the Frobenii on
\omega(Q)[1/p]
and on
\omega^{\prime}(Q)[1/p]
. Hence the isomorphism
\eta:\omega\omega^{\prime}
comes from a (necessarily unique) isomorphism
\Upsilon\Upsilon^{\prime}
in
{G\text{-}\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Keep the setting of §
4.2.2
, and let
(\Upsilon,\iota)
be a pair as in Lemma
4.2.3
. Let
\Upsilon^{\prime}
be an
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure, and let
\iota^{\prime}
be an isomorphism
\Upsilon^{\prime}(L[1/p])(D[1/p],\varphi_{D})
in
{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}
such that
\iota^{\prime}
maps
\Upsilon^{\prime}(s_{\alpha})\in\Upsilon^{\prime}(L[1/p])^{\otimes}
to
s_{\alpha,0}\in D[1/p]^{\otimes}
for each
\alpha\in\bm{\alpha}
. Then there is a unique isomorphism
\Upsilon[1/p]\Upsilon^{\prime}
in the category
{G\text{-}\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}
which takes
\iota
to
\iota^{\prime}.

Proof.

Let
\omega=v\circ\Upsilon[1/p]
and
\omega^{\prime}=v\circ\Upsilon^{\prime}[1/p]
. These are fiber functors
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{K_{0}}
. By Lemma
4.1.10
, there is a unique
\otimes
-isomorphism
\omega\omega^{\prime}
which takes
\iota
to
\iota^{\prime}
. By exactly the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.2.3
, this isomorphism comes from a unique isomorphism
\Upsilon[1/p]\Upsilon^{\prime}
in
{G\text{-}\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}
.
∎

4.2.5
.

Let
G
be a smooth affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with connected fibers. Let
\Upsilon
be an integral
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure over
K_{0}
, and write
\omega
for
v\circ\Upsilon
. By Lemma
4.1.11
,
P_{\omega}(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})
is a non-empty
G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})
-torsor. Then we have the
G(K_{0})
-torsor
Y(\omega)
, and a canonical
G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})
-torsor
Y(\omega)^{\circ}\subset Y(\omega)
, as in §
4.1.12
. In the sequel, we shall write
Y(\Upsilon)
and
Y(\Upsilon)^{\circ}
for
Y(\omega)
and
Y(\omega)^{\circ}
respectively.

Now consider an element
y\in Y(\Upsilon)
. For each
L
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G,
the
K_{0}
-linear
isomorphism
L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}K_{0}\Upsilon(L)[1/p]
induced by
y
allows us to view the Frobenius on
\Upsilon(L)[1/p]
as being given by

\delta_{y,L}\cdot\sigma
for some
\delta_{y,L}\in{\rm GL}(L\otimes K_{0}).
Since every representation in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G
contains a
G
-stable
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattice (see §
4.1.1
), the reconstruction theorem over a field implies that the elements
\delta_{y,L}
for all
L
come from a unique element
\delta_{y}\in G(K_{0})
. Thus we have obtained a map

Y(\Upsilon)\longrightarrow G(K_{0}),\quad y\longmapsto\delta_{y}.

It is clear that the set
\left\{\delta_{y}\mid y\in Y(\Upsilon)\right\}
is a
G(K_{0})
-orbit under
\sigma
-conjugation, and the set
\left\{\delta_{y}\mid y\in Y(\Upsilon)^{\circ}\right\}
is a
G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})
-orbit under
\sigma
-conjugation. We call the last
G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})
-orbit the
invariant
of
\Upsilon
, and denote it by
{\rm inv}(\Upsilon)
.

Lemma 4.2.6
.

Keep the assumptions in §
4.2.5
. The construction
\Upsilon\mapsto{\rm inv}(\Upsilon)
induces a bijection from the set of isomorphism classes in the category

{G\text{-}\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}
, to the set of
G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})
-
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
G(K_{0})
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that the map described in the lemma is well defined. The inverse of the map is induced by the following construction: Given
\delta\in G(K_{0}),
we define a functor
\Upsilon_{\delta}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\rightarrow{\mathrm{Isoc}}^{\circ}_{K_{0}}
by
sending
L
to
L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
and equipping
L\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}K_{0}
with
\delta\sigma
as the Frobenius.
∎

Definition 4.2.7
.

Let
G
be a reductive group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and let
\upsilon:\mathfrak{G}_{m}\to G_{\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}}
be a homomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
-group schemes. Let
\Upsilon
be an integral
F
-isocrystal with
G
-structure over
K_{0}
. With the notation as in §
4.2.5
, we define

Y_{\upsilon}(\Upsilon):=\left\{y\in Y(\Upsilon)\mid\delta_{y}\in G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})\upsilon(p)G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})\right\}.

This is a subset of
Y(\Upsilon)
stable under the
G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})
-action. We define

X_{\upsilon}(\Upsilon):=Y_{\upsilon}(\Upsilon)/G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}).

Remark 4.2.8
.

In the setting of Definition
4.2.7
, if we fix an element
y_{0}\in Y(\Upsilon)
and use it to identify the
G(K_{0})
-torsor
Y(\Upsilon)
with
G(K_{0})
, then
X_{\upsilon}(\Upsilon)
is identified with

\left\{g\in G(K_{0})/G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})\mid g^{-1}\delta_{y_{0}}\sigma(g)\in G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})\upsilon(p)G(\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}})\right\}.

When
K_{0}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}
, the above is the affine Deligne–Lusztig set
X_{\upsilon}(\delta_{y_{0}})
introduced in §
2.2.7
.

4.3.
Crystalline representations with
G
-structure

4.3.1
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}.
Let
K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension inside
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, and let
K_{0}
be the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
inside
K
.
22
22

22

The notations
K
and
K_{0}
are standard in
p
-adic Hodge theory. In the context of Shimura varieties, we often use
K
to denote the level. In such a case we will use notations such as
F
and
F_{0}
to denote
p
-adic fields.
We denote by
\RZ_{G}(K)
the set of pairs
(\mu,\delta)
, where
\mu
is a
K
-homomorphism
\mathbb{G}_{m,K}\to G_{K}
, and
\delta\in G(K_{0})
. These pairs are considered by Rapoport–Zink in
[
RZ96
, §1]
.

Let
\mathrm{MF}_{K}^{\varphi}
be the category of filtered
\varphi
-modules over
K.
This is a Tannakian category, equipped with the fiber functor
v:\mathrm{MF}_{K}^{\varphi}\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{K_{0}}
taking each filtered
\varphi
-module to its underlying
K_{0}
-vector space. There is a bijection

(\mu,\delta)\longmapsto\mathcal{I}_{\mu,\delta}

from the set
\RZ_{G}(K)
to the set of faithful exact
\otimes
-functors
\mathcal{I}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\to\mathrm{MF}_{K}^{\varphi}
such that
v\circ\mathcal{I}
is equal to the standard fiber functor

\idfunc_{K_{0}}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{K_{0}},\quad V\longmapsto V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}K_{0}.

We refer the reader to
[
RZ96
, §1]
for details.

We say that two elements
(\mu_{1},\delta_{1})
and
(\mu_{2},\delta_{2})
of
\RZ_{G}(K)
are
equivalent
, if
\mathcal{I}_{\mu_{1},\delta_{1}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mu_{2},\delta_{2}}
are
\otimes
-isomorphic. We denote this equivalence relation on
\RZ_{G}(K)
by
\sim
.
We call an element
(\mu,\delta)
of
\RZ_{G}(K)

admissible
,
if
\mathcal{I}_{\mu,\delta}
lands in
the subcategory
\mathrm{MF}^{\varphi,\mathrm{a}}_{K}
of admissible filtered
\varphi
-modules.
We call an element
(\mu,\delta)
of
\RZ_{G}(K)

neutral
, if
\kappa_{G}(\delta)=[\mu]\in\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p}}
. Here
\kappa_{G}
is the Kottwitz map
\B(G)\to\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma_{p}}
as in §
1.4.2
. We denote by
\RZ_{G}^{\adm}(K)
(resp.
\RZ_{G}^{\neu}(K)
) the subset of admissible (resp. neutral) elements of
\RZ_{G}(K)
. We also write
\RZ_{G}^{\adm,\neu}(K)
for
\RZ_{G}^{\adm}(K)\cap\RZ_{G}^{\neu}(K)
.

It is easy to see (cf.
[
RZ96
, Def. 1.23]
) that two elements
(\mu_{1},\delta_{1}),(\mu_{2},\delta_{2})\in\RZ_{G}(K)
are equivalent if and only if there exists
g\in G(K_{0})
such that
g\delta_{1}\sigma(g)^{-1}=\delta_{2}
and such that
\Int(g)\circ\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
define the same filtration (in the sense of
[
SR72
, IV, §2]
) on the fiber functor
\idfunc_{K}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{K}
. By
[
SR72
, IV, 2.2.5 (2)]
, the last condition on
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
implies that
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are conjugate by
G(K)
. From this we see that the subset
\RZ_{G}^{\adm}\subset\RZ_{G}
is invariant under the equivalence relation
\sim
. It is also clear that the subset
\RZ_{G}^{\neu}\subset\RZ_{G}
is invariant under
\sim
.

When
G
is a torus, the equivalence relation and the admissibility condition on
\mathrm{RZ}_{G}(K)
can be made more explicit as follows.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

Let
T
be a torus over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The following statements hold.

(i)

Two elements
(\mu_{1},\delta_{1}),(\mu_{2},\delta_{2})\in\RZ_{T}(K)
are equivalent if and only if
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
and
\delta_{1}
is
\sigma
-conjugate to
\delta_{2}
in
T(K_{0})
.

(ii)

We have
\RZ_{T}^{\adm}(K)=\RZ_{T}^{\neu}(K)
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows easily from the discussion in §
4.3.1
. For part (ii), we fix an embedding
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}\to\overline{\breve{\mathbb}{Q}}_{p}
as usual, and write
\breve{K}
for the compositum of
K
and
\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
inside
\overline{\breve{\mathbb}{Q}}_{p}
. The condition of being neutral is equivalent to the second condition in
[
RZ96
, Prop. 1.21]
. Thus by that proposition, an element
(\mu,\delta)\in\RZ_{T}(K)
is neutral if and only if the
\otimes
-functor
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}T\to\mathrm{MF}^{\varphi}_{\breve{K}}
obtained by base changing
\mathcal{I}_{\mu,\delta}
lands in
\mathrm{MF}^{\varphi,\adm}_{\breve{K}}
. Since the admissibility of a filtered
\varphi
-module over
K
is equivalent to the admissibility of its base change to
\breve{K}
, part (ii) follows.
∎

4.3.3
.

Keep the setting of §
4.3.1
. If
K^{\prime}/K
is a finite extension inside
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, then there is a natural map
\mathrm{RZ}_{G}(K)\to\mathrm{RZ}_{G}(K^{\prime})
, which sends equivalent elements to equivalent elements, and preserves neutrality and admissibility. We define

\mathrm{RZ}_{G}:=\varinjlim_{K}\mathrm{RZ}_{G}(K),

where
K
runs through finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
inside
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, and the transition maps are the ones mentioned above. Similarly, we define the three subsets
\RZ_{G}^{\adm}
,
\RZ_{G}^{\neu}
, and
\RZ_{G}^{\adm,\neu}
of
\RZ_{G}
by taking direct limits. We write
\sim
for the inherited equivalence relation on
\RZ_{G}.
The three subsets of
\RZ_{G}
introduced above are all stable under
\sim
.

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let
T
be a torus over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We have
\mathrm{RZ}^{\adm}_{T}=\RZ^{\neu}_{T}
. There is a natural bijection between
\mathrm{RZ}^{\adm}_{T}/{\sim}
and the set

\displaystyle\left\{(\mu,[\delta])\mid\mu\in X_{*}(T),[\delta]\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})/(1-\sigma),\kappa_{T}([\delta])=[\mu]\right\}.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.3.2
.
∎

4.3.5
.

Keep the setting of §
4.3.1
. We say that a homomorphism

\rho:\Gamma_{K}\longrightarrow G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

is a
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-valued crystalline representation of
\Gamma_{K}
, if for some faithful representation
V
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G
, the homomorphism
\Gamma_{K}\to{\rm GL}(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
arising from
\rho
is a crystalline representation. This condition is in fact independent of the choice of
V
. We denote by
\Crys_{G}(K)
the set of all such
\rho
.

We define

\Crys_{G}:=\varinjlim_{K}\Crys_{G}(K),

where
K
runs through finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
inside
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, and the transition maps are given by restriction. We have a natural
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-action on
\Crys_{G}
by conjugation. We denote the resulting equivalence relation on
\Crys_{G}
by
\sim
.

For
\rho\in\Crys_{G}(K)
, we denote the image of
\rho
in
\Crys_{G}
by
[\rho]
. From
\rho
, we obtain a faithful exact
\otimes
-functor
\mathcal{I}_{\rho}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\to\mathrm{MF}^{\varphi}_{K}
taking each
V
to

D_{\cris}(V)=(B_{\cris}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}V)^{\Gamma_{K}},

where
V
is viewed as a crystalline representation of
\Gamma_{K}
via
\rho
. (The fact that
\mathcal{I}_{\rho}
is a faithful exact
\otimes
-functor follows from the fundamental properties of
D_{\cris}

[
Fon79
, §3.4]
.) We thus obtain a fiber functor
v\circ\mathcal{I}_{\rho}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{K_{0}}
. By Steinberg’s theorem, the
G_{K_{0}}
-torsor
\SIsom^{\otimes}(\idfunc_{K_{0}},v\circ\mathcal{I}_{\rho})
becomes trivial after a finite unramified extension of
K_{0}
. Hence if we replace
K
by a suitable finite extension, then we may assume that this torsor is trivial. In this case,
\mathcal{I}_{\rho}
is
\otimes
-isomorphic to
\mathcal{I}_{\mu,\delta}
for some
(\mu,\delta)\in\mathrm{RZ}_{G}(K)
which is unique up to equivalence; see §
4.3.1
. The image of
(\mu,\delta)
in
\mathrm{RZ}_{G}/{\sim}
is independent of all choices, and it depends on
\rho
only via its image in
\Crys_{G}/{\sim}
. Mapping
\rho
to
(\mu,\delta)
, we have obtained a well-defined map

D_{\cris}^{G}:\Crys_{G}/{\sim}\longrightarrow\mathrm{RZ}_{G}/{\sim}.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

The map
D_{\cris}^{G}
is injective with image
\mathrm{RZ}^{\adm,\neu}_{G}/{\sim}
.

Proof.

Firstly, it is clear from the definitions that
\im(D_{\cris}^{G})\subset\RZ_{G}^{\adm}/{\sim}
.

For each finite extension
K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
inside
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, we let
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris}
be the category of crystalline representations of
\Gamma_{K}
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Then
D_{\cris}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris}\to\mathrm{MF}_{K}^{\varphi,\adm}
is a
\otimes
-equivalence of
\otimes
-categories, with a quasi-inverse given by the functor

V_{\cris}:D\longmapsto{\rm Fil}^{0}(B_{\cris}\otimes_{K_{0}}D)^{\varphi=1}

(which is also a
\otimes
-functor). Let
u:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris}\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
be the functor sending a crystalline representation to its underlying
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space.

By a result of Wintenberger
[
Win97
]
, we know that an element
(\mu,\delta)\in\RZ_{G}^{\adm}(K)
is neutral if and only if the composite
\otimes
-functor

u\circ V_{\cris}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mu,\delta}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}G\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}

is
\otimes
-isomorphic to the standard fiber functor
\idfunc_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
23
23

23

This was originally a conjecture of Rapoport–Zink; see the paragraph below
[
RZ96
, Prop. 1.20]
. Wintenberger showed in
[
Win97
]
that it is a consequence of the Colmez–Fontaine Theorem
[
CF00
]
.
From this result, it is clear that
\im(D_{\cris}^{G})\subset\RZ_{G}^{\adm,\neu}/{\sim}
.

Now we construct a map
\mathrm{RZ}^{\adm,\neu}_{G}/{\sim}\to\Crys_{G}/{\sim}
inverse to
D_{\cris}^{G}
. Let
(\mu,\delta)\in\mathrm{RZ}^{\adm,\neu}_{G}(K)
. By the result of Wintenberger mentioned above, the
\otimes
-functor
{\mathcal{F}}:=u\circ V_{\cris}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mu,\delta}
is
\otimes
-isomorphic to
\idfunc_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. By composing the tautological homomorphism
\Gamma_{K}\to\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}(u)(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
with the natural homomorphism
\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}(u)(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}({\mathcal{F}})(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, we obtain a homomorphism
\rho^{\prime}:\Gamma_{K}\to\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}({\mathcal{F}})(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. By choosing a
\otimes
-isomorphism between
\idfunc_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
, we identify the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-group
\underline{\Aut}^{\otimes}({\mathcal{F}})
with

G
, and identify
\rho^{\prime}
with a homomorphism
\rho:\Gamma_{K}\to G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Clearly the
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-conjugacy class of
\rho

is independent of choices. It is straightforward to check that the construction
(\mu,\delta)\mapsto\rho
gives rise to the desired inverse map of
D_{\cris}^{G}
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.7
.

Let
T
be a torus over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The map
D_{\cris}^{T}
induces a bijection

\Crys_{T}\left\{(\mu,[\delta])\mid\mu\in X_{*}(T),[\delta]\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})/(1-\sigma),\kappa_{T}([\delta])=[\mu]\right\}.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
4.3.4
and Proposition
4.3.6
. (Note that
\Crys_{T}=\Crys_{T}/{\sim}
.)
∎

4.3.8
.

Let
T
be a cuspidal torus over
\mathbb{Q}
(see Definition
1.5.4
). We call an element
\delta\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})

motivic
, if for some
n\geq 1,
the element
\gamma=\delta\sigma(\delta)\dots\sigma^{n-1}(\delta)
is in
T(\mathbb{Q})
, and is a
p
-unit (i.e.,
\gamma
lies in a compact open subgroup of
T(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
). We denote by
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}\subset T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
the subset of motivic elements. Note that
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}
is stable under
\sigma
-conjugation by
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
. We denote by
{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}
the connected Néron model of
T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let
\overset{\circ}{\sim}
be the equivalence relation on
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}
defined by
\sigma
-conjugation by
{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
. As in §
1.4.2
, we denote by
w_{T}:T(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\to X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{p,0}}
the Kottwitz homomorphism, which is surjective. By
[
Rap05
, Rmk. 2.2 (iii)]
, we have
\ker(w_{T})={\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
. Hence the restriction of
w_{T}
to
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}
factors through
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}/{\overset{\circ}{\sim}}
.

Lemma 4.3.9
.

In the setting of §
4.3.8
, the map
w_{T}
induces a bijection

(4.3.9.1)

\displaystyle T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}/{\overset{\circ}{\sim}}X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{p,0}}.

Proof.

The surjectivity of (
4.3.9.1
) follows from the second construction in
[
Kis17
, §4.3.9]
. We explain this in more detail. Let
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
, and let
L/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite Galois extension inside
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
splitting
T
. Let
L_{p}
be the completion of
L
at the place above
p
determined by the fixed embedding
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, and let
\pi
be a uniformizer of
L_{p}
. Then the same argument as in
loc. cit.
(with
\mu_{h_{T}}
replaced by
\mu
) shows that there exist
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and a
p
-unit
\gamma\in T(\mathbb{Q})
such that
\N_{L_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(\mu(\pi))^{s}\gamma^{-1}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. By Greenberg’s theorem
[
Gre63
, Prop. 3]
, the map
{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})\to{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p}),c\mapsto c\sigma(c)^{-1}
is surjective. Hence we can find
c\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
such that
24
24

24

In the last paragraph of
[
Kis17
, §4.3.9]
, it is used that such
c
can be found in
T(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

c\N_{L_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(\mu(\pi))^{s}\sigma(c)^{-1}=\gamma.

Let

\delta:=c\N_{L_{p}/L_{p,0}}(\mu(\pi))\sigma(c)^{-1}\in T(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}),

where
L_{p,0}
is the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
inside
L_{p}
. As in
loc. cit.,
we have
\delta\sigma(\delta)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(\delta)=\gamma
, and we have
\delta\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
, where
n=s[L_{p,0}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
. Hence
\delta\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}
. We now check that
w_{T}(\delta)
equals the image of
\mu
in
X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{p,0}}
, which will prove the surjectivity of (
4.3.9.1
). For this, it suffices to show that
w_{T}(\N_{L_{p}/L_{p,0}}(\mu(\pi)))
equals the image of
\mu
, since
w_{T}(c)
is trivial. Writing
F
for
L_{p,0}
, we have
X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{F}}=X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{p,0}}
. Let
\sigma_{F}=\sigma^{[F:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]}
. By
[
Kot85
, §2.5]
,
w_{T}
induces a bijection

\B_{F}(T):=\left\{\sigma_{F}\text{-conjugacy classes in }T(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\right\}X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{F}},

whose inverse is induced by
\mu\mapsto\N_{L_{p}/F}(\mu(\pi)).
(Here we use that
T
splits over
L_{p}
and that
L_{p}/F
is totally ramified.) This gives what we want.

For the injectivity of (
4.3.9.1
), let
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}
be such that
w_{T}(\delta_{1})=w_{T}(\delta_{2})
. Let
\delta=\delta_{1}\delta_{2}^{-1}
. Then
\delta
is also motivic, so we can choose
n
such that
\gamma:=\delta\sigma(\delta)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(\delta)
is a
p
-unit in
T(\mathbb{Q})
. Since
w_{T}(\delta)=0
, we have
\delta\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
, and in particular
\gamma\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. Therefore
\gamma
lies in a congruence subgroup of
T(\mathbb{Q})
, and has finite order by Lemma
1.5.5
. Up to enlarging
n
, we may assume that
\gamma=1
.
Again by Greenberg’s theorem, we can write
\delta\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
as
c\sigma(c)^{-1}
, for some
c\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})
. Since
\gamma=1
, we have
c\sigma^{n}(c)^{-1}=1
, i.e.,
c\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\mathbb{Z}_{p^{n}})
. Hence
\delta_{1}=\delta\delta_{2}=c\delta_{2}\sigma(c)^{-1}
with
c\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
, which means
\delta_{1}\overset{\circ}{\sim}\delta_{2}.

∎

Lemma 4.3.10
.

In the setting of §
4.3.8
, the map
w_{T}
induces a bijection from the set of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
-
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}
to
X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{p}}
.

Proof.

The map
w_{T}
induces a group isomorphism
T(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})/{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\breve{\mathbb}{Z}_{p})X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{p,0}}
. In view of Lemma
1.6.8
, this induces a group isomorphism
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})/{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})X_{*}(T)_{\Gamma_{p,0}}
, which is equivariant for the natural actions of
\sigma
on the two sides. The lemma follows from this fact and Lemma
4.3.9
.
∎

Definition 4.3.11
.

Let
T
be a cuspidal torus over
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\Mot_{T}
be the subset of
\Crys_{T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
consisting of those
[\rho]
whose image under the bijection in Corollary
4.3.7
is of the form
(\mu,[\delta])
where
[\delta]\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})/(1-\sigma)
is in the image of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})^{\mot}
. Note that the definition of
\Mot_{T}
depends on
T
over
\mathbb{Q}
, not just
T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

Proposition 4.3.12
.

In the setting of Definition
4.3.11
, the
\mu
-component of the map
D_{\cris}^{T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
induces a bijection

\mathscr{M}_{T}:\Mot_{T}X_{*}(T).

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
4.3.7
and Lemma
4.3.10
.
∎

4.3.13
.

Let
T
be a cuspidal torus over
\mathbb{Q}
. We now use class field theory to construct certain
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-valued global Galois representations, and show that their localizations at places above
p
give rise to elements of
\Mot_{T}
.

Let
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
, and let
E_{\mu}\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
be the field of definition of
\mu
. Similar to §
1.5.3
, we consider the composite homomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic groups

\displaystyle r(\mu)^{\alg}:\Res_{E_{\mu}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\Res_{E_{\mu}/\mathbb{Q}}\mu}\Res_{E_{\mu}/\mathbb{Q}}T\xrightarrow{\N_{E_{\mu}/\mathbb{Q}}}T.

We have an induced homomorphism between topological groups

(4.3.13.1)

\displaystyle E_{\mu}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{E_{\mu}}^{\times}\longrightarrow T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}).

By Lemma
1.5.5
,
T(\mathbb{Q})
is discrete in
T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, and so we have

T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})=\varprojlim_{U}T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})/U,

where
U
runs through compact open subgroups of
T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. For each such
U
, we have a natural map
\pi_{0}(T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}))\to T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})/U
, cf. §
1.5.3
. In the limit we obtain a map

(4.3.13.2)

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}))\longrightarrow T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f}).

The composition

(4.3.13.3)

\displaystyle E_{\mu}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{E_{\mu}}^{\times}\xrightarrow{(\ref {eq:ST rec law})}T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}))\xrightarrow{(\ref {eq:pi_0T})}T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})

factors through the global Artin map
E_{\mu}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{E_{\mu}}^{\times}\to\pi_{0}(E_{\mu}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{E_{\mu}}^{\times})\cong{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu})
. (Recall from §
1.5.3
that we take the geometric normalization of the global Artin map.) We thus obtain a map

r(\mu):{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu})\longrightarrow T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f}).

Let
U\subset T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a neat compact open subgroup. Since
T(\mathbb{Q})
is discrete in
T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we have
T(\mathbb{Q})\cap U=\left\{1\right\}
(cf. the proof of Lemma
1.5.7
). The kernel of the projection

\pi(U):T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})\to T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{A}_{f})/U

is
T(\mathbb{Q})\backslash T(\mathbb{Q})U
, which we identify with
U
, using that
T(\mathbb{Q})\cap U=\left\{1\right\}
. Let
E_{\mu,U}/E_{\mu}
be the finite extension inside
E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu}
such that
{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu,U})
is the kernel of
\pi_{U}\circ r(\mu)
. Then
r(\mu)
induces a homomorphism

r(\mu)_{U}:{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu,U})\longrightarrow\ker\pi_{U}\cong U.

We denote by
r(\mu)_{U,p}
the composite homomorphism

{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu,U})\xrightarrow{r(\mu)_{U}}U\hookrightarrow T(\mathbb{A}_{f})\xrightarrow{\text{projection}}T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

The fixed embeddings
E_{\mu,U}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
give rise to a place
v
of
E_{\mu,U}
above
p
. Let
K=E_{\mu,U,v}\subset\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
. We denote by
r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}
the composite map

\Gamma_{K}={\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}/K)\longrightarrow{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu,U})\xrightarrow{r(\mu)_{U,p}}T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Proposition 4.3.14
.

In the setting of §
4.3.13
, assume in addition that
U
is of the form
U^{p}U_{p}
, where
U^{p}
is a neat compact open subgroup of
T(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
and
U_{p}
is a compact open subgroup of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Then
r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}:\Gamma_{K}\to T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-valued crystalline representation. The element
[r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}]\in\Crys_{T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
lies in
\Mot_{T}
. Moreover, the image of
[r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}]
under the bijection
\mathscr{M}_{T}
in Proposition
4.3.12
is
-\mu
.

Proof.

Let
f
be the composite map of topological groups

K^{\times}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Art}_{K}}\Gamma_{K}^{\ab}\longrightarrow{\rm Gal}(E^{\ab}_{\mu}/E_{\mu,U})\xrightarrow{r(\mu)_{U}}U,

where
\mathrm{Art}_{K}
is the local Artin map (normalized geometrically). Let
F
be the completion of
E_{\mu}
inside
K=E_{\mu,U,v}
. Let
f_{1}
be the composite map of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebraic groups

\Res_{K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\N_{K/F}}\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\hookrightarrow(\Res_{E_{\mu}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\xrightarrow{r(\mu)^{\alg}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}}T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}.

Then
f_{1}
induces a map

K^{\times}=(\Res_{K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m})(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\xrightarrow{f_{1}}T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\hookrightarrow T(\mathbb{A}_{f}),

which we again denote by
f_{1}
.

We claim that
f
and
f_{1}
induce the same map
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\to T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. In fact, by the definition of
r(\mu)_{U}
and the compatibility of the local and global Artin maps, we have

(4.3.14.1)

\displaystyle f_{1}(x)\in f(x)T(\mathbb{Q})\subset T(\mathbb{A}_{f}),\quad\forall x\in K^{\times}.

Take
x\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
, and let
\gamma\in T(\mathbb{Q})
be such that
f_{1}(x)=f(x)\gamma
. Note that
f_{1}(x)
lies inside the maximal compact subgroup
U_{p,\max}
of
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, by the compactness of
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
. Hence
\gamma
lies in
U^{p}U_{p,\max}
, which is a neat compact open subgroup of
T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
by the neatness of
U^{p}
. Since
T(\mathbb{Q})
is discrete in
T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we have
\gamma=1
. The claim is proved.

Now to check that
r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}
is crystalline, we take an arbitrary representation
V
of
T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and check that
\Gamma_{K}\xrightarrow{r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}}T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to{\rm GL}(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is crystalline. For this, it suffices to check that the composition
K^{\times}\xrightarrow{f}U\to T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to{\rm GL}(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
agrees with a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebraic group homomorphism
\Res_{K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\to{\rm GL}(V)
on
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
, by a well-known criterion in
p
-adic Hodge theory (see for instance
[
Con11
, Prop. B.4 (i)]
and the remark following it). But this follows immediately from our claim proved above.

We now check that
[r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}]\in\Crys_{T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
lies in
\Mot_{T}
. Let
[\delta]\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})/(1-\sigma)
be the element attached to
[r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}]
as in Corollary
4.3.7
, and let
\delta\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
be a representative of
[\delta]
. By taking a faithful representation of
T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and applying
[
Con11
, Prop. B.4 (ii)]
, we know that up to enlarging
n
the element

\gamma_{n}:=\delta\sigma(\delta)\cdots\sigma^{n-1}(\delta)\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

is equal to
25
25

25

Note that in
[
Con11
]
the arithmetic normalization of the local Artin map is used, which is opposite to our normalization. This results in the sign difference in the exponent in the expression below.

[f(\pi)_{p}^{-1}f_{1}(\pi)]^{-n/n_{K}}\in T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Here
\pi
is a uniformizer of
K
,
n_{K}
is the residue degree of
K
, and
f(\pi)_{p}
denotes the component at
p
of
f(\pi)\in U
. It remains to show that
f(\pi)_{p}f_{1}(\pi)^{-1}
is a
p
-unit in
T(\mathbb{Q})
.
By (
4.3.14.1
) there exists
\gamma\in T(\mathbb{Q})
such that
f_{1}(\pi)=f(\pi)\gamma\in T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Then
f(\pi)^{-1}_{p}f_{1}(\pi)
equals the image of
\gamma
in
T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. In addition,
\gamma
and
f(\pi)^{-1}
have the same image in
T(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
, which shows that
\gamma\in U^{p}
. Hence
\gamma
is a
p
-unit.

Finally, we check that
\mathscr{M}_{T}([r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}])=-\mu
. Let
\mathbb{C}_{p}
be the completion of
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
. For each faithful representation
V
of
T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, the
\Gamma_{K}
-representation on
V
induced by
r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}
is Hodge–Tate, and we have the cocharacter
h_{V}:\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{C}_{p}}\to{\rm GL}(V)_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
as in
[
Ser79
, §1.4]
. We know that
h_{V}
factors through
T_{\mathbb{C}_{p}}
(see
loc. cit.
), and the resulting
Hodge–Tate cocharacter

\mu_{\mathrm{HT}}\in X_{*}(T)
is independent of the choice of
V
by the functoriality of the construction. Since the filtration on
D_{\dR}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
jumps at
-1
, it is easy to see that
\mathscr{M}_{T}([r(\mu)_{U,p,\mathrm{loc}}])=-\mu_{\mathrm{HT}}
. We are left to check that
\mu_{\mathrm{HT}}=\mu
.

Let
T^{\prime}=\Res_{K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
. We identify
T^{\prime}_{\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}}
with
\prod_{\tau\in\Hom_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(K,\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p})}\mathbb{G}_{m,\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}}
, and define
\mu^{\prime}\in X_{*}(T^{\prime})
by
\mu^{\prime}(z)=(z,1,\cdots,1)
, where the first spot corresponds to the canonical embedding
K\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
. Let
r^{\prime}:\Gamma_{K,0}\to\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
be the Lubin–Tate character (cf.
[
Ser79
, §2.1]
). Then
r^{\prime}
is a
T^{\prime}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-valued crystalline representation. Since
\mathrm{Art}_{K}\circ r^{\prime}
is the inclusion
\Gamma_{K,0}\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{K}
(thanks to the geometric normalization of
\mathrm{Art}_{K}
), we know that the restriction of
r(\mu)_{U,p}
to
\Gamma_{K,0}
equals the composition

\Gamma_{K,0}\xrightarrow{r^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\xrightarrow{f}U\to T(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Moreover, by our previous claim that
f
and
f_{1}
induce the same map
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\to T(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we know that the above composition is equal to the composition of
r^{\prime}
with
f_{1}:K^{\times}\to T(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Therefore if we let
\mu^{\prime}_{\mathrm{HT}}\in X_{*}(T^{\prime})
be the Hodge–Tate cocharacter of
r^{\prime}
, then
\mu_{\mathrm{HT}}
equals
f_{1}\circ\mu^{\prime}_{\mathrm{HT}}
. (Recall that
f_{1}
is an algebraic homomorphism
T^{\prime}\to T_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.) By the last paragraph of
[
Ser79
, §2.1]
, we have
\mu^{\prime}_{\mathrm{HT}}=\mu^{\prime}
. Therefore
\mu_{\mathrm{HT}}=f_{1}\circ\mu^{\prime}
, which is easily seen to be equal to
\mu
.
∎

4.4.
Crystalline lattices with
G
-structure

4.4.1
.

Let
K
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
inside
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, with residue field
k
. Let
K_{0}
be the maximal unramified extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
inside
K_{0}
, and let
\sigma\in\Aut(K_{0})
be the arithmetic
p
-Frobenius.

We write
W
for
W(k)=\mathcal{O}_{K_{0}}
. Fix a uniformizer
\pi
of
K,
and let
E=E(u)
be its Eisenstein polynomial over
K_{0}
.
We set
\mathfrak{S}=W[\![u]\!]
, and let
\varphi
be the endomorphism of
\mathfrak{S}
that restricts to
\sigma
on
W
and sends
u
to
u^{p}.
We have a
W
-algebra isomorphism
\mathfrak{S}/(E)\mathcal{O}_{K}
, which sends
u\mod(E)
to
\pi
. Thus we have specialization maps
\mathfrak{S}\to\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
\mathfrak{S}\to W
, sending
u
to
\pi
and
0
respectively. Using these two maps we view
K
and
K_{0}
as
\mathfrak{S}
-algebras respectively. (These
\mathfrak{S}
-algebra structures are not compatible with the inclusion
K_{0}\hookrightarrow K
.)

For any height
1
prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\mathfrak{S}
, the localization
{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is a DVR, and we write
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for its completion.

We denote by
\Mod_{/\mathfrak{S}}^{\varphi}
the category of pairs
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi_{\mathfrak{M}})
, where
\mathfrak{M}
is a finite free
\mathfrak{S}
-module, and
\varphi_{\mathfrak{M}}
is a
\mathfrak{S}
-module
isomorphism
\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}[1/E]\mathfrak{M}[1/E].
For such a pair
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi_{\mathfrak{M}})
, the
\mathfrak{S}
-module
\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}
carries a filtration, given by

{\rm Fil}^{i}(\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M})=\varphi_{\mathfrak{M}}^{-1}(E^{i}\mathfrak{M})\cap\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}\subset\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}[1/E]
for
i\in\mathbb Z.

Let

{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris\circ}
be the category of
\Gamma_{K}
-stable
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattices in crystalline representations of
\Gamma_{K}
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Recall from
[
Kis10
, §1.2]
that there is a faithful
\otimes
-functor

\mathfrak{M}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris\circ}\longrightarrow\Mod_{/\mathfrak{S}}^{\varphi}.

For each
L
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris\circ}
, the following statements hold (see
loc. cit.
).

(i)

There is a canonical isomorphism

\mathfrak{M}(L)\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}K_{0}\cong D_{\cris}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})

of isocrystals over
K_{0}
. The Frobenius on the left is induced by
\varphi_{\mathfrak{M}(L)}
. This isomorphism is functorial in
L
and compatible with tensor products.

(ii)

There is a canonical isomorphism

(4.4.1.1)

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}(L)\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}K\cong D_{\dR}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})

of filtered
K
-vector spaces. The filtration on the left is induced by the filtration on
\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}(L)
. This isomorphism is functorial in
L
and compatible with tensor products.

(iii)

There is a faithfully flat and formally étale
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(p)}
-algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{E}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}},
and a canonical
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{E}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}
-linear isomorphism

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{E}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}L\cong\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{E}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}}\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}\mathfrak{M}(L).

This isomorphism is functorial in
L
and compatible with tensor products.

We set
M_{\cris}(L):=\mathfrak{M}(L)\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}W
. Thus
M_{\cris}
is a
\otimes
-functor

{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris\circ}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}.

By (i) above,
M_{\cris}(L)
is a
W
-lattice in the
K_{0}
-vector space
D_{\cris}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. The following property is proved by Tong Liu in
[
Liu18
, §4]
.

(iv)

Inside
D_{\cris}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, the
W
-lattice
M_{\cris}(L)
is independent of the choice of a uniformizer in
F
(which is needed to define the functor
\mathfrak{M}
). Moreover, if
K^{\prime}/K
is a finite extension in
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
and
L^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K^{\prime}}}^{\cris\circ}
denotes
L
equipped with the inherited
\Gamma_{K^{\prime}}
-action, then we have a canonical identification

M_{\cris}(L^{\prime})\cong M_{\cris}(L)\otimes_{W(k)}W(k^{\prime}).

This is compatible with the usual identification

D_{\cris}(L^{\prime})\cong D_{\cris}(L)\otimes_{K_{0}}K_{0}^{\prime}.

Here
k^{\prime}
denotes the residue field of
K^{\prime}
, and
K_{0}^{\prime}
denotes
W(k^{\prime})[1/p]
.

The filtered isomorphism (
4.4.1.1
) is induced by another canonical filtered isomorphism, which we now recall. Note that
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(E)}
is a complete DVR with residue field
K
, which has characteristic zero. Hence
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(E)}
is canonically a
K
-algebra, and the
K
-algebra structure is compatible with the natural
W
-algebra structure. The following statements follow from the proof of
[
Kis10
, Thm. 1.2.1]
, and
[
Kis06
, Lem. 1.2.12 (4)]
. For each
L
in
{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris\circ}
, there is a canonical filtered isomorphism

(4.4.1.2)

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}(L)\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(E)}D_{\dR}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})\otimes_{K}\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(E)}.

Here the filtration on the right is the tensor product filtration, coming from the filtration on
D_{\dR}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and the
E
-adic filtration on
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(E)}
. The filtration on the left is the one induced by the filtration on
\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}(L)
(which is also the same as the tensor product filtration coming from the filtration on
\varphi^{*}\mathfrak{M}(L)
and the
E
-adic filtration on
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(E)}
). Now (
4.4.1.1
) is induced by (
4.4.1.2
) by passing to the residue field
K
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{(E)}
.

4.4.2
.

Let
G
be a flat, finite-type, affine group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. We say that a homomorphism

\rho:\Gamma_{K}\longrightarrow G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})

is a
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-valued crystalline representation
, if the composition of
\rho
with the inclusion
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\subset G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-valued crystalline representation as in §
4.3.5
.

Given a
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-valued crystalline representation
\rho
, we obtain a tautological functor

\sideset{{}_{\rho}}{}{\idfunc}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris\circ},

sending each
L
to the
\Gamma_{K}
-stable lattice
L
in the crystalline representation
L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

We shall need a generalization of
\sideset{{}_{\rho}}{}{\idfunc}
. Let
\Gamma_{K}
act on the left on
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
by

\gamma(gG(\mathbb{Z}_{p})):=\rho(\gamma)gG(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\quad\forall\gamma\in\Gamma_{K},g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Since
\rho(\Gamma_{K})\subset G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, the coset of
1
in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is fixed by
\Gamma_{p}
. Now let
\lambda\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
be a point fixed by
\Gamma_{K}.
Let

\sideset{{}_{\rho}}{{}^{\lambda}}{\idfunc}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\Gamma_{K}}^{\cris\circ}

be the functor sending each
L
to the
\Gamma_{K}
-stable lattice
\lambda\cdot L
inside
L\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

We define
\otimes
-functors

\omega_{\rho}^{\lambda}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}\circ\sideset{{}_{\rho}}{{}^{\lambda}}{\idfunc}}\Mod_{/\mathfrak{S}}^{\varphi}\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{\mathfrak{S}},\quad L\mapsto\mathfrak{M}(\lambda\cdot L)

and

\omega_{\rho,0}^{\lambda}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\xrightarrow{M_{\cris}\circ\sideset{{}_{\rho}}{{}^{\lambda}}{\idfunc}}{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}\to{\mathrm{Mod}}^{\mathrm{fp}}_{W},\quad L\mapsto M_{\cris}(\lambda\cdot L).

Here the last arrows in both cases are the natural forgetful functors.
Clearly

\omega_{\rho,0}^{\lambda}=(\omega_{\rho}^{\lambda})_{W}.

We also write
\Upsilon_{\rho}^{\lambda}
for the
\otimes
-functor

M_{\cris}\circ\sideset{{}_{\rho}}{{}^{\lambda}}{\idfunc}:{\mathrm{Rep}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}G\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Isoc}}_{K_{0}}^{\circ}.

When
\lambda
is the coset of
1
, we omit it from the superscripts.
Note that the definition of
\omega_{\rho}^{\lambda}
