\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})
are canonically identified. Define

\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}):=\varprojlim_{\phi\in\mathscr{J}}\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi}).

If we view
\beta
as an element of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})
, then
\mathscr{J}^{\beta}
depends only on
\mathscr{J}
and
\beta
, not on
\phi\in\mathscr{J}
.

Similarly, for different choices of
x\in\mathscr{I}
, the groups
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x})
are canonically identified. Similarly as above we define

\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}):=\varprojlim_{x\in\mathscr{I}}\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x}).

The canonical isomorphisms
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x})
for all
\phi\in\mathscr{J}
and
x\in\mathscr{I}
induce a canonical isomorphism

\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}).

We have the twisted isogeny class
\mathscr{I}^{\beta}
constructed in
[
Kis17
, §4.4.7, Prop. 4.4.8]
.
If we view
\beta
as an element of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})
, then
\mathscr{I}^{\beta}
depends only on
\mathscr{I}
and
\beta
.

By §
2.6.16
, the abelian groups
\mathcal{H}(\phi)
and
\mathcal{H}(\phi^{\beta})
are canonically identified, since
\phi\approx\phi^{\beta}
. Similarly, by §
5.10.5
, the abelian groups
{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
and
{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi^{\beta})
are canonically identified.

5.12.4
.

We take this opportunity to correct a mistake in
[
Kis10
, §3.1]
, and we freely use the notation
introduced there. Above we used a twisting construction defined in
[
Kis10
, §3.1]
. The statement of Lemma 3.1.5 of
loc. cit.
should include the condition that the
Z
-torsor
\mathcal{P},
is trivial over
\mathbb R
, i.e., that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a trivial
Z_{\mathbb{R}}
-torsor. The result is not true without this condition, as the
\mathbb{Q}
-isogeny
\lambda^{\mathcal{P}}

need not be a weak polarization.

Unfortunately, this error was imported into
[
Kis17
, §4.1.6]
and
[
KP18
, Lemma 4.4.8]
via citation.
Fortunately, in all instances where this construction is applied to Shimura varieties in these papers, the condition of triviality at

\infty
holds. Moreover, the result is always applied to give a moduli theoretic description of a construction defined via complex uniformization. Thus, logically, the fact that
\lambda^{\mathcal{P}}
is a weak polarization is never used, but rather follows

a posteriori
in all these cases.

Nevertheless, let us explain why
\lambda^{\mathcal{P}}
is a weak polarization if
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb R}
is trivial.
Recall that for any
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R,
an
R
-isogeny

\phi:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{*}
is an element of
\Hom({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{*})\otimes R
which has an inverse in
\Hom({\mathcal{A}}^{*},{\mathcal{A}})\otimes R,
cf.
[
Kot92b
, §9]
. If
R
is a subring of
\mathbb{R},
we say that
\phi
is an
R
-polarization if it is an

R
-linear combination of polarizations, with positive coefficients. We say
\phi
is a weak
R
-polarization if
\phi
or
-\phi
is a polarization. Thus a weak
\mathbb{Q}
-polarization is the same thing as a weak polarization.
Now if
\phi_{0}:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{*}
is a

\mathbb{Q}
-isogeny, then
\phi_{0}
is a weak polarization if and only if it is a weak
\mathbb{R}
-polarization when viewed as an

\mathbb{R}
-isogeny. This follows from that fact that the set of
\mathbb{Q}
-polarizations is a convex cone in
\Hom({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{*})\otimes\mathbb{Q}.

Similarly
\phi_{0}
is a weak polarization if and only if
\phi_{0}
is a weak
\mathbb{R}
-polarization.
Moreover, the set of weak
\mathbb{R}
-polarizations is stable under multiplication by
\mathbb{R}^{\times}.

We now return to the explanation that
\lambda^{\mathcal{P}}
is a weak polarization if
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb R}
is trivial.
Thus, suppose
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb R}
is trivial, and let
x\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb R).
Specializing the commutative
diagram
[
Kis10
, (3.1.6)]
by
x,
we see that
f_{c}(x)^{-1}\lambda^{\mathcal{P}}
is a weak
\mathbb{R}
-polarization.
Thus, by what we just saw,
\lambda^{\mathcal{P}}
is a weak
\mathbb{R}
-polarization and hence a weak polarization.

Theorem 5.12.5
.

Keep the setting of §
5.12.3
. Assume that
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
is amicable. Then
(\mathscr{I}^{\beta},\mathscr{J}^{\beta})
is again amicable. Moreover, the elements

(\tau^{{\mathfrak{H}}}(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}),\tau^{{\mathcal{H}}}(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J}))\in{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)\oplus{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)

and

(\tau^{{\mathfrak{H}}}(\mathscr{I}^{\beta},\mathscr{J}^{\beta}),\tau^{{\mathcal{H}}}(\mathscr{I}^{\beta},\mathscr{J}^{\beta}))\in{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi^{\beta})\oplus{\mathcal{H}}(\phi^{\beta})

(see Definition
5.10.9
) correspond to each other under the canonical identification
{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)\oplus\mathcal{H}(\phi)\cong{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi^{\beta})\oplus\mathcal{H}(\phi^{\beta})
.

Proof.

Let
T
be a maximal torus in
I_{\phi}
such that
\beta
comes from a class
\beta_{T}
in
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T)
. Such a maximal torus always exists by
[
Bor98
, Thm. 5.10]
. Let
j^{\prime}:T\hookrightarrow I_{\phi}
be the inclusion map. As in the proof of Theorem
5.12.2
, we reduce the theorem to the following situation.

•

There is a special fork
({\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})=(T,h,i,i^{\prime})
. We have
\phi=\phi({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})
, and
j^{\prime}
is the canonical embedding, namely the one whose composition with
I_{\phi,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is
i^{\prime}
. We have
x_{{\mathfrak{s}}}\in\mathscr{I}
. Moreover, in the canonical
I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})
-orbit of isomorphisms
I_{x_{{\mathfrak{s}}}}I_{\phi}
, we can find an isomorphism
f
such that
f^{-1}\circ j^{\prime}:T\to I_{x_{{\mathfrak{s}}}}
is the canonical embedding.

Define
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
and
\Sha_{G_{\mathrm{der}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T^{\dagger})
as in §
5.11.2
, with respect to the special fork
(T,h,i,i^{\prime})
. We will still need to modify our choice of
i
, but note that
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
and
\Sha_{G_{\mathrm{der}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T^{\dagger})
are already determined by
(T,i^{\prime})
. Also note that
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
is equal to the preimage of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})
under the map
\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)\to\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})
induced by
j^{\prime}
.
By
[
Kis17
, Lem. 4.4.5]
, the map of pointed sets
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{R},T)\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{R},I_{\phi})
induced by
j^{\prime}
has trivial kernel. Hence
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
is equal to the preimage of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})
under the map
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T)\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})
induced by
j^{\prime}
. We conclude that
\beta_{T}\in\Sha^{\infty}_{G}(\mathbb{Q},T)
. Now by Lemma
1.2.6
, we can find a class
\beta_{0}\in\Sha_{G_{\mathrm{der}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T^{\dagger})
lifting
\beta_{T}
. Fix a cocycle
\beta_{0}(\cdot)
representing
\beta_{0}
.

Choose
\lambda^{\prime}\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
such that

(5.12.5.1)

\displaystyle i^{\prime}(\beta_{0}(\rho))={\lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda^{\prime},\quad\forall{\rho}\in\Gamma.

Since
\beta_{0}
is trivial at infinity, we have

\lambda^{\prime}\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})i^{\prime}(T^{\dagger}(\mathbb{C}))
. Since
G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{Q})G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
is equal to
G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})
(by real approximation), and since
\lambda^{\prime}
is determined by (
5.12.5.1
) up to left multiplication by
G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{Q})
, we may and shall assume that

(5.12.5.2)

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime}\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}i^{\prime}(T^{\dagger}(\mathbb{C})).

We define

\displaystyle i^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Int(\lambda^{\prime})\circ i^{\prime}:T\longrightarrow G.

By (
5.12.5.1
),
i_{1}^{\prime}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Choose an arbitrary
y^{\prime}\in X(\Psi_{T,h})_{\ntr}
, and choose
y\in Y(\Upsilon_{(T,h)})^{\circ}
as in Lemma
5.11.5
with respect to
(T,h,i^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. Then
{\mathfrak{g}}=(T,h,i,i^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})
is a quasi-gauge, and
(\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{g}}},\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{g}}})=(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
. Since
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
is amicable,
{\mathfrak{g}}
has a rectification
u
by Lemma
5.11.7
. Since
u\in\ker(G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{*})\to\pi^{*}(G))
, we can write
u=u^{(0)}u^{(1)}
, with
u^{(0)}\in G(\mathbb{Q})_{+}
and
u^{(1)}\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{A}^{*}_{f}){\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}})
. Note that
(T,h,\Int(u^{(0)})^{-1}\circ i,i^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})
is still a gauge, and that
u^{(1)}
is a rectification of it. Moreover, writing
{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}
for the special point datum
(T,\Int(u^{(0)})^{-1}\circ i,h)
, we have
x_{{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}}\in\mathscr{I}
, and in the canonical
I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})
-orbit of isomorphisms
I_{x_{{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}}}I_{\phi}
we can find an isomorphism
f_{0}
such that
f_{0}^{-1}\circ j^{\prime}:T\to I_{x_{{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}}}
is the canonical embedding. Therefore after replacing
i
by
\Int(u^{(0)})^{-1}\circ i
we can arrange that
{\mathfrak{g}}
admits a rectification
u\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{*}){\mathcal{G}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}})
, and that there exists a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-adapted isomorphism
I_{x_{\mathfrak{g}}}I_{\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.

By the same argument as before, there exists
\lambda\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
such that

(5.12.5.3)

\displaystyle i(\beta_{0}(\rho))={\lambda}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda,\quad\forall{\rho}\in\Gamma,

and

(5.12.5.4)

\displaystyle\lambda\in G_{\mathrm{der}}(\mathbb{R})^{+}i(T^{\dagger}(\mathbb{C})).

We define

\displaystyle i_{1}

\displaystyle:=\Int(\lambda)\circ i:T\longrightarrow G,

which is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
by (
5.12.5.3
). By (
5.12.5.2
), (
5.12.5.4
), and the fact that
i\circ h
and
i^{\prime}\circ h
lie in the same connected component of
X
, we know that all four points

i_{1}\circ h,~i^{\prime}_{1}\circ h,~i\circ h,~i^{\prime}\circ h

lie in the same connected component of
X
. It is also clear that
i_{1}
and
i_{1}^{\prime}
are conjugate by
G^{\ad}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. Hence
(T,h,i_{1},i_{1}^{\prime})
is a special fork. It follows that the tuple
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}=(T,h,i_{1},i_{1}^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})
is a quasi-gauge, by our choice of
y
(see Lemma
5.11.5
). The same argument as in the proof of
[
Kis17
, Cor. 4.6.5]
shows that
(\mathscr{I}^{\beta},\mathscr{J}^{\beta})=(\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}},\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}})
. Now
{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}_{1},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
satisfy all the assumptions in Proposition
5.11.13
, so the current theorem follows from Lemma
5.11.7
, Proposition
5.11.13
, and Proposition
5.11.16
.
∎

5.13.
Construction and properties of a bijection

5.13.1
.

In the current setting of Hodge type, it is expected that there should be a canonical bijection between the set of conjugacy classes of admissible morphisms
\mathfrak{Q}\to\mathfrak{G}_{G}
and the set of isogeny classes in
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}(\overline{\mathbb F}_{p})
. One candidate for such a bijection is constructed in
[
Kis17
]
. However, even giving a general characterization of what “canonical” should mean for such a bijection seems to be out of current reach. In the following, we construct such a bijection in a way that is different from
[
Kis17
]
(cf. Remark
5.13.8
below).
Throughout this subsection we keep the setting of §
5.1
.

We write
{\mathbb I}
for the set of isogeny classes in
\mathscr{S}_{K_{p}}(\overline{\mathbb F}_{p})
, and write
{\mathbb J}
for the set of conjugacy classes of admissible morphisms
\mathfrak{Q}\to\mathfrak{G}_{G}
.
37
37

37

In §
2.6.16
, the set
{\mathbb J}
was also denoted by
\mathcal{AM}/\mathrm{conj}
. In the current setting of Hodge type we choose the notation
{\mathbb J}
to reflect the symmetry with the set
{\mathbb I}
of isogeny classes.

In §
2.6.16
, we defined an equivalence relation
\approx
on the set of admissible morphisms
\mathfrak{Q}\to\mathfrak{G}_{G}
. This descends to an equivalence relation on
{\mathbb J}
, which we still denote by
\approx
. By Proposition
2.6.12
, for
\mathscr{J}_{1},\mathscr{J}_{2}\in{\mathbb J}
we have
\mathscr{J}_{1}\approx\mathscr{J}_{2}
if and only if
\mathscr{J}_{1}=\mathscr{J}_{2}^{\beta}
for some (unique)
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{1})
. (See §
5.12.3
for the notations
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{1})
and
\mathscr{J}_{1}^{\beta}
.) When this is the case, for any
\phi_{1}\in\mathscr{J}_{1}
and
\phi_{2}\in\mathscr{J}_{2}
we have a canonical equivalence class of inner twistings between
I_{\phi_{1}}
and
I_{\phi_{2}}
. The induced equivalence class of inner twistings between
I_{\phi_{1},\mathbb{R}}
and
I_{\phi_{2},\mathbb{R}}
is trivial, i.e., it contains an
\mathbb{R}
-isomorphism. Thus we have an induced canonical isomorphism
\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{1}})\cong\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{2}})
, which restricts to an isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{1}})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi_{2}})
. If we view the last isomorphism as an isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{1})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{2})
, then it depends only on
\mathscr{J}_{1}
and
\mathscr{J}_{2}
, not on any other choices.

Similarly, we define a binary relation
\approx
on
{\mathbb I}
by declaring
\mathscr{I}_{1}\approx\mathscr{I}_{2}
when there exists
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{1})
such that
\mathscr{I}_{2}=\mathscr{I}_{1}^{\beta}
. (In Corollary
5.13.4
below we will see that
\approx
is an equivalence relation on
{\mathbb I}
.) Similarly as before, if
\mathscr{I}_{1}\approx\mathscr{I}_{2}
then for any
x_{1}\in\mathscr{I}_{1}
and
x_{2}\in\mathscr{I}_{2}
there is a canonical equivalence class of inner twistings between
I_{x_{1}}
and
I_{x_{2}}
, and moreover the induced equivalence class of inner twistings between
I_{x_{1},\mathbb{R}}
and
I_{x_{2},\mathbb{R}}
is trivial. In fact, in this case the
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme
I_{x_{1},x_{2}}
considered in §
5.4
is an
I_{x_{1}}
-torsor, and its class in
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x_{1}})
is the element
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{1})
with
\mathscr{I}_{2}=\mathscr{I}_{1}^{\beta}
; see the proof of
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.4.8]
. The above-mentioned equivalence class of inner twistings between
I_{x_{1}}
and
I_{x_{2}}
is induced by elements of
I_{x_{1},x_{2}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. In particular, the element of
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x_{1}}^{\ad})
corresponding to this equivalence class of inner twistings (see Remark
1.2.3
) is the image of
\beta
under the natural map
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x_{1}})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x_{1}}^{\beta})
. Since
\beta
has trivial image in
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{R},I_{x_{1}})
, our assertion that the induced equivalence class of inner twistings between
I_{x_{1},\mathbb{R}}
and
I_{x_{2},\mathbb{R}}
is trivial follows.

From the above discussion, we have a canonical isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x_{1}})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{x_{2}})
. Again, if we view this isomorphism as an isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\mathscr{I}_{1})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\mathscr{I}_{2})
, then it depends only on
\mathscr{I}_{1}
and
\mathscr{I}_{2}
.

Lemma 5.13.2
.

Let
\mathscr{J}_{1},\mathscr{J}_{2}\in{\mathbb J}
. Assume that
\mathscr{J}_{2}=\mathscr{J}_{1}^{\beta}
for some
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{1})
. Let
\beta^{\prime}\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{1})
, also viewed as an element of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{2})
via the canonical isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{1})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{2})
. Then
\mathscr{J}_{1}^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}=\mathscr{J}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We first make a reduction step that is very similar to the proof of Theorem
5.12.5
. Let
\phi\in\mathscr{J}_{1}
. By
[
Bor98
, Thm. 5.10]
, there exists a maximal torus
T\subset I_{\phi}
such that both
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
come from elements
\beta_{T}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{T}
of
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T)
. By Theorem
3.3.9
, we reduce to the case where
\phi=\phi(T,i,h)
for some
(T,i,h)\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
, and where the inclusion
T\hookrightarrow I_{\phi}
is the canonical inclusion (namely the one whose composition with
I_{\phi,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is
i
). Define
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
to be the kernel of
\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)\to\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},G)
induced by
i
. By the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
5.12.5
, we have
\beta_{T},\beta^{\prime}_{T}\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
. Now fix cocycles
\beta_{T}(\cdot)
and
\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\cdot)
representing
\beta_{T}
and
\beta_{T}^{\prime}
, and find
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
such that

\lambda_{1}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}=i(\beta_{T}(\rho)),\quad\lambda_{2}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda_{2}=i(\beta_{T}(\rho)\beta^{\prime}_{T}(\rho)),\quad\forall\rho\in\Gamma.

Define
i_{1}=\Int(\lambda_{1})\circ i
and
i_{2}=\Int(\lambda_{2})\circ i
. Then
(T,i_{1},h)
and
(T,i_{2},h)
are special point data in
\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
. Moreover, it is easy to check that
\mathscr{J}_{2}=\mathscr{J}_{(T,i_{1},h)}
and
\mathscr{J}_{1}^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}=\mathscr{J}_{(T,i_{2},h)}
.

To finish the proof, we need to show that
\mathscr{J}_{(T,i_{2},h)}
is equal to
\mathscr{J}_{(T,i_{1},h)}^{\beta^{\prime}}
. By the same argument as before, we need only show that there exists
\lambda\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

We have
\lambda^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda=i_{1}(\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho)),~\forall\rho\in\Gamma
.

(ii)

We have
i_{2}=\Int(\lambda)\circ i_{1}
.

Clearly
\lambda=\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1}
satisfies the second condition. To check the first condition, we compute

\displaystyle\lambda^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda_{2}{\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}^{-1}=\lambda_{1}i\big(\beta_{T}(\rho)\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho)\big){\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}i(\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho)){\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}^{-1}={\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}i(\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho)){\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}^{-1}.

Since
i
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, the above is equal to

{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\bigg(\lambda_{1}i\big({\rho^{-1}}(\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho))\big)\lambda_{1}^{-1}\bigg)={\vphantom{\rho}}^{\rho}\bigg(i_{1}\big({\rho^{-1}}(\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho))\big)\bigg).

But
i_{1}
is also defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, so the above is equal to
i_{1}(\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho))
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.13.3
.

Let
\mathscr{I}_{1},\mathscr{I}_{2}\in{\mathbb I}
. Assume that
\mathscr{I}_{2}=\mathscr{I}_{1}^{\beta}
for some
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{1})
. Let
\beta^{\prime}\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{1})
, also viewed as an element of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{2})
via the canonical isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{1})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{2})
. Then
\mathscr{I}_{1}^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}=\mathscr{I}_{2}^{\beta^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to that of Lemma
5.13.2
. Let
x\in\mathscr{I}_{1}
. By
[
Bor98
, Thm. 5.10]
, there exists a maximal torus
T\subset I_{x}
such that both
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
come from elements
\beta_{T}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{T}
of
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T)
. By Theorem
5.7.8
, we reduce to the case where
x=x_{(T,i,h)}
for some
(T,i,h)\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
, and where the inclusion
T\hookrightarrow I_{x}
is the canonical inclusion (as in (
5.7.2.2
)). Define
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
to be the kernel of the map
\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)\to\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},G)
induced by
i
. As in the proof of Lemma
5.13.2
, we have
\beta_{T},\beta^{\prime}_{T}\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T)
. Now fix cocycles
\beta_{T}(\cdot)
and
\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\cdot)
representing
\beta_{T}
and
\beta_{T}^{\prime}
, and find
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
such that

\lambda_{1}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda_{1}=i(\beta_{T}(\rho)),\quad\lambda_{2}^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda_{2}=i(\beta_{T}(\rho)\beta^{\prime}_{T}(\rho)),\quad\forall\rho\in\Gamma.

Define
i_{1}=\Int(\lambda_{1})\circ i
and
i_{2}=\Int(\lambda_{2})\circ i
. Then
(T,i_{1},h)
and
(T,i_{2},h)
are special point data in
\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
. Moreover, the same argument as in the proof of
[
Kis17
, Cor. 4.6.5]
shows that
\mathscr{I}_{2}=\mathscr{I}_{(T,i_{1},h)}
and
\mathscr{I}_{1}^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}=\mathscr{I}_{(T,i_{2},h)}
.

To finish the proof, we need to show that
\mathscr{I}_{(T,i_{2},h)}
is equal to
\mathscr{I}_{(T,i_{1},h)}^{\beta^{\prime}}
. By the same argument as before, we need only show that there exists
\lambda\in G(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

We have
\lambda^{-1}{\vphantom{\rho}}{}^{\rho}\lambda=i_{1}(\beta_{T}^{\prime}(\rho)),~\forall\rho\in\Gamma
.

(ii)

We have
i_{2}=\Int(\lambda)\circ i_{1}
.

As in the proof of Lemma
5.13.2
, the element
\lambda=\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1}
satisfies the above conditions.
∎

Corollary 5.13.4
.

The relation
\approx
on
{\mathbb I}
is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

To see reflexivity, for each
\mathscr{I}\in{\mathbb I}
we have by definition
\mathscr{I}=\mathscr{I}^{\beta}
with
\beta=0\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})
. The transitivity and symmetry follow directly from Lemma
5.13.3
.
∎

5.13.5
.

For
\mathscr{I}\in{\mathbb I}
and
y\in\bar{Y}(\mathscr{I})
, the image of
{\mathfrak{k}}(y)\in{{\mathfrak{KT}}^{\mathrm{str}}/{\equiv}}
under
{{\mathfrak{KT}}^{\mathrm{str}}/{\equiv}}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
depends only on
\mathscr{I}
. We denote this element by
{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathscr{I})
. Thus we have a map
{\mathbb I}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
. Similarly, for
\mathscr{J}\in{\mathbb J}
and
y\in\bar{Y}(\mathscr{J})
, the image of
[{\mathfrak{k}}(y)]\subset{{\mathfrak{KT}}^{\mathrm{str}}/{\equiv}}
under
{{\mathfrak{KT}}^{\mathrm{str}}/{\equiv}}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
consists of a unique element, and this element depends only on
\mathscr{J}
. We denote this element by
{\mathfrak{k}}(\mathscr{J})
. Thus we have a map
{\mathbb J}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
.

It follows from Theorem
3.3.9
, Theorem
5.7.8
, Proposition
5.7.6
, and Proposition
5.9.2
, that the images of
{\mathbb I}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
and
{\mathbb J}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
are both equal to
\left\{{\mathfrak{k}}({\mathfrak{s}})\mid{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)\right\}
. (See §
5.3.9
for
{\mathfrak{k}}({\mathfrak{s}})
.) We denote this set by
{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
.

5.13.6
.

Let
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
be a weakly amicable pair. For each
x\in\mathscr{I}
and
\phi\in\mathscr{J}
, we have an isomorphism
I_{x}I_{\phi}
that is canonical up to composing with inner automorphisms defined over
\mathbb{Q}
; see §
5.10.3
and Remark
5.10.10
. We thus have a canonical isomorphism of abelian groups

(5.13.6.1)

\displaystyle\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},\mathscr{J})

that is independent of all choices (also cf. §
5.12.3
).

As a special case, let
{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
be a special point datum and consider the pair
(\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{s}}},\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{s}}})
. By Corollary
5.11.9
,
(\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{s}}},\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{s}}})
is amicable. Hence we get a canonical identification

\displaystyle\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{s}}})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}).

We denote the identified abelian group by
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{s}})
.

More generally, we call a pair
(\mathscr{I}^{\prime},\mathscr{J}^{\prime})
consisting of
\mathscr{I}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb I}
and
\mathscr{J}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb J}
an
acquainted pair
, if there exists a weakly amicable pair
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
such that
\mathscr{I}^{\prime}\approx\mathscr{I}
and
\mathscr{J}^{\prime}\approx\mathscr{J}
. Recall that in this case we have canonical isomorphisms
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}^{\prime})
and
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}^{\prime})
. Composing these two with the isomorphisms (
5.13.6.1
) with respect to the weakly amicable pair
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
, we obtain a canonical isomorphism

\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}^{\prime})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}^{\prime})

which depends only on the acquainted pair
(\mathscr{I}^{\prime},\mathscr{J}^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.13.7
.

There exists a (non-canonical) bijection

\bij:{\mathbb J}{\mathbb I}
satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

For each
\mathscr{J}\in{\mathbb J}
, there exists a special point datum
{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
and an element
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{s}})
such that
\mathscr{J}=\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\beta}
and
\bij(\mathscr{J})=\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\beta}
.

(ii)

Whenever
\mathscr{I}=\bij(\mathscr{J})
, the pair
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
is an acquainted pair. In particular we have a canonical isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})
.

(iii)

Suppose we have
\mathscr{I}=\bij(\mathscr{J})
. For any
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})
, we have
\bij(\mathscr{J}^{\beta})=\mathscr{I}^{\beta}.

Remark 5.13.8
.

A similar bijection
{\mathbb J}{\mathbb I}
is implicitly used in
[
Kis17
]
; see the proof of
[
Kis17
, Cor. 4.6.5]
. However, the bijection in
[
Kis17
]
satisfies a different set of conditions than those in Lemma
5.13.7
. More specifically, the groups
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{s}})
and
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})
in conditions (i) and (iii) in Lemma
5.13.7
are replaced by the subgroups consisting of elements that come from the centers of
I_{\phi({\mathfrak{s}})}
and
I_{\phi}
(for
\phi\in\mathscr{J}
).

Proof of Lema
5.13.7
.

As we explained in §
5.13.5
, the maps
{\mathbb J}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
and
{\mathbb I}\to{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
have the same image
{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}\subset{\mathfrak{KT}}/{\sim}
. By
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.5.7]
, each fiber of the map
{\mathbb J}\to{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
is contained in one equivalence class of
\approx
. By
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.4.13]
, each fiber of the map
{\mathbb I}\to{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
is contained in one equivalence class of
\approx
.

By
[
Kis17
, Lem. 4.4.11, Prop. 4.4.13, Lem. 4.5.6, Prop. 4.5.7]
, we know that there is an equivalence relation on
{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
whose pull-back to
{\mathbb J}
is
\approx
and whose pull-back to
{\mathbb I}
is
\approx
. We denote this equivalence relation on
{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
also by
\approx
. In the current proof we do not need an explicit description of this equivalence relation.
38
38

38

In the notation of
[
Kis17
, §4]
, we have
{\mathfrak{k}}_{1}\approx{\mathfrak{k}}_{2}
in
{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
if and only if there exists
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I)
such that
{\mathfrak{k}}_{2}={\mathfrak{k}}_{1}^{\beta}
. Here
I
is the reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
associated with
{\mathfrak{k}}_{1}
, which is unique up to an isomorphism that is canonical up to composing with inner automorphisms defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

From the above discussion, we have natural bijections
{\mathbb J}/{\approx}{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}/{\approx}
and
{\mathbb I}/{\approx}{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}/{\approx}
.
We now fix a set of representatives
\left\{\mathfrak{k}_{j}\mid j\in J\right\}\subset{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
for the equivalence relation
\approx
on
{(\mathfrak{KT}/{\sim})^{\mathrm{sp}}}
. For each
j\in J
, we choose a special point datum
{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
such that
\mathfrak{k}_{j}={\mathfrak{k}}({\mathfrak{s}}_{j})
. We then let

\mathscr{J}_{j}:=\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}}
and
\mathscr{I}_{j}:=\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}}.

Consider the subset
B:=\left\{\mathscr{J}_{j}\mid j\in J\right\}
of
{\mathbb J}
, and the subset
A:=\left\{\mathscr{I}_{j}\mid j\in J\right\}
of
{\mathbb I}
. For each
j\in J
, we have a canonical identification
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I}_{j})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J}_{j})
. As in §
5.13.6
, we denote the identified group by
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{s}}_{j})
.

Define the set

D:=\left\{(j,\beta)\mid j\in J,\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{s}}_{j})\right\}.

Then we have a map
\mathscr{G}:D\to{\mathbb J}
sending
(j,\beta)
to
\mathscr{J}_{j}^{\beta}
, and a map
\mathscr{F}:D\to{\mathbb I}
sending
(j,\beta)
to
\mathscr{I}_{j}^{\beta}
. Clearly
B
(resp.
A
) is a set of representatives for the equivalence relation
\approx
on
{\mathbb J}
(resp. on
{\mathbb I}
). It follows that both
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{F}
are surjective. Moreover, by
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.4.8, Lem. 4.5.6]
, both
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{F}
are injective, and so they are bijections.

We now define the desired bijection
\bij
to be
\mathscr{F}\circ\mathscr{G}^{-1}
. Then condition (i) follows from the construction. Condition (ii) follows from condition (i). We are left to check condition (iii). Suppose we have
\bij(\mathscr{J})=\mathscr{I}
. Let
(j,\beta^{\prime})=\mathscr{G}^{-1}(\mathscr{J})
. Then
\mathscr{J}=\mathscr{J}_{j}^{\beta^{\prime}}
and
\mathscr{I}=\mathscr{I}_{j}^{\beta^{\prime}}
. Let
\beta
be an arbitrary element of
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})
. By Lemma
5.13.2
, we have
\mathscr{J}^{\beta}=\mathscr{J}_{j}^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}
. Hence
\bij(\mathscr{J}^{\beta})=\mathscr{I}_{j}^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}
by the definition of
\bij
. But by Lemma
5.13.3
, we have
\mathscr{I}_{j}^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}=(\mathscr{I}_{j}^{\beta^{\prime}})^{\beta}
, and this is equal to
\mathscr{I}^{\beta}
. Therefore
\bij(\mathscr{J}^{\beta})=\mathscr{I}^{\beta}
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 5.13.9
.

There exists a (non-canonical) bijection

\bij:{\mathbb J}{\mathbb I}

satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

Whenever
\mathscr{I}=\bij(\mathscr{J})
, the pair
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
is an amicable pair. In particular we have a canonical isomorphism
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})
.

(ii)

Suppose we have
\mathscr{I}=\bij(\mathscr{J})
. For any
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{I})\cong\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathscr{J})
, we have
\bij(\mathscr{J}^{\beta})=\mathscr{I}^{\beta}.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the bijection
\bij
as in Lemma
5.13.7
satisfies the conditions. Condition (i) in the theorem follows from condition (i) in Lemma
5.13.7
, Corollary
5.11.9
, and Theorem
5.12.5
. Condition (ii) in the theorem is just condition (iii) in Lemma
5.13.7
.
∎

5.13.10
.

Fix a bijection
\bij
as in Theorem
5.13.9
. Let
\mathscr{J}\in{\mathbb J}
and let
\phi\in\mathscr{J}
. Since
(\bij(\mathscr{J}),\mathscr{J})
is an amicable pair, we have the elements
\tau^{{\mathfrak{H}}}(\bij(\mathscr{J}),\mathscr{J})\in{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
and
\tau^{{\mathcal{H}}}(\bij(\mathscr{J}),\mathscr{J})\in{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)
as in Definition
5.10.9
. We also write
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}(\phi)
for
\tau^{{\mathcal{H}}}(\bij(\mathscr{J}),\mathscr{J})
. The assignment
\phi\mapsto\underline{\tau}_{\bij}(\phi)
is thus an element of
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})
, in the notation of Definition
2.6.17
. We denote this element by
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}
. Since
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}(\phi)
depends on
\phi
only via its conjugacy class (that is, after we canonically identify all
{\mathcal{H}}(\phi^{\prime})
for
\phi^{\prime}
in the conjugacy class of
\phi
), we know that
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{1}
.

Corollary 5.13.11
.

We have
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}
.

Proof.

This follows from the two conditions satisfied by
\bij
in Theorem
5.13.9
and Theorem
5.12.5
.
∎

6.
The Langlands–Rapoport–
\tau
Conjecture in case of abelian type

6.1.
More sheaves on the set of admissible morphisms

6.1.1
.

Let
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
be an unramified Shimura datum. We let
(G^{\ad},X^{\ad})
be the adjoint Shimura datum. Let
{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}
be the adjoint group of
{\mathcal{G}}
, which is a reductive model of
G^{\ad}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

As in §
2.6.16
, we write
\mathcal{AM}(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
and
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad},X^{\ad},p,{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad})
for the sets of admissible morphisms with respect to
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
and
(G^{\ad},X^{\ad},p,{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad})
respectively. For simplicity, in the sequel we denote these two sets by
\mathcal{AM}(G)
and
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
respectively. Since
(G,X)
is arbitrary, our discussion below regarding
\mathcal{AM}(G)
will also be valid for
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
.

Definition 6.1.2
.

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
, we define the
\mathbb{Q}
-reductive group
I_{\phi}^{\dagger}
, the abelian group
{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
, and the map
I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})\to{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
in exactly the same way as in §
5.10.5
. Taking the direct sum of the map
I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})\to{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
and the quotient map
I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})\to{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)=I_{\phi}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\backslash I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})
, we obtain a natural map
I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)\oplus\mathcal{H}(\phi)
. We denote its image by
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\phi)
. We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{\dagger}(\phi)
the abelian group
\mathfrak{E}(Z_{\phi}^{\dagger},I_{\phi}^{\dagger};\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Here
Z_{\phi}^{\dagger}
denotes the center of
I_{\phi}^{\dagger}
as usual.

6.1.3
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
. We have a natural map
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}(\phi)\to{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
induced by the identity map on
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},Z_{\phi}^{\dagger})
. We also have a natural map
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}(\phi)\to{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)
induced by the inclusions
Z_{\phi}^{\dagger}\hookrightarrow Z_{I_{\phi}}
and
I_{\phi}^{\dagger}\hookrightarrow I_{\phi}
, in view of the presentation of
{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)
as a quotient of
\mathfrak{E}(Z_{I_{\phi}},I_{\phi};\mathbb{A}_{f})
as in Lemma
2.6.14
. We thus have a natural map

(6.1.3.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}(\phi)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)\oplus{\mathcal{H}}(\phi).

As in (
5.10.5.3
), we have the boundary map
I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},Z_{\phi}^{\dagger})
arising from the short exact sequence
1\to Z_{\phi}^{\dagger}\to I_{\phi}^{\dagger}\to I_{\phi}^{\ad}\to 1
. The image of the map is
\mathfrak{D}(Z_{\phi}^{\dagger},I_{\phi}^{\dagger};\mathbb{A}_{f})\cong\mathfrak{E}(Z_{\phi}^{\dagger},I_{\phi}^{\dagger};\mathbb{A}_{f})={\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}(\phi)
.
It follows that the image of (
6.1.3.1
) is precisely

\mathcal{H}^{+}(\phi)
. In particular,
\mathcal{H}^{+}(\phi)
is a subgroup of the abelian group
{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)\oplus\mathcal{H}(\phi)
since (
6.1.3.1
) is a group homomorphism.

As in §
2.6.16
, we view
\mathcal{AM}(G)
as a discrete topological space. We have already defined the sheaf of abelian groups
{\mathcal{H}}
on
\mathcal{AM}(G)
, whose stalk at each
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
is
{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)
. We now define similarly sheaves of abelian groups
{\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{H}}^{+},{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}
on
\mathcal{AM}(G).
In §
2.6.16
we saw that
{\mathcal{H}}
descends canonically to a sheaf on
\mathcal{AM}(G)/{\approx}
, since we have a canonical isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}(\phi_{1})\cong{\mathcal{H}}(\phi_{2})
whenever
\phi_{1}\approx\phi_{2}
, and such canonical isomorphisms satisfy the cocycle relation. Similarly, the sheaves
{\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{H}}^{+},{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}
on
\mathcal{AM}(G)
all descend canonically to sheaves on
\mathcal{AM}(G)/{\approx}
.

In the next definition we introduce notations analogous to those in Definition
2.6.17
.

Definition 6.1.4
.

For
\mathcal{F}\in\left\{{\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{H}}^{+},{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}\right\}
, let
\Gamma({\mathcal{F}})
denote the set of global sections of the sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
\mathcal{AM}(G)
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\approx}
denote the canonical descent of
{\mathcal{F}}
over
\mathcal{AM}(G)/{\approx}
. Let
\Gamma({\mathcal{F}})_{0}
denote the subset of
\Gamma({\mathcal{F}})
consisting of those global sections that descend to global sections of
{\mathcal{F}}_{\approx}
.

6.1.5
.

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G),
we have natural maps
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}(\phi)\to{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\phi)\to{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)\oplus{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)
, and the map
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}(\phi)\to{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\phi)
is surjective. It follows that we have natural maps
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})\to\Gamma({\mathfrak{H}})\oplus\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})
, which restrict to natural maps
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathfrak{H}})_{0}\oplus\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}
. Clearly the maps
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})
and
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})_{0}
are surjective.

Definition 6.1.6
.

We say that an element
\underline{\tau}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}\in\Gamma({\mathfrak{H}})
is
tori-rational
, if for each
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
and each maximal torus
T
in
I_{\phi}
, the element
\underline{\tau}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}(\phi)\in{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
has trivial image in

\cok({\mathcal{G}}^{\ab}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\xrightarrow{\partial}\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T^{\dagger})/\Sha_{G_{\mathrm{der}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T^{\dagger})).

Here the definition of the above group, as well as the definition of the natural map from
{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)
to the above group, are the same as in §
5.12.1
.
We say that an element
\underline{\tau}^{+}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})
is
tori-rational
, if its image in
\Gamma({\mathfrak{H}})
is tori-rational.

Lemma 6.1.7
.

Let
\underline{\tau}^{+}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})
be a tori-rational element. Then its image in
{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)
is tori-rational (see Definition
2.6.19
).

Proof.

It suffices to note that for each
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
and each maximal torus
T
in
I_{\phi}
, the composition
{\mathcal{G}}^{\ab}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\xrightarrow{\partial}\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T^{\dagger})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T)
is the zero map. This is indeed the case, since
\partial
is induced by the boundary map
G^{\ab}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\dagger})
associated with the short exact sequence
1\to T^{\dagger}\to T\to G^{\ab}\to 1
.
∎

6.1.8
.

We let
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger,\ad},{\mathcal{H}}^{+,\ad},{\mathfrak{H}}^{\ad},{\mathcal{H}}^{\ad}
be the sheaves on
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
defined in the same way as
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger},{\mathcal{H}}^{+},{\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{H}}
, with
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
replaced by
(G^{\ad},X^{\ad},p,{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad})
. We shall apply Definition
6.1.4
and Definition
6.1.6
to these sheaves.

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
, it is easy to see that the composition of
\phi
with the natural morphism
\mathfrak{G}_{G}\to\mathfrak{G}_{G^{\ad}}
is an admissible morphism
\phi^{\ad}:\mathfrak{Q}\to\mathfrak{G}_{G^{\ad}}
in
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
. Thus we have a natural map
\mathcal{AM}(G)\to\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad}),\phi\mapsto\phi^{\ad}
. This further induces a map
\mathcal{AM}(G)/{\approx}\to\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})/{\approx}
.
39
39

39

Here we have used the same symbol
\approx
for equivalence relations on
\mathcal{AM}(G)
and
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
. They are defined separately, with respect to the two unramified Shimura data
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
and
(G^{\ad},X^{\ad},p,{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad})
.

Lemma 6.1.9
.

Let
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
. Let
\mathcal{U}(\phi_{0})
be the set of conjugacy classes of
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
such that
\phi^{\ad}
is conjugate to
\phi_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{U}(\phi_{0})
is non-empty and is acted on transitively by the abelian group
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G})
, where the action is given by the usual twisting construction (see Proposition
2.6.12
).

Proof.

We claim that

\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G})=\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G})\cap\im(\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G_{\mathrm{sc}}})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G})).

In fact, as in the proof of
[
Kis17
, Lem. 3.4.8]
, the group on the right hand side is the same as

\ker(\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G})\to\coh^{1}(\mathbb{R},Z_{G})\oplus\coh^{1}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},G)),

which is equal to

\ker(\Sha^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G})\to\Sha^{\infty}_{\ab}(\mathbb{Q},G)),

and equal to
\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},Z_{G})
. The claim is proved. The lemma now follows from the claim and
[
Kis17
, Lem. 3.4.8, Prop. 3.4.11]
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.10
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
. The natural map
I_{\phi}\to I_{\phi^{\ad}}
is surjective with kernel
Z_{G}
(which is canonically a
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
I_{\phi}
). In particular, we have a natural identification
I_{\phi}^{\ad}\cong I_{\phi^{\ad}}^{\ad}
, and a natural surjective map

{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)=I_{\phi}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\backslash I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{\phi}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}(\phi^{\ad})=I_{{\phi^{\ad}}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\backslash I_{{\phi^{\ad}}}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{{\phi^{\ad}}}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q}).

Proof.

As a subgroup of
G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
,
I_{\phi,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is the centralizer of
\im(\phi^{\Delta})
. Note that
\im(\phi^{\Delta})
is a subtorus of
G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, since
\mathfrak{Q}=(\mathfrak{Q}^{L})_{L}
and each
\mathfrak{Q}^{L,\Delta}=Q^{L}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is a torus (see §
2.2.8
). Similarly,
I_{\phi^{\ad},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is the centralizer of the subtorus
\im(\phi^{\ad,\Delta})
of
G^{\ad}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

Clearly
\im(\phi^{\ad,\Delta})
is the image of
\im(\phi^{\Delta})
under the natural map
G\to G^{\ad}
. It is a standard result (see for instance
[
Bor91
, p. 153, Cor. 2]
) that if
S
is a subtorus of
G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
with image
S^{\prime}
in
G^{\ad}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, then the natural map
Z_{G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}(S)\to Z_{G^{\ad}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}(S^{\prime})
is surjective with kernel
(Z_{G})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Since we already know that the natural map
I_{\phi}\to I_{\phi^{\ad}}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and that
Z_{G}
is contained in the kernel, this finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.1.11
.

The natural map
\mathcal{AM}(G)/{\approx}\to\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})/{\approx}
is a bijection.

Proof.

The surjectivity follows from Lemma
6.1.9
. We show injectivity. Let
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
be such that
\phi^{\ad}\approx\phi^{\prime,\ad}
. We need to show that
\phi\approx\phi^{\prime}
.

By assumption, there exists
\beta_{0}\in\Sha_{G^{\ad}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi^{\ad}})
such that
\phi^{\prime,\ad}
is conjugate to
(\phi^{\ad})^{\beta_{0}}
. By Lemma
6.1.10
, we have
I_{\phi^{\ad}}\cong I_{\phi}/Z_{G}
. By this fact and by Corollary
1.2.9
(applied to
I=I_{\phi}
and
Z=Z_{G}
, where
I
indeed has the same absolute rank as
G
), the natural map

\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})\to\Sha_{G^{\ad}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi^{\ad}})
is surjective. Thus we can find
\beta\in\Sha_{G}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},I_{\phi})
lifting
\beta_{0}
. Then
(\phi^{\beta})^{\ad}
is conjugate to
\phi^{\prime,\ad}
. (Here
\phi^{\beta}
is only well defined up to conjugacy, but the ambiguity does not affect the conjugacy class of
(\phi^{\beta})^{\ad}
.) By the transitivity in Lemma
6.1.11
, we have
\phi^{\beta}\approx\phi^{\prime}
. But
\phi\approx\phi^{\beta}
, so
\phi\approx\phi^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 6.1.12
.

We have a natural surjection
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0}
.

Proof.

We can identify
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}
with the group of global sections of the sheaf
{\mathcal{H}}/{\approx}
on
\mathcal{AM}(G)/{\approx}
, and identify
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0}
with the group of global sections of the sheaf
{\mathcal{H}}^{\ad}/{\approx}
on
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})/{\approx}
. By Lemma
6.1.11
, we identify the spaces
\mathcal{AM}(G)/{\approx}
and
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})/{\approx}
. It follows from Lemma
6.1.10
that we have a natural surjection
{\mathcal{H}}/{\approx}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{\ad}/{\approx}
between sheaves on the identified space. The proposition follows.
∎

6.1.13
.

Consider two unramified Shimura data
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
and
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
together with an isomorphism

\iota:(G^{\ad},X^{\ad})(G_{2}^{\ad},X_{2}^{\ad})

between the adjoint Shimura data. Assume that
\iota:G^{\ad}G_{2}^{\ad}
lifts to a (unique) central isogeny
\widetilde{\iota}:G_{\mathrm{der}}\to G_{2,\mathrm{der}}
, and that
\iota_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\ad}G_{2,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\ad}
extends to an isomorphism
{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}{\mathcal{G}}_{2}^{\ad}
. We still use the symbols
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger},{\mathcal{H}}^{+},{\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{H}}
to denote the sheaves on
\mathcal{AM}(G)=\mathcal{AM}(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
as in §
6.1.3
. Their counterparts on
\mathcal{AM}(G_{2})=\mathcal{AM}(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
will be denoted with a subscript
2
. On
\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})=\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad},X^{\ad},p,{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad})
we define the sheaves
{\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger,\ad}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{+,\ad}
,
{\mathfrak{H}}^{\ad}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{\ad}
as in §
6.1.8
, and on
\mathcal{AM}(G_{2}^{\ad})=\mathcal{AM}(G_{2}^{\ad},X_{2}^{\ad},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2}^{\ad})
we have the counterparts denoted with a subscript
2
. Then
\iota
induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{AM}(\iota):\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})\mathcal{AM}(G_{2}^{\ad})

under which the four sheaves on one space are identified with the four on the other respectively.

Let
\mu^{\ab}\in X_{*}(G^{\ab})
denote the composite cocharacter

\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\mu}G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\to G^{\ab}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},

where
\mu
is any element of
\bbmu_{X}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. Clearly
\mu^{\ab}
is independent of the choice of
\mu
.

Let
\underline{\tau}^{+}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})_{0}
, and let
\underline{\tau}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}
be the image of
\underline{\tau}^{+}
. By Proposition
6.1.12
, we have surjections
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0}
and
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0}
. Let
\underline{\tau}_{0}
be the image of
\underline{\tau}
in
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0}
.

Proposition 6.1.14
.

Keep the setting of §
6.1.13
. Assume that
\underline{\tau}^{+}
is tori-rational, and assume that
X_{*}(G^{\ab})
is generated by
\mu^{\ab}
as a
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-module. Then there exists a tori-rational element
\underline{\tau}_{2}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{0}
mapping to
\underline{\tau}_{0}
under the composite map

(6.1.14.1)

\displaystyle\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2}^{\ad})_{0}\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0},

where the last isomorphism is induced by
\mathcal{AM}(\iota)^{-1}
.

Proof.

Consider
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
and
\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{AM}(G_{2})
such that
\mathcal{AM}(\iota)
sends
\phi^{\ad}\in\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
to
\phi_{2}^{\ad}\in\mathcal{AM}(G_{2}^{\ad})
. By Lemma
6.1.10
,
\iota
induces a
\mathbb{Q}
-isomorphism
I_{\phi}^{\ad}I_{\phi_{2}}^{\ad}
. Clearly this isomorphism lifts to a unique central isogeny
I_{\phi}^{\dagger}\to I_{\phi_{2}}^{\dagger}
, and the latter is induced by the central isogeny
\widetilde{\iota}:G_{\mathrm{der}}\to G_{2,\mathrm{der}}
. In particular, we have a natural map
\mathcal{H}^{\dagger}(\phi)\to\mathcal{H}^{\dagger}_{2}(\phi_{2})
induced by
\iota
. By this observation, the same argument as the proof of Proposition
6.1.12
shows that there is a natural map
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}_{2})_{0}
induced by
\iota
.

Recall that the natural map
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})_{0}
is surjective. We fix an element
\underline{\tau}^{\dagger}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})_{0}
lifting
\underline{\tau}^{+}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})_{0}
. Let
\underline{\tau}_{2}^{\dagger}
be the image of
\underline{\tau}^{\dagger}
under the natural map
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\dagger}_{2})_{0}
in the above paragraph. By construction,
\underline{\tau}_{2}
is sent to
\underline{\tau}_{0}
under the map in the proposition. We are left to check that
\underline{\tau}_{2}
is tori-rational.

Let
\underline{\tau}_{2}^{+}
and
\underline{\tau}_{2}
be the images of
\underline{\tau}_{2}^{\dagger}
in
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+}_{2})_{0}
and
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{0}
respectively.
Let
\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{AM}(G_{2})
and let
T_{2}
be a maximal torus in
I_{\phi_{2}}
. We need to show that the image of
\underline{\tau}_{2}(\phi_{2})
in
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T_{2})/\Sha_{G_{2}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T_{2})
is trivial.

By Lemma
6.1.11
we find
\phi\in\mathcal{AM}(G)
such that
\mathcal{AM}(\iota)(\phi^{\ad})=\phi_{2}^{\ad}
. To simplify notation in the rest of the proof we treat
\iota
as the identity and omit it from the notation. Write
\phi_{0}
for
\phi^{\ad}
, which we identify with
\phi_{2}^{\ad}
. Let
T_{0}
be the image of
T_{2}
under
I_{\phi_{2}}\to I_{\phi_{0}}
, and let
T

be the preimage of
T_{0}
under
I_{\phi}\to I_{\phi_{0}}
. It follows from Lemma
6.1.10
that
T_{0}
(resp.
T
) is a maximal torus in
I_{\phi_{0}}
(resp.
I_{\phi}
).
As usual, set

\displaystyle T^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\ker(T\to G^{\ab})=T\cap I_{\phi}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle T_{2}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=\ker(T_{2}\to G_{2}^{\ab})=T_{2}\cap I_{\phi_{2}}^{\dagger}.

Then there is a natural map
u:T^{\dagger}\to T_{2}^{\dagger}
induced by the central isogeny
I_{\phi}^{\dagger}\to I_{\phi_{2}}^{\dagger}
discussed at the beginning of the proof.

Define the torus
U:=(T\times_{T_{0}}T_{2})^{0}
. Denote the natural maps
U\to T
and
U\to T_{2}
by
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
respectively. The inclusion map
T^{\dagger}\hookrightarrow T
and the composite map
T^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{u}T_{2}^{\dagger}\hookrightarrow T_{2}
together define a map
T^{\dagger}\to U
, which we denote by
\Delta
. Let
V
be the quotient torus
U/\Delta(T^{\dagger})
. Then we have a commutative diagram with exact rows in the category of tori over
\mathbb{Q}
:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{\ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{p}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{p}_{2}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{2}^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{2}^{\ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

Here
\bar{p}_{1}
and
\bar{p}_{2}
are induced by
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
respectively.

We claim that the image of the map
X_{*}(V)\to X_{*}(G^{\ab})
induced by
\bar{p}_{1}
contains
\mu^{\ab}
. To show the claim, let
\mu_{T}
be an arbitrary element of
\bbmu_{X}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
that factors through
T_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
(which is embedded into
G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
via
I_{\phi,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow G_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
). Similarly, pick
\mu_{T_{2}}\in\bbmu_{X_{2}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
that factors through
T_{2,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. The two cocharacters of
T_{0,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
induced by
\mu_{T}
and
\mu_{T_{2}}
respectively are conjugate by
G^{\ad}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
, and are hence in the same orbit under the Weyl group of
(G^{\ad}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},T_{0,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
(cf.
[
Kot84a
, Lem. (1.1.3)]
). Since the Weyl group of
(G^{\ad}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},T_{0,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
and that of
(G_{2,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},T_{2,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}})
are canonically isomorphic, we can replace
\mu_{T_{2}}
by a Weyl-conjugate and assume that
\mu_{T}
and
\mu_{T_{2}}
induce the same cocharacter of
T_{0,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. We then obtain from
\mu_{T}
and
\mu_{T_{2}}
an element of
X_{*}(U)
. The image of this element under the composite map
X_{*}(U)\to X_{*}(V)\to X_{*}(G^{\ab})
is
\mu_{T}
, by the upper right commutative square in (
6.1
). The claim is proved.

By the claim and by our assumption on
X_{*}(G^{\ab})
, we know that
X_{*}(V)\to X_{*}(G^{\ab})
is surjective.
It follows that the kernel of
\bar{p}_{1}:V\to G^{\ab}
is a torus, which we denote by
V^{\dagger}
. Now it is easy to see that the map
(\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2}):V\to G^{\ab}\times G_{2}^{\ab}
is an isogeny between tori over
\mathbb{Q}
. Since
G^{\ab}
and
G_{2}^{\ab}
are both unramified over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we deduce that
V
and
V^{\dagger}
are both unramified tori over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
denote the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-torus extending
V_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. The kernel of
\mathcal{V}\to{\mathcal{G}}^{\ab}
is a torus over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, namely the one extending the unramified
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-torus
V^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. By Lang’s theorem applied to that kernel (which is smooth over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and has connected fibers), we know that the map
\mathcal{V}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to{\mathcal{G}}^{\ab}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is surjective.

Using this surjectivity result and the commutative diagram (
6.1
), we see that the natural map

\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T^{\dagger})/\Sha^{\infty}_{G_{\mathrm{der}}}(\mathbb{Q},T^{\dagger})\longrightarrow\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T_{2}^{\dagger})/\Sha^{\infty}_{G_{2,\mathrm{der}}}(\mathbb{Q},T_{2}^{\dagger})

induced by
u:T^{\dagger}\to T_{2}^{\dagger}
descends to a map

\cok\bigg({\mathcal{G}}^{\ab}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T^{\dagger})/\Sha_{G_{\mathrm{der}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T^{\dagger})\bigg)\longrightarrow\\
\cok\bigg({\mathcal{G}}_{2}^{\ab}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f},T_{2}^{\dagger})/\Sha_{G_{2,\mathrm{der}}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{Q},T_{2}^{\dagger})\bigg).

(The point is that the first cokernel does not change if
{\mathcal{G}}^{\ab}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is replaced by
\mathcal{V}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.) From this, we see that tori-rationality of
\underline{\tau}^{+}
(which is our assumption) implies tori-rationality of
\underline{\tau}_{2}^{+}
. Finally, we apply Lemma
6.1.7
to deduce tori-rationality of
\underline{\tau}_{2}
from tori-rationality of
\underline{\tau}_{2}^{+}
.
∎

Remark 6.1.15
.

The significance of Proposition
6.1.14
is that it allows the propagation of the tori-rational condition between the unramified Shimura data
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
and
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
, at least when
X_{*}(G^{\ab})
satisfies the technical condition. In §
6.3
below we shall apply the lemma to an arbitrary
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
of abelian type and an auxiliary
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
of Hodge type.

6.2.
Reformulation of results from
[
Kis17
]

Throughout this subsection, we fix a prime
p
, and fix an unramified Shimura datum
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
where
(G_{2},X_{2})
is of abelian type. For every Shimura datum
(G,X)
, we have an embedding of fields
E(G,X)\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
as in §
2.4.1
. We denote the completion of
E(G,X)
with respect to this embedding by
E(G,X)_{p}
. (In §
2.4.1
this was denoted by
E(G,X)_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.)

Definition 6.2.1
.

By a
nice lifting
of
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
, we mean an unramified Shimura datum of the form
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
together with an isomorphism of Shimura data
\iota:(G^{\ad},X^{\ad})(G_{2}^{\ad},X_{2}^{\ad})
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

(G,X)
is of Hodge type.

(ii)

We have
E(G,X)_{p}=E(G^{\ad},X^{\ad})_{p}
.

(iii)

\iota:G^{\ad}G_{2}^{\ad}
lifts to a (unique) central isogeny
\widetilde{\iota}:G_{\mathrm{der}}\to G_{2,\mathrm{der}}
.

(iv)

\iota_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\ad}G_{2,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\ad}
extends to an isomorphism
{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}{\mathcal{G}}_{2}^{\ad}

Lemma 6.2.2
.

A nice lifting exists.

Proof.

By
[
Kis17
, Lem. 4.6.6]
40
40

40

In
[
Kis17
, Lem. 4.6.6]
it is used that every totally real field
F
admits a totally imaginary quadratic extension
K/F
such that every prime of
F
over
p
splits in
K
. This fact is an immediate consequence of Thm. 5 or Thm. 6 in
[
AT09
, §X.2]
, regardless of the parity of
p
.
, there exist a Shimura datum
(G,X)
and an isomorphism
\iota:(G^{\ad},X^{\ad})(G_{2}^{\ad},X_{2}^{\ad})
satisfying conditions (i), (ii), (iii) in Definition
6.2.1
. The fact that we can extend
(G,X)
to an unramified Shimura datum
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
satisfying condition (iv) is shown in the proof of
[
Kis10
, Cor. 3.4.14]
. (The assumption that
p>2
in
loc. cit.
is not used for the construction of
{\mathcal{G}}
.)
∎

6.2.3
.

Fix a nice lifting
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}},\iota)
of
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
. We apply the notation in §
6.1.13
to our current
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
and
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
. Fix a bijection
\bij
as in Theorem
5.13.9
, and define the corresponding element
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}
as in Definition
5.13.10
and Corollary
5.13.11
. Let
\underline{\tau}_{2}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{0}
be an arbitrary element whose image in
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0}
under (
6.1.14.1
) is equal to that of
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}
.

Theorem 6.2.4
.

In the setting of §
6.2.3
, the statement
\mathsf{LR}(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2},\underline{\tau}_{2})
(see §
2.7.1
) holds.

Proof.

The existence of a canonical smooth integral model having well-behaved
\coh^{*}_{c}
follows from Theorem
2.5.3
and Theorem
2.5.7
. Hence the question is only about the bijection in the statement
\mathsf{LR}(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2},\underline{\tau}_{2})
. We first explain why this bijection is essentially proved in
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.6.2, Cor. 4.6.5, Thm. 4.6.7]
, at least when
Z_{G}
is a torus and
p>2
. We then explain how to remove the last two assumptions.
41
41

41

For the purpose of
[
Kis17
]
, the assumption that
Z_{G}
is a torus is harmless. This is because by
[
Kis17
, Lem. 4.6.6]
, for the fixed
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
one can always choose a nice lifting
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}},\iota)
such that
Z_{G}
is a torus. However, in the current paper we will need to consider choices of
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
which do not necessarily satisfy this condition. See Remark
6.3.4
below.

For the first part, there are two points that deserve clarification. The first point is that by Proposition
5.10.4
, the elements
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}(\phi_{0})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\ad}(\phi_{0})
, for
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{AM}(G^{\ad})
, are indeed the same as the elements denoted by
\tau
in
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.6.2]
(except that in
loc. cit.
\tau
is viewed as in
I_{\phi_{0}}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, instead of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\ad}(\phi_{0})=I_{\phi_{0}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\backslash I_{\phi_{0}}^{\ad}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/I_{\phi_{0}}^{\ad}(\mathbb{Q})
). The second point is that in
[
Kis17
, §4.6]
the following property of the bijection
\bij
is assumed
(where
{\mathbb J}
is defined as in §
5.13.1
, with respect to
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
):

(*)

For every
\mathscr{J}\in{\mathbb J}
, there exists
{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
such that
\mathscr{J}=\mathscr{J}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}
and
\bij(\mathscr{J})=\mathscr{I}_{{\mathfrak{s}}}
.

This condition is indeed satisfied by the bijection
\bij^{\prime}:{\mathbb J}{\mathbb I}
that is implicitly used in
[
Kis17
]
42
42

42

In
[
Kis17
]
this bijection is not explicitly defined, but it is any bijection as in Remark
5.13.8
. That such a bijection satisfies condition (*) is shown in the proof of
[
Kis17
, Cor. 4.6.5]
.
, but it may not be satisfied by
\bij
in our current discussion. Nevertheless, it is clear from the proof of
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.6.2]
that the hypothesis in that proposition can be weakened as follows: Instead of requiring
\mathscr{J}
and
\mathscr{I}
to be associated with one same
{\mathfrak{s}}\in\mathcal{SPD}(G,X)
, we only require that
(\mathscr{I},\mathscr{J})
is amicable. From this variant of
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.6.2]
, the conclusion of
[
Kis17
, Cor. 4.6.5]
easily follows. More specifically, in the proof of
[
Kis17
, Cor. 4.6.5]
, instead of applying
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.6.2]
to all pairs
(\mathscr{J},\mathscr{I})
with
\mathscr{J}=\mathscr{J}_{T,i^{\beta},h},\mathscr{I}=\mathscr{I}_{T,i^{\beta},h}
, we apply the above-mentioned variant of
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.6.2]
to all pairs
(\mathscr{J},\mathscr{I})
with arbitrary
\mathscr{J}\in{\mathbb J}
and
\mathscr{I}=\bij(\mathscr{J})
. This is valid because
(\mathscr{J},\bij(\mathscr{J}))
is indeed amicable by Theorem
5.13.9
.

We now explain how to remove the assumption that
Z_{G}
is a torus, which is made in
[
Kis17
, §4.6]
. This assumption comes from
[
Kis17
, Prop. 4.4.17]
. As is explained in the proof of that proposition (see especially footnote 24), this assumption can be removed once we know that
[
Kis17
, Lem. 1.2.18]
can be generalized to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-group schemes of the form
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=\Res_{\mathcal{O}_{F}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathcal{G}}
, where
F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is an arbitrary finite extension and
{\mathcal{G}}
is a reductive group scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. (The result
[
Kis17
, Lem. 1.2.18]
is only proved for reductive group schemes over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, but
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is not reductive unless
F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is unramified.) This desired generalization is provided by Corollary
4.4.16
.

Finally, we explain why the assumption
p>2
in
[
Kis17
]
is no longer needed. In fact, there are two reasons why this assumption is made in
[
Kis10
]
and
[
Kis17
]
. Firstly, this assumption is made in
[
Kis10
, Lem. 2.3.1]
. That this is unnecessary is explained in the proof of
[
KMP16
, Lem. 4.7]
. (We already mentioned this in §
5.1.1
.) Secondly, the assumption
p>2
is needed for the integral comparison isomorphism (
5.2.2.1
), which is key to both the papers
[
Kis10
]
and
[
Kis17
]
. We have already explained in §
5.2.2
why
p>2
is no longer needed for the integral comparison isomorphism.
∎

6.3.
Proof of the Langlands–Rapoport–
\tau
Conjecture

6.3.1
.

In the following, by a
CM field
we mean a totally imaginary quadratic extension of a totally real field contained in our fixed
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. We denote by
\iota
the complex conjugation in
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
, defined with respect to our fixed embedding
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. For any
\mathbb{Q}
-torus
T
and
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
, we write
E_{\mu}
for the field of definition of
\mu
inside
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Following
[
Del82
, §A, (a)]
, we consider the category
I
of pairs
(T,\mu)
, where
T
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-torus and
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
, satisfying that
w:=\mu+\iota(\mu)
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and that
(T/w(\mathbb{G}_{m}))_{\mathbb{R}}
is anisotropic. By definition, a morphism
(T,\mu)\to(T^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
in
I
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-homomorphism
T\to T^{\prime}
taking
\mu
to
\mu^{\prime}
. For each
(T,\mu)
in
I
, we know that
T
is a cuspidal torus since it satisfies condition (ii) in Lemma
1.5.5
. By condition (vii) in that lemma,
T
splits over a CM or totally real field, so
E_{\mu}
is either CM or totally real. In the latter case we have
E_{\mu}=\mathbb{Q}
, since
\mu+\iota(\mu)
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and is equal to
2\mu
.

As in
[
Del82
, §A, (a)]
, for every
(T,\mu)
in
I
, there exists an object in
I
of the form
(S^{L},\mu^{L})
which maps to
(T,\mu)
. Here
L
is a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
L_{0}
, and
S^{L}
is the
\mathbb{Q}
-torus
43
43

43

In
[
Del82
, §A, (a)]
, the CM field is denoted by
K
and our
S^{L}
is denoted by
{\vphantom{K}}{}^{K}S
. We have avoided the usage of
K
, and avoided the notation
{\vphantom{L}}{}^{L}S
as this conflicts with the L-group notation.

\Res_{L/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}/\ker(\N_{L_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}:\Res_{L_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}).

The cocharacter
\mu^{L}\in X_{*}(S^{L})
is the one induced by the cocharacter of
\Res_{L/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
corresponding to the canonical embedding
L\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. In fact, as discussed above,
E_{\mu}
is either a CM field or
\mathbb{Q}
. We can take
L
to any CM field containing
E_{\mu}
, and take the homomorphism
S^{L}\to T
to be the one induced by the composite homomorphism

\Res_{L/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\Res_{L/\mathbb{Q}}\mu}\Res_{L/\mathbb{Q}}T\xrightarrow{\N_{L/\mathbb{Q}}}T.

The fact that the above homomorphism factors through
S^{L}
follows easily from the fact that
(T,\mu)
satisfies the defining conditions for objects in
I
.

Lemma 6.3.2
.

Keep the setting and notation of §
6.2
. There exists nice lifting
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}},\iota)
of
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
such that
X_{*}(G^{\ab})
is generated by
\mu^{\ab}
as a
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-module. Here
\mu^{\ab}
is as in §
6.1.13
.

Remark 6.3.3
.

The purpose of this lemma is to ensure that the technical assumption on
X_{*}(G^{\ab})
in Proposition
6.1.14
can be met, so that we can apply that proposition to propagate the tori-rational condition.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.2.2
we can find a nice lifting
(G_{1},X_{1},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{1},\iota_{1})
of
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
. Let
\mu_{1}^{\ab}\in X_{*}(G_{1}^{\ab})
denote the composite cocharacter
\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\mu_{1}}G_{1,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\to G_{1,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}^{\ab}
where
\mu_{1}\in\bbmu_{X_{1}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
.
Since
(G_{1},X_{1})
is of Hodge type, as in the proof of Lemma
5.1.2
we know that the weight cocharacter
w_{1}
of
X_{1}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, and that
(Z_{G_{1}}^{0}/w_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m}))_{\mathbb{R}}
is anisotropic. Since
G_{1}^{\ab}
is isogenous to
Z_{G_{1}}^{0}
and since
\mu_{1}^{\ab}+\iota(\mu_{1}^{\ab})\in X_{*}(G_{1}^{\ab})
is induced by
w_{1}\in X_{*}(Z_{G_{1}}^{0})
, we know that the pair
(G_{1}^{\ab},\mu_{1}^{\ab})
is in the category
I
in §
6.3.1
. As discussed in §
6.3.1
, the field
E_{\mu_{1}^{\ab}}
is either CM or
\mathbb{Q}
. In the former case, we let
L
be
E_{\mu_{1}^{\ab}}
. In the latter case, we let
L
be an arbitrary imaginary quadratic field in which
p
splits. We construct the morphism
(S^{L},\mu^{L})\to(G_{1}^{\ab},\mu_{1}^{\ab})
in the category
I
as in §
6.3.1
. Since we have an unramified Shimura datum
(G_{1},X_{1},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{1})
, the torus
G_{1}^{\ab}
is unramified over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Hence every conjugate of the subgroup
\Gamma_{p,0}\subset{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
fixes
\mu_{1}^{\ab}
. It follows that the Galois closure of
E_{\mu_{1}^{\ab}}/\mathbb{Q}
is unramified over
p
, and so is the Galois closure of
L/\mathbb{Q}
. Hence the torus
S^{L}
is unramified over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
since it splits over this Galois closure of
L/\mathbb{Q}
.

We now view
(S^{L},\mu^{L})
as a Shimura datum
(S^{L},\left\{h^{L}\right\})
. (Recall that to specify a Shimura datum for a given torus over
\mathbb{Q}
is the same as to specify the Hodge cocharacter, which can be an arbitrary cocharacter over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.) By the paragraph preceding
[
Del82
, §A, (a)]
and by
[
Del82
, Lem. A.2]
, we know that
S^{L}
admits a faithful representation over
\mathbb{Q}
such that the Hodge structure on that representation determined by
h^{L}
is of type
\left\{(-1,0),(0,-1)\right\}
. By
[
Del79
, Prop. 2.3.2]
, the last fact implies that
(S^{L},\left\{h^{L}\right\})
is a Shimura datum of Hodge type.

Let
G=G_{1}\times_{G_{1}^{\ab}}S^{L}
. Fix an element
h_{1}\in X_{1}
. Note that
h_{1}
and
h^{L}
induce the same map
\mathbb S\to G_{1,\mathbb{R}}^{\ab}
. Hence we obtain a homomorphism
h=(h_{1},{\vphantom{L}}^{L}h):\mathbb S\to G_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
X
be the
G(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of
h
. It is clear that
X
is a Shimura datum for
G
.
44
44

44

Here
X
may depend on the choice of
h_{1}
, but it does not matter for our proof.
Since
(G_{1},X_{1})
and
(S^{L},\left\{h^{L}\right\})
are both of Hodge type, we know that
(G,X)
is of Hodge type (by taking the direct sum of the faithful symplectic representations of the two factors). There is a canonical identification
\iota:G^{\ad}\cong G_{1}^{\ad}
determined by
\iota_{1}
, under which
X^{\ad}
is identified with
X_{1}^{\ad}
. Then
(G,X)
and
\iota
satisfy conditions (i) and (iii) in Definition
6.2.1
. Note that
E_{\mu^{L}}
is contained in
L
, and the completion of
L
with respect to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
is equal to the completion of
E_{\mu_{1}^{\ab}}
with respect to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
, which is a subfield of
E(G_{1},X_{1})_{p}
. It follows that
E(G,X)_{p}=E(G_{1},X_{1})_{p}
. Since
(G_{1},X_{1})
satisfies condition (ii) in Definition
6.2.1
, so does
(G,X)
. Since
G_{1}
and
S^{L}
are both unramified over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, so is
G
. Thus as in the proof of
[
Kis10
, Cor. 3.4.14]
we can extend
(G,X)
to an unramified Shimura datum
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
satisfying condition (iv) in Definition
6.2.1
.

We have thus produced a nice lifting
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}},\iota)
of
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
. It remains to show that
X_{*}(G^{\ab})
is generated by
\mu^{\ab}
as a
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-module. Now
G^{\ab}
is canonically identified with
S^{L}
, and
\mu^{\ab}
is identified with
\mu^{L}
. It is clear from the definition that
X_{*}(S^{L})
is generated by
\mu^{L}
as a
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-module.
∎

Remark 6.3.4
.

In the proof of Lemma
6.2.2
, even if
Z_{G_{1}}
is a torus, it can happen that
Z_{G}
is not a torus. This is why in Theorem
6.2.4
we needed to remove the assumption that
Z_{G}
is a torus which is made in
[
Kis17
, §4.6]
.

Theorem 6.3.5
.

Let
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
be an unramified Shimura datum such that
(G_{2},X_{2})
is of abelian type. Then Conjecture
2.7.3
holds for
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
.

Proof.

We choose a nice lifting
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}},\iota)
of
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
as in Lemma
6.3.2
. We apply the notation in §
6.1.13
to our current
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
and
(G_{2},X_{2},p,{\mathcal{G}}_{2})
. Fix a bijection
\bij
as in Theorem
5.13.9
with respect to
(G,X,p,{\mathcal{G}})
, and define the corresponding element
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}
as in Definition
5.13.10
and Corollary
5.13.11
. In view of Theorem
6.2.4
, we only need to show that there exists a tori-rational element
\underline{\tau}_{2}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{0}
whose image in
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{\ad})_{0}
under (
6.1.14.1
) is equal to that of
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}
.

It is clear that the assignment

\mathcal{AM}(G)\ni\phi\longmapsto\bigg(\tau^{{\mathfrak{H}}}(\bij(\mathscr{J}),\mathscr{J}),\tau^{{\mathcal{H}}}(\bij(\mathscr{J}),\mathscr{J})\bigg)\in{\mathfrak{H}}(\phi)\oplus{\mathcal{H}}(\phi)

as in §
5.13.10
, where
\mathscr{J}
is the conjugacy class of
\phi
, defines an element
\underline{\tau}^{+}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})
. By the conditions satisfied by
\bij
in Theorem
5.13.9
and by Theorem
5.12.5
, we know that
\underline{\tau}^{+}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})_{0}
. Also, by Theorem
5.12.2
, we know that
\underline{\tau}^{+}
is tori-rational.

It is clear that
\underline{\tau}^{+}
maps to
\underline{\tau}_{\bij}
under the natural map
\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}^{+})_{0}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{H}})_{0}
. Since
\underline{\tau}^{+}
is tori-rational and since
X_{*}(G^{\ab})
is generated by
\mu^{\ab}
as a
{\rm Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-module, the existence of the desired tori-rational
\underline{\tau}_{2}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{0}
follows from Proposition
6.1.14
.
∎

Theorem 6.3.6

(cf. Theorem
2
in the Introduction)
.

Conjecture
1.8.8
holds for all Shimura varieties of abelian type.

Proof.

This follows from Theorems
2.7.4
and
6.3.5
.
∎

Part III
Stabilization

7.
Preliminaries for stabilization

7.1.
Central character data and the trace formula

7.1.1
.

To stabilize the point counting formula for Shimura varieties (
1.8.8.1
) in general, it is necessary to work with fixed central characters.
To this end, we are going to introduce the formalism of central character data following
[
Art13
, Ch. 3.1]
.
The point counting formula can be understood through the lens of a particular central character datum, but it is useful to allow flexible central character data to accommodate
z
-extensions during the stabilization process.

The rest of §
7.1
is devoted to discussing the invariant trace formula with fixed central character. Though this is not logically needed for the stabilization in §
8
, it puts central character data into context, motivates the definition of stable distributions with fixed central characters, and also has an application in §
9
below.

Throughout §
7.1
, let
G
be a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}
with center
Z
. Write
A_{Z}
for the maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split torus in
Z
and set
A_{Z,\infty}:=A_{Z}(\mathbb{R})^{0}
.

Definition 7.1.2
.

A
central character datum
for
G
is a pair
({\mathfrak{X}},\chi)
, where
{\mathfrak{X}}
is a closed subgroup of
Z(\mathbb{A})
containing
A_{Z,\infty}
such that
Z(\mathbb{Q}){\mathfrak{X}}
is closed in
Z(\mathbb{A})
, with a choice of Haar measure on
{\mathfrak{X}}
(often implicit),
and
\chi:{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
a continuous character which is trivial on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\mathbb{Q}}:={\mathfrak{X}}\cap Z(\mathbb{Q})
.

Remark 7.1.3
.

The two extreme cases where
{\mathfrak{X}}=A_{Z,\infty}
or
{\mathfrak{X}}=Z(\mathbb{A})
are already interesting, but it is important to allow intermediate groups for our purpose.

7.1.4
.

Let
\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
denote the set of elliptic conjugacy classes in
G(\mathbb{Q})
. Given a central character datum
({\mathfrak{X}},\chi)
. Denote by
\Gamma_{{\mathfrak{X}},\mathrm{ell}}(G)
the set of
{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}
-orbits in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
with respect to the multiplication action.
Write
\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathfrak{X}}}(\gamma)
for the stabilizer subgroup of
{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}
fixing
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. It is not hard to see that
\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathfrak{X}}}(\gamma)
is finite, for instance by reducing to the case of a product of general linear groups via a (possibly reducible) faithful representation of
G
.

Fix a maximal compact subgroup
K_{\infty}\subset G(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
v
be a place of
\mathbb{Q}
, and
{\mathfrak{Z}}
a closed subgroup of
Z(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
.
For a continuous character
\omega:{\mathfrak{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
define
{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{v}),\omega^{-1})
to be the Hecke algebra of smooth functions on
G(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
which transform under
{\mathfrak{Z}}
by
\omega^{-1}
and have compact support modulo
{\mathfrak{Z}}
; we also require
K_{\infty}
-finiteness if
v=\infty
. Let
\pi
be an admissible representation of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
which has central character on
{\mathfrak{Z}}
equal to
\omega
. For
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{v}),\omega^{-1})
define

\displaystyle\pi(f)(u)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{G(\mathbb{Q}_{v})/{\mathfrak{Z}}}f(g)\pi(g)u\cdot dg,\quad u\in\pi.

The trace of the trace-class operator
\pi(f)
is denoted by
{\rm tr}\,(f|\pi)
or
{\rm tr}\,\pi(f)
. The orbital integrals for
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{v}),\omega^{-1})
are defined by the same formula as for
{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{v}))
, cf. §
1.8.2
.
These definitions obviously extend to the adelic setting.

7.1.5
.

We recall the invariant distributions

I_{{\rm geom},\chi_{0}},~I_{{\rm spec},\chi_{0}},~I_{\mathrm{ell},\chi_{0}},~I_{\disc,\chi_{0}},~T_{\mathrm{ell},\chi_{0}},~T_{\disc,\chi_{0}}

in the classical setup where the central character datum consists of
{\mathfrak{X}}=A_{Z,\infty}
and
\chi_{0}:A_{Z,\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
First off,
I_{{\rm geom},\chi_{0}}
and
I_{{\rm spec},\chi_{0}}
are Arthur’s invariant distributions given in sections 3 and 4 of
[
Art88
]
, respectively. Define
I_{\mathrm{ell},\chi_{0}}
to be the
M=G
part of formula (3.3) and
I_{\disc,\chi_{0}}
to be formula (4.4), both referenced to
loc. cit
. All the four distributions are distributions on
{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A}),\chi_{0}^{-1})
.
45
45

45

Arthur defines them as distributions on a certain space of functions on
G(\mathbb{A})^{1}
named
{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A})^{1})
, but this space is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A}),\chi_{0}^{-1})
via the product decomposition
G(\mathbb{A})=G(\mathbb{A})^{1}\times A_{Z,\infty}
. We do not mention
{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A})^{1})
again.

For
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
, write
I_{\gamma}
for the connected centralizer of
\gamma
in
G
.
We put

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{ell},\chi_{0}}(f)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}\iota(\gamma)^{-1}\mathrm{vol}(I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{A})/A_{Z,\infty})O_{\gamma}(f),

\displaystyle T_{\disc,\chi_{0}}(f)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\,(f\,|\,L^{2}_{\disc,\chi_{0}}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))),\quad\quad f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A}),\chi_{0}^{-1}).

In general
I_{\star}(f)
is more complicated than
T_{\star}(f)
for
\star\in\{\mathrm{ell},\disc\}
. Arthur’s invariant trace formula is the equality

(7.1.5.1)

I_{{\rm geom},\chi_{0}}=I_{{\rm spec},\chi_{0}}.

When
G/Z
is anisotropic over
\mathbb{Q}
,

(7.1.5.2)

T_{\mathrm{ell},\chi_{0}}=I_{\mathrm{ell},\chi_{0}}=I_{{\rm geom},\chi_{0}}=I_{{\rm spec},\chi_{0}}=I_{\disc,\chi_{0}}=T_{\disc,\chi_{0}}.

7.1.6
.

Next we introduce the trace formula with respect to a fixed character on a closed central subgroup. This must be well known to experts but for the lack of a convenient reference we state the formula here.
46
46

46

Chapter 3.1 of
[
Art13
]
discusses such a variant in the discrete part of the trace formula. Sections 2 and 3 of
[
Art02
]
present both the spectral and geometric expansions of the trace formula with fixed central character on an induced torus. We treat a more general case than
loc. cit.
but proceed in a similar spirit.

Let
({\mathfrak{X}},\chi)
be a central character datum for
G
. Suppose that
\chi_{0}:A_{Z,\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is the restriction of
\chi
. We will obtain the
\chi
-versions of the above invariant distributions by averaging.

Lemma 7.1.7
.

Let
D
be a multiplicative group over
\mathbb{Q}
,
A_{D}
its maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split subtorus, and
A_{D,\infty}:=A_{D}(\mathbb{R})^{0}
. Then
D(\mathbb{Q})\backslash D(\mathbb{A})/A_{D,\infty}
is compact.

Proof.

Replacing
D
by
D^{0}
, we may assume that
D
is a torus. Via a closed embedding, we reduce to the case where
D
is a finite product of tori of the form
\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
for a finite extension
F
over
\mathbb{Q}
. When
D=\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, the lemma is clear since
F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}^{1}
is compact, where
\mathbb{A}_{F}^{1}
denotes the group of ideles of norm
1
.
∎

Corollary 7.1.8
.

The quotient
{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}
is compact.

Proof.

This is clear since the inclusion
{\mathfrak{X}}\subset Z(\mathbb{A})
