induces a closed embedding
from
{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}
into
Z(\mathbb{Q})\backslash Z(\mathbb{A})/A_{Z,\infty}
. (The image is closed since
Z(\mathbb{Q}){\mathfrak{X}}
is closed in
Z(\mathbb{A})
.)
∎

7.1.9
.

There is a surjection
{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A}),\chi_{0}^{-1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A}),\chi^{-1})
sending
f
to the function

g\mapsto\overline{f}_{\chi}(g):=\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}f(gz)\chi(z)dz,

where the integral converges because
z\mapsto f(gz)
has compact support in
{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}
.
Given a function
f
on
G(\mathbb{A})
and
z\in Z(\mathbb{R})
, write
f_{z}
for the translated function
g\mapsto f(gz)
. For each
\star\in\{{\rm geom},{\rm spec},\mathrm{ell},\disc\}
define

(7.1.9.1)

I_{\star,\chi}(f):=\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty})}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}\chi(z)I_{\star,\chi_{0}}(f_{z})dz,\quad f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A}),\chi_{0}^{-1}),

as well as
T_{\mathrm{ell},\chi}
and
T_{\disc,\chi}
in a similar manner.
In the special case where
G/Z
is anisotropic over
\mathbb{Q}
, it is clear from (
7.1.5.2
) that

T_{\mathrm{ell},\chi}(f)=I_{\mathrm{ell},\chi}(f)=I_{{\rm geom},\chi}(f)=I_{{\rm spec},\chi}(f)=I_{\disc,\chi}(f)=T_{\disc,\chi}(f).

Write

L^{2}_{\disc,\chi}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))

for the discrete spectrum in the
L^{2}
-space of complex-valued functions
\phi
on
G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
such that

•

\phi(gz)=\chi(z)\phi(g)
for every
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
and every
z\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,

•

\int_{G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})^{1}/{\mathfrak{X}}\cap G(\mathbb{A})^{1}}|\phi(g)|^{2}dg<\infty
(for any Haar measure), where
G(\mathbb{A})^{1}
denote the “norm one” subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A})
as
defined in
[
Art78
, p. 917]
.

Proposition 7.1.10
.

For
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{A}),\chi_{0}^{-1})
the following equalities hold.

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{ell},\chi}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell},{\mathfrak{X}}}(G)}|\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathfrak{X}}}(\gamma)|^{-1}\iota(\gamma)^{-1}\mathrm{vol}(I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{A})/{\mathfrak{X}})O_{\gamma}(\overline{f}_{\chi}),

\displaystyle T_{\disc,\chi}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\,(\overline{f}_{\chi}\,|\,L^{2}_{\disc,\chi}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}))).

Proof.

We compute
T_{\mathrm{ell},\chi}(f)
as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}\chi(z)\iota(\gamma)^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{A})/A_{Z,\infty})}{\mathrm{vol}}O_{\gamma z}(f)dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}\chi(z)\iota(\gamma)^{-1}\mathrm{vol}(I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{A})/{\mathfrak{X}})O_{\gamma z}(f)dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell},{\mathfrak{X}}}(G)}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{A})/{\mathfrak{X}})}{|\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathfrak{X}}}(\gamma)|\iota(\gamma)}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}\chi(z)O_{\gamma z}(f)dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{ell},{\mathfrak{X}}}(G)}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash I_{\gamma}(\mathbb{A})/{\mathfrak{X}})}{|\mathrm{Stab}_{{\mathfrak{X}}}(\gamma)|\iota(\gamma)}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}O_{\gamma}(\overline{f}_{\chi}).

The equality for
T_{\disc,\chi}(f)
follows from the following two observations. First, given an admissible representation
\pi
of
G(\mathbb{A})
with central character
\chi_{\pi}
on
{\mathfrak{X}}
,

\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}\chi(z)\pi(f_{z})dz=\pi(f)\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}\chi(z)\chi_{\pi}^{-1}(z)dz,

which equals 0 if
\chi\neq\chi_{\pi}
and
\mathrm{vol}({\mathfrak{X}}{Q}\backslash{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty})\pi(f)
if
\chi=\chi_{\pi}
. Second, if
\chi=\chi_{\pi}
,

\displaystyle\pi(\overline{f}_{\chi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G(\mathbb{A})/{\mathfrak{X}}}\pi(g)\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}f(gz)\chi(z)dzdg

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G(\mathbb{A})/{\mathfrak{X}}}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}/A_{Z,\infty}}\pi(gz)f(gz)dzdg=\pi(f).

∎

7.1.11
.

We record a simplification of the trace formula with fixed central character when the test function is a stable cuspidal function at the real place.

Given a central character datum
({\mathfrak{X}},\chi)
, suppose that
{\mathfrak{X}}={\mathfrak{X}}^{\infty}\times{\mathfrak{X}}_{\infty}
with
{\mathfrak{X}}^{\infty}\subset Z(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\infty}\subset Z(\mathbb{R})
. (In particular
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\infty}
contains
A_{Z,\infty}
.) Accordingly we decompose
\chi=\chi^{\infty}\chi_{\infty}
. We also assume that
G(\mathbb{R})
contains a maximal torus which is compact modulo
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\infty}
.

Let
\xi
be an irreducible algebraic representation of
G{C}
. The inverse of the central character of
\xi
on
Z(\mathbb{R})
is denoted by
\chi_{\xi}
. Let
\Pi_{2}(\xi)
denote the set of isomorphism classes of (irreducible) discrete series representations of
G(\mathbb{R})
whose central and infinitesimal characters are the same as those of the contragredient of
\xi
. Define
f_{\xi}\in{\mathcal{H}}(G(\mathbb{R}),\chi^{-1}_{\xi})
to be the sum of pseudocoefficients of
\pi_{\infty}
as
\pi_{\infty}
runs over
\Pi_{2}(\xi)
, cf.
[
Art89
, Lem. 3.1]
,
[
CD90
]
.

A
stable cuspidal function
on
G(\mathbb{R})
(relative to
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\infty}
) is defined to be
f_{\infty}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{R}),\chi_{\infty}^{-1})
such that for every irreducible tempered representation
\pi_{\infty}
of
G(\mathbb{R})
whose central character restricts to
\chi_{\infty}
on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\infty}
,
we have (i)
{\rm tr}\,\pi_{\infty}(f_{\infty})=0
unless
\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete series representation, and (ii)
{\rm tr}\,\pi_{\infty}(f_{\infty})
has a constant value as
\pi_{\infty}
runs over each discrete series
L
-packet, cf.
[
Art89
, §4, p. 266]
.
An example is
f_{\xi}
in the last paragraph. In general, a stable cuspidal function is a finite linear combination of character twists of functions of the form
f_{\xi}
(for different
\xi
’s), up to a function whose orbital integrals are identically zero.

Proposition 7.1.12
.

If
f_{\infty}
is a stable cuspidal function then

I_{{\rm spec},\chi}(f)=I_{\disc,\chi}(f)=T_{\disc,\chi}(f).

Proof.

This is proved in
[
Art89
, §3]
when
{\mathfrak{X}}_{\infty}=A_{Z,\infty}
. The same proof extends.
∎

Remark 7.1.13
.

When
f_{\infty}
is stable cuspidal,
a simple expansion for
I_{{\rm geom},\chi_{0}}(f)
is obtained in
[
Art89
, Thm. 6.1]
.
A similar expansion for
I_{{\rm geom},\chi}(f)
is given in
[
Dal19
, 6.4, 6.5]
for more general central character data.

7.2.
Endoscopic data and
z
-extensions

7.2.1
.

From here throughout §
7
, let
G
be a connected reductive group over a local or global field
F
of characteristic zero.

Langlands–Shelstad
[
LS87
, §1.2]
and Kottwitz–Shelstad
[
KS99
, §2.1]
have defined endoscopic data and related notions in the untwisted and twisted settings. Here we recall the untwisted case as well as a specific kind of local twisted endoscopy (generalizing the unramified base change) as studied in
[
Mor10
, App. A]
.

Definition 7.2.2
.

Let
F,G
be as above. An
endoscopic datum
for
G
is a quadruple
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)
, where

•

H
is a quasi-split reductive group over
F
,

•

{\mathcal{H}}
is a split extension of
W_{F}
by
\widehat{H}
such that the
L
-action of
W_{F}
on
\widehat{H}
determined by
{\mathcal{H}}
coincides with the
L
-action of the
L
-group
{}^{L}H
,

•

s
is a semi-simple element of
\widehat{G}
,

•

\eta:{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow{}^{L}G
is an
L
-morphism inducing an isomorphism
\widehat{H}\cong\mathrm{Cent}(s,\widehat{G})^{0}
(via
\eta
we view
s
also as an element of
\widehat{H}
),

such that
\Int(s)\circ\eta=a\cdot\eta
for a 1-cocycle
a:W_{F}\rightarrow Z(\widehat{G})
which is trivial (resp. locally trivial) if
F
is local (resp. global). In this case
H
is said to be an
endoscopic group
for
G
.

The datum
{\mathfrak{e}}
is said to be
elliptic
if
\eta(Z(\widehat{H})^{\Gamma})^{0}\subset Z(\widehat{G})
. When
F
is non-archimedean, we say that
{\mathfrak{e}}
is
unramified
if
H
and
G
are unramified groups over
F
and if
\eta
is inflated from an
L
-morphism with respect to the Weil group of
F^{{\mathrm{ur}}}
over
F
. An
isomorphism
from
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)
to
{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}=(H^{\prime},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime},s^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
is an element
g\in\widehat{G}
such that
g\eta({\mathcal{H}})g^{-1}=\eta^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})
in
{}^{L}G
and
gsg^{-1}=s^{\prime}
in
\widehat{G}/Z(\widehat{G})
.

7.2.3
.

Automorphisms of an endoscopic datum
{\mathfrak{e}}
induce outer automorphisms of
H
as in
[
KS99
, (2.1.8)]
. By
{\rm Out}_{F}({\mathfrak{e}})
we mean the image subgroup of the outer automorphism group
{\rm Out}_{F}(H):=\Aut_{F}(H)/H_{\textrm{ad}}(F)
. Set

\lambda({\mathfrak{e}}):=|{\rm Out}_{F}({\mathfrak{e}})|\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

The set of endoscopic data is denoted by
E(G)
. Write
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
for the subset of elliptic endoscopic data. Write
{\mathcal{E}}(G)
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
for the corresponding sets of isomorphism classes.

For
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)\in E(G)
, there exists a canonical injection over
F

(7.2.3.1)

Z_{G}\hookrightarrow Z_{H}

as we now explain. Since an inner twisting induces a canonical isomorphism of centers, we may assume that
G
is quasi-split over
F
. Choose a maximal torus
T_{H}
of
H
over
F
. Then there exist a maximal torus
T
of
G
and an isomorphism
T_{H}\cong T
, both defined over
F

[
LS87
, p. 226]
such that the composite embedding
T\cong T_{H}\subset H
is canonical up to
H(\overline{F})
-conjugacy. Restricting from
T_{H}\cong T
, we get the desired map
Z_{G}\hookrightarrow Z_{H}
, which is independent of the choices involved.

7.2.4
.

If
G_{\mathrm{der}}=G_{\textup{sc}}
then by
[
Lan79c
, Prop. 1]
, every
{\mathfrak{e}}\in{\mathcal{E}}(G)
is represented by
(H,{}^{L}H,s,\eta)
, that is, we can take
{\mathcal{H}}={}^{L}H
. In general there is no guarantee that this is possible, so we use
z
-extensions to reduce to this case.

A
z
-
extension
of
G
over
F
is defined to be a connected reductive group
G_{1}
equipped with a short exact sequence

(7.2.4.1)

1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow G_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1

such that
G_{1,\mathrm{der}}=G_{1,\mathrm{sc}}
,
Z_{1}\subset Z_{G_{1}}
, and
Z_{1}\cong\prod_{i\in I}\Res_{F_{i}/F}\mathbb{G}_{m}
for finite extensions
F_{i}
of
F
over a finite index set
I
. We also call such a short exact sequence itself a
z
-extension of
G
.

Lemma 7.2.5
.

If
F
is non-archimedean and if
G
is unramified, then there exists a
z
-extension
G_{1}
of
G
that is unramified.
Similarly, if
F
is a number field and if
G
is unramified at a finite set of finite places
S
, then there exists a
z
-extension
G_{1}
of
G
that is unramified at
S
.

Proof.

The lemma follows from
[
MS82
, Prop. 3.1]
, possibly except the point, pertaining to the global case, that there exists a maximal torus
T
of
G
such that
T
splits over an extension of
F
unramified at
S
. Let us verify it.

Since
G
is unramified over
F_{v}
for each
v\in S
, so is
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
. Thus there exists an unramified maximal torus
T_{\mathrm{sc},v}
in
G_{\mathrm{sc},F_{v}}
. Write
T_{\mathrm{sc},v}(F_{v})_{\textup{rs}}\subset T_{\mathrm{sc},v}(F_{v})
for the open subset consisting of regular semi-simple elements.
Then the non-empty subset

Y_{v}:=\bigcup_{g_{v}\in G_{\mathrm{sc}}(F_{v})}g_{v}\cdot T_{\mathrm{sc},v}(F_{v})_{\textup{rs}}\cdot g_{v}^{-1}\subset G_{\mathrm{sc}}(F_{v})

is open in
G_{\mathrm{sc}}(F_{v})
by Harish-Chandra’s submersion principle
[
HC80
]
.
By weak approximation for
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
, there exists an element
\gamma_{0}\in G_{\mathrm{sc}}(F)\cap(\prod_{v\in S}Y_{v})
. Let
T^{\prime}
denote the connected centralizer in
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
of
\gamma_{0}
. Then
T^{\prime}
is unramified at
S
as it is conjugate to
T_{\mathrm{sc},v}
at each
v\in S
. The obvious image of
T^{\prime}\times Z_{G}^{0}
in
G
is then a maximal torus of
G
which is unramified at
S
.
∎

Lemma 7.2.6
.

Fix a
z
-extension of
G
as in (
7.2.4.1
).
For each
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)\in E(G)
, there exists a central extension
1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow H_{1}\rightarrow H\rightarrow 1
over
F
, with
H_{1}
connected reductive, such that the induced short exact sequence
1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow Z_{H_{1}}\rightarrow Z_{H}\rightarrow 1
fits in the following row-exact commutative diagram:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{G_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(
7.2.3.1
)
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{H_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

Moreover, when
F
is non-archimedean, we can choose
H_{1}
to be unramified if
H
and
G_{1}
are unramified. When
F
is a number field,
H_{1}
can be chosen to be unramified at a finite set of places
S
if
H
and
G_{1}
are unramified at
S
.

Remark 7.2.7
.

We do not claim that
H_{1}
is a
z
-extension of
H
, that is, the derived subgroup of
H_{1}
need not be simply connected.

Proof.

Choose maximal tori
T_{H}\subset H
and
T\subset G
over
F
together with an isomorphism
T_{H}\cong T
over
F
as in §
7.2.3
. We pull back the resulting embedding
Z_{H}\subset T_{H}\cong T\subset G
via the surjection
G_{1}\rightarrow G
to obtain the preimage
\widetilde{Z}_{1}\subset G_{1}
fitting in the exact sequence

(7.2.7.1)

1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow\widetilde{Z}_{1}\rightarrow Z_{H}\rightarrow 1.

Recall from
[
Del79
, §2.0.1]
and the notation therein that
H=H_{\mathrm{sc}}*_{Z(H_{\mathrm{sc}})}Z_{H}
. Setting

H_{1}:=H_{\mathrm{sc}}*_{Z(H_{\mathrm{sc}})}\widetilde{Z}_{1}
,
we have a surjection
H_{1}\rightarrow H
induced by
\widetilde{Z}_{1}\rightarrow Z_{H}
, whose kernel is identified with
Z_{1}
. The construction yields
Z_{H_{1}}=\widetilde{Z}_{1}
and the commutative diagram of the lemma.
∎

7.2.8
.

Let
F
be a local or global field of characteristic
0
.
Fix a
z
-extension of
G
as in (
7.2.4.1
).
Let us explain how each
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)\in E(G)
gives rise to an endoscopic datum for
G_{1}
.

Fix inner twistings to quasi-split inner forms
H_{\overline{F}}\cong H^{*}_{\overline{F}}
and
G_{\overline{F}}\cong G^{*}_{\overline{F}}
together with
F
-pinnings for
H^{*}
and
G^{*}
. Along the central extension
H_{1}\rightarrow H
provided by the preceding lemma, we can lift inner twistings to obtain
H_{1,\overline{F}}\cong H_{1,\overline{F}}^{*}
and
G_{1,\overline{F}}\cong G_{1,\overline{F}}^{*}
as well as
F
-pinnings for
H_{1}^{*}
and
G_{1}^{*}
. As explained in
[
Kot84b
, 1.8]
, we obtain

\Gamma
-equivariant maps
\widehat{H}\rightarrow\widehat{H}_{1}
,
\widehat{G}\rightarrow\widehat{G}_{1}
,
\widehat{H}_{1}\rightarrow\widehat{Z}_{1}
, and
\widehat{G}_{1}\rightarrow\widehat{Z}_{1}
. We will consider the natural extension of the last three maps to
L
-morphisms
\zeta_{G_{1}}:{}^{L}G\rightarrow{}^{L}G_{1}
,
{}^{L}H_{1}\rightarrow{}^{L}Z_{1}
, and
{}^{L}G_{1}\rightarrow{}^{L}Z_{1}
, respectively. (As for
\widehat{H}\rightarrow\widehat{H}_{1}
, we have
\zeta_{H_{1}}
in the lemma below.) The composition
\widehat{H}\rightarrow\widehat{G}\rightarrow\widehat{G}_{1}
factors through the embedding
\widehat{H}\rightarrow\widehat{H}_{1}
to yield the following commutative diagram.

(7.2.8.1)

\textstyle{\widehat{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}\widehat{H}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{G}_{1}}

Lemma 7.2.9
.

Maintain the notation of §
7.2.8
.

(i)

The embedding
\widehat{H}\hookrightarrow\widehat{H}_{1}
can be extended to an
L
-morphism
\zeta_{H_{1}}:{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow{}^{L}H_{1}
such that
\zeta_{H_{1}}
induces a homeomorphism from
{\mathcal{H}}
onto its image.

(ii)

There exists an
L
-morphism
\eta_{1}:{}^{L}H_{1}\rightarrow{}^{L}G_{1}
such that the following is a commutative diagram extending (
7.2.8.1
).

\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{H_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{{}^{L}H_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{1}}
\textstyle{{}^{L}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{G_{1}}}
\textstyle{{}^{L}G_{1}}

(iii)

The quadruple
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}:=(H_{1},{}^{L}H_{1},s_{1},\eta_{1})
, with
s_{1}:=\zeta_{G_{1}}(s)
, is an endoscopic datum for
G_{1}
. The isomorphism class of
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
is independent of the choices in (i) and (ii).

Proof.

To verify (i), consider the split extension of
\widehat{H}_{1}
by
W_{F}
given by
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}:=(Z(\widehat{H}_{1})\rtimes{\mathcal{H}})/Z(\widehat{H})
, where
Z(\widehat{H})
embeds in the semi-direct product diagonally. The assignment
h\mapsto(1\rtimes h)
induces an embedding
{\mathcal{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
extending the map
\widehat{H}\hookrightarrow\widehat{H}_{1}
. As remarked in §
7.2.4
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\cong{}^{L}H_{1}
since
G_{1,\textup{sc}}=G_{1,\mathrm{der}}
, so we obtain the desired map
\zeta_{H_{1}}
by composition. Let us verify (ii). Since
G_{1,\textup{sc}}=G_{1,\mathrm{der}}
, we can extend
\widehat{H}_{1}\rightarrow\widehat{G}_{1}
to
\eta_{0}:{}^{L}H_{1}\rightarrow{}^{L}G_{1}
by
[
Lan79c
, Prop. 1]
. The two
L
-morphisms
\zeta_{G_{1}}\eta
and
\eta_{0}\zeta_{H_{1}}
differ by a 1-cocycle
a:W_{F}\rightarrow C
, where
C:=\mathrm{Cent}(\widehat{H},\widehat{G}_{1})
. Clearly
Z(\widehat{H}_{1})\subset C
. We also note that
Z(\widehat{H}_{1})\cap(C\cap\widehat{G})=Z(\widehat{H}_{1})\cap\widehat{G}=Z(\widehat{H})
. Thus

\widehat{Z}_{1}=Z(\widehat{H}_{1})/Z(\widehat{H})\subset C/C\cap\widehat{G}\subset\widehat{G}_{1}/\widehat{G}=\widehat{Z}_{1},

implying that
C=Z(\widehat{H}_{1})
.
As
a
is valued in
Z(\widehat{H}_{1})
, one can twist
\eta_{0}
by
a
to obtain
\eta_{1}
, which then makes the diagram commute. Lastly (iii) is a routine check.
∎

7.2.10
.

Given a central extension
H_{1}
of
H
as above, choose a splitting
W_{F}\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}
to consider the composition

W_{F}\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}\xrightarrow{\zeta_{H_{1}}}{}^{L}H_{1}\rightarrow{}^{L}Z_{1}.

Write
\lambda_{1}:Z_{1}(F)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
if
F
is local, or
\lambda_{1}:Z_{1}(F)\backslash Z_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{F})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
if
F
is global, for the corresponding continuous character, which is independent of the choice of splitting. This character naturally shows up in endoscopic transfer.

We show that the assignment
{\mathfrak{e}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
admits an inverse map.

Lemma 7.2.11
.

The map
{\mathfrak{e}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
defined by Lemma
7.2.9
induces a bijection from
\mathcal{E}(G)
onto
\mathcal{E}(G_{1})
.

Proof.

The injectivity is easy to see since
\widehat{G}_{1}
is generated by
\widehat{G}
and
Z(\widehat{G}_{1})
.

To prove the surjectivity, let
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}=(H_{1},{}^{L}H_{1},s_{1},\eta_{1})\in E(G_{1})
.
Replacing
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
by an isomorphic datum, we may assume that
\eta_{1}(W_{F})
lies in the subgroup
{}^{L}G
of
{}^{L}G_{1}
. Indeed, consider the exact sequence of continuous cohomology

\coh^{1}(W_{F},\widehat{G})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(W_{F},\widehat{G}_{1})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(W_{F},\widehat{Z}_{1}).

The image of
\eta_{1}
under the second map lifts to a 1-cocycle
c
valued in
Z(\widehat{G}_{1})
, up to a 1-coboundary, via the map
\coh^{1}(W_{F},Z(\widehat{G}_{1}))\rightarrow\coh^{1}(W_{F},\widehat{Z}_{1})
, which is surjective by
[
Lan79c
, Lem. 4]
. Then
c\cdot\eta_{1}
comes from a 1-cocycle valued in
\widehat{G}
up to a 1-coboundary.

We define
H
to be the cokernel of the composite map
Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z_{G_{1}}\hookrightarrow Z_{H_{1}}\hookrightarrow H_{1}
.
We can write
s_{1}=sz
for some
s\in\widehat{G}
and
z\in Z(\widehat{G}_{1})
. By pulling back
\eta_{1}:{}^{L}H_{1}\rightarrow{}^{L}G_{1}
via
{}^{L}G\hookrightarrow{}^{L}G_{1}
, we obtain an injection
\eta:{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow{}^{L}G
and see that
{\mathcal{H}}
is a split extension of
W_{F}
by
\widehat{H}
(as
{\mathcal{H}}
is generated by
\eta_{1}(W_{F})
and
\widehat{H}
).

It is enough to verify that
{\mathfrak{e}}:=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)
is an endoscopic datum for
G
, since it would then be obvious that
{\mathfrak{e}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
by construction, and we will be done. The main point to show is that
\Int(s)\circ\eta=a\eta
with trivial (resp. locally trivial) 1-cocycle
a:W_{F}\rightarrow Z(\widehat{G})
if
F
is local (resp. global). Let us check this when
F
is global as the local case is only simpler. Since
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{E}}(G_{1})
we know that
\Int(s)\circ\eta_{1}=\Int(s_{1})\circ\eta_{1}=a_{1}\eta_{1}
with a locally trivial 1-cocycle
a_{1}:W_{F}\rightarrow Z(\widehat{G}_{1})
. Since
\eta_{1}(W_{F})\subset\widehat{G}
we have that
a_{1}(W_{F})\subset Z(\widehat{G}_{1})\cap\widehat{G}=Z(\widehat{G})
and that
\Int(s)\circ\eta=a_{1}\eta
. As
G_{1}
is a
z
-extension of
G
, the map
\coh^{1}(W_{F_{v}},Z(\widehat{G}))\rightarrow\coh^{1}(W_{F_{v}},Z(\widehat{G}_{1}))
is injective at each place
v
(e.g., by
[
Kot84b
, Cor. 2.3]
). Hence
a_{1}
is locally trivial as a cocycle valued in
Z(\widehat{G})
.
∎

7.2.12
.

From here until the end of §
7.2
,
F
is assumed to be non-archimedean. Write
F_{m}
for the unramified extension of
F
of degree
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
in a fixed algebraic closure
\overline{F}
. Denote by
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(F_{m}/F)
the arithmetic Frobenius generator. Fix a
z
-extension
1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow G_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1
. Set
R:=\Res_{F_{m}/F}G
and
R_{1}:=\Res_{F_{m}/F}G_{1}
. Write
\theta
(resp.
\theta_{1}
) for the automorphism of
R
(resp.
R_{1}
) induced by
\sigma
. Identify

\widehat{R}=\widehat{G}^{\Hom(F_{m},\overline{F})}=\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}\widehat{G}

such that the
j
-th component corresponds to the inclusion
F_{m}\subset\overline{F}
precomposed by
\sigma^{j}
, and similarly for
\widehat{R}_{1}
. There are unique embeddings
i:{}^{L}G\hookrightarrow{}^{L}R
and
i_{1}:{}^{L}G_{1}\hookrightarrow{}^{L}R_{1}
such that the maps are diagonal embeddings on the dual groups and the identity map on the Weil groups.

The following is a variant of Lemma
7.2.6
. In practice
G_{1}
and
H_{1}
over
F
will come from central extensions over
\mathbb{Q}
independently of
m
. By contrast, the extensions
G^{\prime}_{1}
and
H^{\prime}_{1}
below depend on
m
and will be considered only in a local setting.

Lemma 7.2.13
.

Suppose that
G
and
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)
are unramified. Consider
z
-extensions
1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow G_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1
and
1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow H_{1}\rightarrow H\rightarrow 1
as constructed in Lemma
7.2.6
(disregarding the last assertion) such that
G_{1}
and
H_{1}
are unramified. Let
F^{\prime}/F
be a finite unramified extension. Then there exist

(i)

a
z
-extension
1\rightarrow Z^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow G^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1
such that
Z^{\prime}_{1}\cong\prod_{j\in J}\Res_{F^{\prime}_{j}/F}\mathbb{G}_{m}
with
J
a finite index set and
F^{\prime}_{j}\supset F^{\prime}
, and

(ii)

a central extension
1\rightarrow Z^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow H^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow H\rightarrow 1
arising from (i) as in Lemma
7.2.6
,

such that
G^{\prime}_{1}
and
H^{\prime}_{1}
are unramified over
F
, and such that
there is a commutative diagram with an injective middle vertical arrow

(7.2.13.1)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 71.5278pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.5278pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 113.8764pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 113.8764pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.73889pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 151.73889pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

as well as the analogous diagram with
H,H_{1},H^{\prime}_{1}
in place of
G,G_{1},G^{\prime}_{1}
.

Proof.

We have
Z_{1}\cong\prod_{j\in J}\Res_{E_{j}/F}\mathbb{G}_{m}
for finite unramified extensions
E_{j}/F
. Take
Z^{\prime}_{1}:=\prod_{j\in J}\Res_{E_{j}F^{\prime}/F}\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Along the canonical inclusion
Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z^{\prime}_{1}
, we make a pushout diagram from the top row of Lemma
7.2.6
as follows:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{G_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{G_{1}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

With the bottom row in place of (
7.2.7.1
) we construct a
z
-extension
1\rightarrow Z^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow G^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1
such that
Z_{G^{\prime}_{1}}=Z_{G_{1}}^{\prime}
as in the proof of Lemma
7.2.6
. By construction, we have (i) and (
7.2.13.1
).
Applying Lemma
7.2.6
to
G^{\prime}_{1}
, we obtain (ii).
Since
Z_{H_{1}}
and
Z_{H^{\prime}_{1}}
are preimages of
Z_{H}\subset G
under
G_{1}\rightarrow G
and
G^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow G
, we have
Z_{H_{1}}\subset Z_{H^{\prime}_{1}}
. This in turn induces
H_{1}=H_{\textup{sc}}*_{Z(H_{\textup{sc}})}Z_{H_{1}}\subset H^{\prime}_{1}=H_{\textup{sc}}*_{Z(H_{\textup{sc}})}Z_{H^{\prime}_{1}}
. With this inclusion as the middle vertical arrow, we see that there is a commutative diagram analogous to (
7.2.13.1
) for
H,H_{1},H^{\prime}_{1}
.
∎

7.2.14
.

Under a temporary assumption on
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)\in E(G)
that

s\in Z(\widehat{H})^{\Gamma_{F}}

(in general
s\in Z(\widehat{H})^{\Gamma_{F}}Z(\widehat{G})
), we construct some twisted endoscopic data to be used in the stabilization (§
8
). Put
\widetilde{s}:=(s,1,...,1)\in\widehat{R}
, which lies in
{\mathcal{Z}}:=\mathrm{Cent}(i\eta({\mathcal{H}}),\widehat{R})
. Define the
L
-morphism
\widetilde{\eta}:{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow{}^{L}R
to be the twist of
i\eta
by the unramified 1-cocycle
a:W_{F}\rightarrow{\mathcal{Z}}
mapping
\sigma
to
\widetilde{s}
. Then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},\widetilde{s},\widetilde{\eta})
is checked to be a twisted endoscopic datum for
(R,\theta)
, cf.
[
Mor10
, A.1.3, A.2.6]
or
[
Kot90
, §7]
. Replacing
{\mathfrak{e}}
by
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
(noting that the temporary assumption is still satisfied for
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
, i.e.,
s_{1}\in Z(\widehat{H}_{1})^{\Gamma_{F}}
) we construct a twisted endoscopic datum
\widetilde{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}=(H_{1},{}^{L}H_{1},\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{1})
for
(R_{1},\theta_{1})
. With
H^{\prime}_{1}
playing the role of
H_{1}
, we also construct
{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}_{1}
.

7.3.
Cohomological lemmas

7.3.1
.

Let
F
,
G
, and
{\mathfrak{e}}
be as in §
7.2
. (The field
F
is either local or global.) Take a
z
-extension
G_{1}
as in Lemma
7.2.6
. By Hilbert 90 the map
G_{1}(F)\rightarrow G(F)
is onto. Let
\gamma\in G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
and choose a lift
\gamma_{1}\in G_{1}(F)
. We have a commutative diagram of reductive groups

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{\gamma_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1,}

which gives rise to a
\Gamma
-equivariant commutative diagram by
[
Kot84b
, 1.8]
:

(7.3.1.1)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z(\widehat{I}_{\gamma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z(\widehat{I}_{\gamma_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z(\widehat{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z(\widehat{G}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{Z}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

In particular we get a
\Gamma
-equivariant isomorphism

Z(\widehat{I}_{\gamma})/Z(\widehat{G})\cong Z(\widehat{I}_{\gamma_{1}})/Z(\widehat{G}_{1}).

Lemma 7.3.2
.

We have

(i)

If
F
is global or local, there is a canonical isomorphism

{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{\gamma}/F)\cong{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{\gamma_{1}}/F).

(ii)

If
F
is local non-archimedean, there is a canonical bijection

{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma_{1}},G_{1};F)\cong{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F).

Proof.

Let us check (i) in the global case. We obtain the following commutative diagram from
[
Kot84b
, Cor. 2.3]
and diagram (
7.3.1.1
):

\textstyle{\pi_{0}((Z(\widehat{I}_{\gamma})/Z(\widehat{G}))^{\Gamma_{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\coh^{1}(F,Z(\widehat{G}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}((Z(\widehat{I}_{\gamma_{1}})/Z(\widehat{G}_{1}))^{\Gamma_{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{1}(F,Z(\widehat{G}_{1})).}

The left vertical map is an isomorphism. The right vertical map is injective by
[
Kot84b
, Cor. 2.3]
since
\widehat{Z}_{1}^{\Gamma_{F}}
is connected (a product of copies of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
).
Further observe that the injective right vertical map induces an isomorphism
\ker^{1}(F,Z(\widehat{G}))\cong\ker^{1}(F,Z(\widehat{G}_{1}))
. To see this, notice that the cokernel is mapped injectively into
\ker^{1}(F,\widehat{Z}_{1})
, which is trivial since
Z_{1}
is a product of induced tori.
Since
{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{\gamma}/F)
(resp.
{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{\gamma_{1}}/F)
) is the preimage of
\ker^{1}(F,Z(\widehat{G}))
(resp.
\ker^{1}(F,Z(\widehat{G}_{1}))
) under the top (resp. bottom) horizontal arrow, the left vertical map induces the desired isomorphism of (i). In the case of local fields, the same argument works if we replace the
\ker^{1}
-groups with the trivial group.

For (ii) consider the following commutative diagram of pointed sets (or of abelian groups by identifying
\coh^{1}
with
\coh^{1}_{\ab}
)

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma_{1}},G_{1};F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{1}(F,I_{\gamma_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{1}(F,G_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{1}(F,I_{\gamma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{1}(F,G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{2}(F,Z_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coh^{2}(F,Z_{1}),}

where the middle and right columns come from the exact sequences preceding the lemma and the fact that
\coh^{1}(F,\cdot)=\coh^{1}_{\ab}(F,\cdot)
when
F
is non-archimedean (see
1.1.6
). Assertion (ii) now follows from a diagram chase.
∎

7.3.3
.

Let
(G,X)
be a Shimura datum.
We study Kottwitz parameters and their invariants with respect to a
z
-extension

1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow G_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1
over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
T\subset G_{\mathbb{R}}
be an elliptic maximal torus.
There exists
h\in X
such that
\mu_{h}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow G{C}
factors through
T_{\mathbb{C}}
.
In the notation of §
2.4.1
,
\mu_{h}\in\bbmu_{X}(\mathbb{C})
.
Write
T_{1}\subset G_{1,\mathbb{R}}
for the preimage of
T
. Then
\mu_{h}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow T{C}
lifts to a cocharacter
\mu_{1}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow T_{1,\mathbb{C}}
, which we fix henceforth and view also as a cocharacter of
G_{1}
over
\mathbb{C}
. As noted in
[
MS82
, 3.4]
, the conjugacy class of
\mu_{1}
comes from a Shimura datum
(G_{1},X_{1})
for a suitable
X_{1}
, that is,
\mu_{1}\in\bbmu_{X_{1}}(\mathbb{C})
.
In particular the discussion of cohomological invariants (§§
1.7.5
–
1.7.7
) applies to
(G_{1},X_{1})
and
\mu_{1}
.

Let
\gamma_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
and
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
such that
\gamma_{0,1}
maps to
\gamma_{0}
. Write
I_{0,1}
and
I_{0}
for the connected centralizers of
\gamma_{0,1}
and
\gamma_{0}
in
G_{1}
and
G
over
\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}
, respectively.

Lemma 7.3.4
.

Suppose that
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
is the image of an element
\gamma_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})_{\textup{ss}}
. Then the natural map
{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})\rightarrow{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{0},G;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
is a bijection.

Proof.

This follows from (
1.1.6.1
) and part (ii) of Lemma
7.3.2
.
∎

7.3.5
.

Now consider a
z
-extension
1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow G_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
(which need not come from
\mathbb{Q}
-groups via base change). Let
\mu:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow G_{\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}}
be a cocharacter over
\overline{\mathbb Q}_{p}
. Let
\gamma_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})_{\textup{ss}}
, and
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
the image of
\gamma_{0,1}
. As usual,
I_{0}
and
I_{0,1}
are the connected centralizers of
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{0,1}
in
G
and
G_{1}
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, respectively. Fix a level
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Denote by
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
the set of all
[b]\in\B(I_{0})
satisfying condition
KP1
of Definition
1.6.5
with the given
\gamma_{0}
and
n
. (Here we do not require
KP0
, which will come into play in Corollary
7.4.18
below.) This means that for some (thus every) representative
b\in I_{0}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
of
[b]
, there exists
c\in G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that

c^{-1}\gamma_{0}c=c^{-1}b\sigma(b)\cdots{\sigma^{n-1}}(b){\sigma^{n}}(c).

Given
[b]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, we will often write
\delta_{[b]}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
for the element arising from
[b]
(well defined up to
\sigma
-conjugacy in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
) as in Lemma
1.6.7
. Then
\gamma_{0}
is a degree
n
norm of
\delta_{[b]}
.

Likewise we define
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. The natural map
I_{0,1}\rightarrow I_{0}
induces a map

(7.3.5.1)

{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Lemma 7.3.6
.

Let
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0,1}
be as above. Suppose that
Z_{1}\cong\prod_{j\in J}\Res_{F_{j}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
with all
F_{j}
containing
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}
and
J
a finite index set.
Then the map (
7.3.5.1
) is a bijection.

Proof.

The map (
7.3.5.1
) fits in the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(
7.3.5.1
)
\textstyle{\B(I_{0,1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\B(R_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\B(I_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\B(R),}

where the vertical maps are induced by the natural maps
I_{0,1}\rightarrow I_{0}
and
R_{1}\rightarrow R
.

We may assume that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is non-empty since the lemma is vacuously true otherwise.
We claim that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is also non-empty. To see this, fix
[b]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and pick a lift
\delta^{\prime}_{1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
of
\delta_{[b]}
. Let
\gamma^{\prime}_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be a degree
n
norm of
\delta^{\prime}_{1}
. Then the norm of
z\delta^{\prime}_{1}
is
z{}^{\sigma}z\cdots{}^{\sigma^{n-1}}z\gamma^{\prime}_{0,1}
with
z\in Z_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
. The norm map
Z_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})\rightarrow Z_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is onto by the hypothesis on
Z_{1}
, so we may choose
z
such that
\gamma_{0,1}
is a norm of
\delta_{1}:=z\delta^{\prime}_{1}
. This implies that there is
c_{1}\in G_{1}(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that
c_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{0,1}c_{1}=\delta_{1}{}^{\sigma}\delta_{1}\cdots{}^{\sigma^{n-1}}\delta_{1}
. Setting
b_{1}:=c_{1}\delta_{1}{}^{\sigma}c_{1}^{-1}
, we see that
b_{1}\in I_{0,1}(\breve{\mathbb}{Q}_{p})
(since
G_{1}
has simply connected derived subgroup) and that
[b_{1}]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, proving the claim.

We fix
[b]
,
\delta_{1}
, and
[b_{1}]
as in the last paragraph. In particular
\delta_{1}
maps to
\delta:=\delta_{[b]}
, and
[b_{1}]
to
[b]
.
By Corollary
1.6.11
,
[b_{1}]\in\B(I_{0,1})
and
[b]\in\B(I_{0})
are basic. Define
{\mathfrak{D}}([b_{1}],R_{1})
to be the set of basic elements
[b^{\prime}_{1}]\in\B(I_{0,1})
such that
\kappa_{I_{0,1}}([b^{\prime}_{1}])-\kappa_{I_{0,1}}([b_{1}])
lies in
\ker(\pi_{1}(I_{0,1})_{\Gamma_{p},\tors}\to\pi_{1}(R_{1})_{\Gamma_{p},\tors})
. Define
{\mathfrak{D}}([b],R)
exactly in the same way with
I_{0}
and
R
in place of
I_{0,1}
and
R_{1}
.

We claim that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a subset of
{\mathfrak{D}}([b],R)
. Indeed, consider an element
[b^{\prime}]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, which gives rise to
\delta^{\prime}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
as in Lemma
1.6.7
. Corollary
1.6.11
implies that
\kappa_{I_{0,1}}([b^{\prime}_{1}])-\kappa_{I_{0,1}}([b_{1}])
is a torsion element in
\pi_{1}(I_{0,1})_{\Gamma_{p}}
via the right half of the commutative diagram on
[
RR96
, p. 162]
. We know from Lemma
1.6.7
that
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
have the same norm, implying that
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
are stably
\sigma
-conjugate by
[
Kot82
, Prop. 5.7]
. Therefore
[\delta]
and
[\delta^{\prime}]
are equal in
\B(R)
. Since
b
and
b^{\prime}
are
\sigma
-conjugate to
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
in
G(\breve{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, respectively, it follows that
[b]=[b^{\prime}]
in
\B(R)
. Hence
\kappa_{I_{0}}([b^{\prime}])
and
\kappa_{I_{0}}([b])
have the same image in
\pi_{1}(R)_{\Gamma_{p}}
, completing the proof of the claim.

What we have shown is summarized in the following commutative diagram.

(7.3.6.1)

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}([b_{1}],R_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{bij}.}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{D}}([b],R)}

The right vertical map is a bijection by the proof of
[
Kot82
, Lem. 5.6.(2)]
. (This relies on the assumption of the lemma on
Z_{1}
.) Indeed, the proof there shows a canonical bijection from

\ker(\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},I_{\delta_{1}})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},R_{1}))

to

\ker(\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},I_{\delta})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},R)),

but this is exactly the right vertical map above via the functorial bijection between
\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},H)
and
\pi_{1}(H)_{\Gamma_{p},\tors}
for an arbitrary connected reductive group
H
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, cf.
[
Lab99
, Prop. 1.6.7]
and
[
RR96
, Thm. 1.15.(i)]
.

Now we verify that the top horizontal map in (
7.3.6.1
) is a bijection. As we have seen in the last paragraph,
{\mathfrak{D}}([b_{1}],R_{1})
is in a canonical bijection with
\ker(\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},I_{\delta_{1}})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},R_{1}))
, which in turn is canonically bijective onto the set of
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in the stable
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
\delta_{1}
in
G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
. Recall that
\gamma_{0,1}
is a norm of
\delta_{1}
. Therefore each
\delta^{\prime}_{1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
stably
\sigma
-conjugate to
\delta_{1}
gives rise to an element of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})
as described in the second paragraph of the proof of the current lemma (more detailed on p. 167 of
[
Kot90
]
). It is routine to check that this map gives the inverse of the top horizontal map in (
7.3.6.1
).

Going back to diagram (
7.3.6.1
), it is now clear that the left vertical map (as well as the lower horizontal map) is a bijection. ∎

Corollary 7.3.7
.

Assume that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is non-empty. Then the map

[b]\mapsto\delta_{[b]}
gives a surjection from
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
onto the set of
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
the stable
\sigma
-conjugacy class in
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
whose norm is
\gamma_{0}
.
If
G
has simply connected derived subgroup then this map is a bijection.

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
7.2.13
, we can choose a
z
-extension
G_{1}
such that the condition on
Z_{1}
in Lemma
7.3.6
is satisfied.
Then the corollary follows from the bijectivity of the lower horizontal map of (
7.3.6.1
), together with the interpretation of
{\mathfrak{D}}([b],R)
in terms of
\sigma
-conjugacy classes below (
7.3.6.1
).
∎

7.3.8
.

Now we turn to the real place.
Let
(G,X)
and
(G_{1},X_{1})
be as in §
7.3.3
.
Following §
1.7.5
and §
1.7.6
, for each elliptic element
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{R})
, we have
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0})\in\pi_{1}(I_{0})=X^{*}(Z(\widehat{I}_{0}))
mapping to
[\mu]\in\pi_{1}(G)
for any
\mu\in\bbmu_{X}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. The definition involves an extra choice, but the restriction of
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0})
to
Z(\widehat{I}_{0})^{\Gamma_{\infty}}Z(\widehat{G})
is independent of the choice. Analogously, for each elliptic element
\gamma_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0,1})\in\pi_{1}(I_{0,1})=X^{*}(Z(\widehat{I}_{0,1}))
mapping to
[\mu_{1}]\in\pi_{1}(G_{1})
with
\mu_{1}\in\bbmu_{X_{1}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Lemma 7.3.9
.

Assume that
\gamma_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{R})
are elliptic elements such that
\gamma_{0,1}
maps to
\gamma_{0}
. Then the image of
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0,1})
under the natural map
\pi_{1}(I_{0,1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(I_{0})
coincides with
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0})
on
Z(\widehat{I}_{0})^{\Gamma_{\infty}}Z(\widehat{G})
.

Proof.

In §
1.7.5
and §
1.7.6
(adapted to
(G_{1},X_{1})
), choose any
\mathbb{R}
-elliptic maximal torus
T_{1}\subset G_{1}
and
h_{1}\in X_{1}
factoring through
T_{1}
to define
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0,1})
. Take
T\subset G
and
h\in X
to be the images of
T_{1}
and
h_{1}
in the definition of
\widetilde{\beta}_{\infty}(\gamma_{0})
.
Then the lemma is true on the nose.
∎

7.4.
Langlands–Shelstad–Kottwitz transfer

7.4.1
.

Let
F
,
G
, and
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)
be as in Definition
7.2.2
.
Throughout §
7.4
, assume
F
to be a local field (of characteristic
0
).
Let
\psi:G^{*}_{\overline{F}}\rightarrow G_{\overline{F}}
be an inner twisting of
F
-groups with
G^{*}
quasi-split over
F
. We will use the following notation for
G
(and likewise for other reductive groups).

•

\Gamma(G)=\Gamma(G(F))
is the set of semi-simple
G(F)
-conjugacy classes in
G(F)
,

•

\Sigma(G)=\Sigma(G(F))
is the set of stable semi-simple conjugacy classes in
G(F)
,

•

{\mathcal{H}}(G)={\mathcal{H}}(G(F))
and
{\mathcal{H}}(G,\omega^{-1})={\mathcal{H}}(G(F),\omega^{-1})
as in §
7.1.4
,

•

When
F
is non-archimedean and
G
is unramified, we fix a hyperspecial subgroup
K\subset G(F)
and define
{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G)\subset{\mathcal{H}}(G)
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G,\omega^{-1})\subset{\mathcal{H}}(G,\omega^{-1})
to be the subalgebra consisting of
K
-bi-invariant functions.

7.4.2
.

We explain the transfer of conjugacy classes in endoscopy following
[
Kot86
, §3.1]
. See also
[
LS87
, §1.3]
.

Let
\gamma_{H}\in H(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
. Choose a maximal torus
T_{H}
of
H
over
F
containing
\gamma_{H}
. There exists a canonical
G^{*}(\overline{F})
-conjugacy class of embeddings
j:T_{H}\rightarrow G^{*}
over
\overline{F}
. Fix a choice of
j
and put
T^{*}:=j(T_{H})
. Denoting the set of absolute roots of
T_{H}
in
H
(resp.
T^{*}
in
G^{*}
) by
R(T_{H},H)
(resp.
R(T^{*},G^{*})
), we have
R(T_{H},H)\subset R(T^{*},G^{*})
. The element
\gamma_{H}
is said to be
(G,H)
-regular
if
\alpha(\gamma_{H})\neq 1
for all
\alpha\in R(T^{*},G^{*})\backslash R(T_{H},H)
. The definition depends only on the
H(\overline{F})
-conjugacy class of
\gamma_{H}
and not on the extra choices. The
(G,H)
-regular subset of
H(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
and
\Sigma(H)
will be denoted by
H(F)_{(G,H)\text{-}{\rm reg}}
and
\Sigma(H)_{(G,H)\text{-}{\rm reg}}
, respectively.

Let
\gamma_{H}\in H(F)_{(G,H)\text{-}{\rm reg}}
. The
\overline{F}
-embedding
\psi\circ j:T_{H}\rightarrow G
is canonical up to
G(\overline{F})
-conjugacy, so
\gamma_{H}
determines a semi-simple
G(\overline{F})
-conjugacy class in
G(\overline{F})
defined over
F
. If this conjugacy class contains an element
\gamma
of
G(F)
, then we take the stable conjugacy class of
\gamma
to be the image of
\gamma_{H}
. Otherwise the image of
\gamma_{H}
is formally denoted by the empty set symbol
\emptyset
.
(Such a
\gamma
always exists by
[
Kot82
, Thm. 4.4]
if
G
is quasi-split and has simply connected derived subgroup, but not in general.)
To summarize, we obtain a map

(7.4.2.1)

\Sigma(H)_{(G,H)\text{-}{\rm reg}}\longrightarrow\Sigma(G)\cup\{\emptyset\}.

We say that
\gamma_{H}
and
\gamma\in G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
have
matching conjugacy classes
, or simply that
\gamma
is an
image
of
\gamma_{H}
. If the centralizer of
\gamma
in
G
is connected (e.g., if
G_{\mathrm{der}}=G_{\mathrm{sc}}
) then the centralizer of
\gamma_{H}
in
H
is also connected by
[
Kot86
, Lem. 3.2]
.

If
G_{1}
and
H_{1}
are as in (
7.2.4.1
) and Lemma
7.2.6
, then the above construction can be performed for
G_{1}
and
H_{1}
in place of
G
and
H
. This is visibly compatible with the surjections
G_{1}\rightarrow G
and
H_{1}\rightarrow H
, leading to the following commutative diagram (where the symbol
\emptyset
maps to itself under the right vertical map).

\textstyle{\Sigma(H_{1})_{(G_{1},H_{1})\text{-}{\rm reg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma(G_{1})\cup\{\emptyset\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma(H)_{(G,H)\text{-}{\rm reg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma(G)\cup\{\emptyset\}}

By slight abuse of language (as
H_{1}
is not an endoscopic group of
G
),
\gamma\in G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
is said to be an image of
\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(F)_{(G_{1},H_{1})\text{-}{\rm reg}}
if the stable conjugacy class of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
maps to that of
\gamma
in the above diagram.

7.4.3
.

We introduce
\kappa
-orbital integrals, of which stable orbital integrals are the special case.
Let us assume that
F
is local for convenience. The main definitions here extend to the adelic setting in the obvious manner.

Let
\gamma\in G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
and
x\in G(\overline{F})
. Suppose that
\gamma_{x}:=x^{-1}\gamma x\in G(F)
and
x\rho(x)^{-1}\in I_{\gamma}(\overline{F})
for every
\rho\in\Gamma_{F}
. Then
x
and the 1-cocycle
\rho\mapsto x\rho(x)^{-1}
define an element of
\coh^{0}(F,I_{\gamma}\backslash G)
, to be denoted by
\dot{x}
. The map
x\mapsto\gamma_{x}
factors through
\coh^{0}(F,I_{\gamma}\backslash G)
, namely there is an induced map

\coh^{0}(F,I_{\gamma}\backslash G)\longrightarrow G(F),\quad\dot{x}\longmapsto\gamma_{\dot{x}}.

Recall that there is a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow I_{\gamma}(F)\backslash G(F)\rightarrow\coh^{0}(F,I_{\gamma}\backslash G)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)\rightarrow 1,

coming from a long exact sequence. Given Haar measures on
I_{\gamma}(F)
and
G(F)
and the counting measure on
{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)
, there is a unique way to equip
\coh^{0}(F,I_{\gamma}\backslash G)
with a compatible measure. The map
\dot{x}\mapsto\gamma_{\dot{x}}
induces a map

{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)\longrightarrow\Gamma(G),\qquad[x]\longmapsto\gamma_{[x]},

whose image consists of conjugacy classes in the stable conjugacy class of
\gamma
. If
G_{\gamma}=\mathrm{Cent}(\gamma,G)
is connected (so that it equals
I_{\gamma}
) then
[x]\mapsto\gamma_{[x]}
is a bijection onto the image. In general the fiber over the conjugacy class of
\gamma^{\prime}\in G(F)
(stably conjugate to
\gamma
) is in bijection with
\ker(\coh^{1}(F,I_{\gamma^{\prime}})\rightarrow\coh^{1}(F,G_{\gamma^{\prime}}))
.
Recall the map
{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)\rightarrow{\mathfrak{E}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)={\mathfrak{K}}(I_{\gamma}/F)
from §
1.1.7
. Thus we have a pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:~{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)\times{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{\gamma}/F)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

Given
\kappa\in{\mathfrak{K}}(I_{\gamma}/F)
and
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G)
we define the
\kappa
-orbital integral of
\gamma
by

\displaystyle O^{G(F),\kappa}_{\gamma}(f)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{\coh^{0}(F,I\backslash G)}e(I_{\gamma_{\dot{x}}})\langle\dot{x},\kappa\rangle O^{G(F)}_{\gamma_{\dot{x}}}(f)d\dot{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{[x]\in{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)}e(I_{\gamma_{[x]}})\langle[x],\kappa\rangle O^{G(F)}_{\gamma_{[x]}}(f).

When
\kappa
is trivial, one has the stable orbital integral

SO^{G(F)}_{\gamma}(f):=O^{G(F),1}_{\gamma}(f).

The superscript
G(F)
will be omitted if there is no danger of confusion. The above definition of
\kappa
-orbital integrals works verbatim for
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G(F),\omega^{-1})
.

7.4.4
.

We recall the Langlands–Shelstad transfer in the local untwisted case. See §
7.4.12
below for the twisted case.

When
{\mathcal{H}}={}^{L}H
in the endoscopic datum (this assumption will be removed via
z
-extensions), Langlands–Shelstad
[
LS87
,
LS90
]
define the transfer factor

\Delta(\cdot,\cdot):H(F)_{(G,H)\text{-}{\rm reg}}\times G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

which vanishes on
(\gamma_{H},\gamma)
unless
\gamma
is an image of
\gamma_{H}
.

When
G
is quasi-split, the canonical transfer factor
\Delta_{0}
was given by Langlands–Shelstad depending only on a choice of
F
-pinning for
G
. Another natural normalization in the quasi-split case is the Whittaker normalization in
[
KS99
, §5.3]
. While there is no direct analogue of either when
G
is not quasi-split, the Whittaker normalization can be extended to
G
given a suitable rigidification of an inner twisting of
G
against its quasi-split inner form. See
[
Kal16
,
Kal18
]
for Kaletha’s notion of rigid inner forms and a discussion of other rigidifications. In this paper, we do not attempt to choose a rigidification or a canonical normalization of transfer factor at every place. However when
G
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, we may and will always choose a global normalization as in
[
LS87
, §6.4]
so that the product formula (Corollary 6.4.B therein) holds true.

In fact there are more than one sign conventions for (untwisted and twisted) transfer factors as explained in
[
KS12
]
. We work with the factor
\Delta^{\prime}
in
loc. cit.
, which coincides with the one in
[
Kot90
]
(see p. 178 therein) but differs from the definition of
[
LS87
]
by the map
(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)\mapsto(H,{\mathcal{H}},s^{-1},\eta)
. The reason for our choice is that the former is better suited for extension to the twisted setting.

7.4.5
.

There exists a smooth character
\lambda_{H}:Z_{G}(F)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that

(7.4.5.1)

\Delta(z\gamma_{H},z\gamma)=\lambda_{H}(z)\Delta(\gamma_{H},\gamma),\qquad z\in Z_{G}(F).

This is
[
LS90
, Lem. 3.5.A]
. We can describe
\lambda_{H}
explicitly on
Z_{G}^{0}(F)
as follows.

Lemma 7.4.6
.

When
{\mathcal{H}}={}^{L}H
, the restriction of
\lambda_{H}
to
Z_{G}^{0}(F)
corresponds to the composite Langlands parameter

W_{F}\rightarrow{}^{L}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta}}{{\rightarrow}}{}^{L}G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta}}{{\rightarrow}}{}^{L}Z_{G}^{0},

where the first map is the distinguished splitting, and
\zeta
is dual to the inclusion
Z_{G}^{0}\hookrightarrow G
.

Proof.

Consider
\gamma_{H}\in H(F)
,
\gamma\in G(F)
, and maximal tori
T\subset G
and
T_{H}\subset H
as in
[
LS87
, §3]
. In particular we are given an isomorphism
i:T\cong T_{H}
, inducing an isomorphism
{}^{L}i:{}^{L}T_{H}\cong{}^{L}T
.
They construct
L
-morphisms
\xi_{T_{H}}:{}^{L}T_{H}\rightarrow{}^{L}H
and
\xi:{}^{L}T\rightarrow{}^{L}G
(depending on some additional choices) as well as a 1-cocycle
\mathbf{a}:W_{F}\rightarrow\widehat{T}_{H}
such that
\eta\xi_{T_{H}}=\mathbf{a}\cdot\xi_{T}{}^{L}i
as
L
-morphisms from
{}^{L}T_{H}
to
{}^{L}G
. Restricting the equality via the splitting
W_{F}\rightarrow{}^{L}T_{H}
, we obtain

(7.4.6.1)

\eta(\xi_{T_{H}}(w))=\mathbf{a}(w)\xi_{T}(w)\in{}^{L}G,\qquad w\in W_{F}.

The first paragraph of
[
LS87
, p. 253]
tells us that
\lambda_{H}|_{Z_{G}^{0}(F)}
corresponds to
\mathbf{a}
composed with
\widehat{T}_{H}\cong\widehat{T}\rightarrow\widehat{Z}_{G}^{0}
, which is dual to
Z_{G}^{0}\subset T\cong T_{H}
. To prove the lemma, it is thus enough to verify that the composite map in the lemma is
w\mapsto\zeta(\mathbf{a}(w))\rtimes w
.

To this end, write
\xi_{T_{H}}(w)=b(w)\rtimes w
and
\xi_{T}(w)=c(w)\rtimes w
. From the construction of
\xi_{T}
in
[
LS87
, §2.6]
, it follows that
\zeta(c(w))=1
. Indeed, the two main points are that the image of every morphism
\mathrm{SL}_{2}\rightarrow\widehat{G}
maps trivially in
\widehat{Z}_{G}^{0}
(thus also
n(\omega_{T}(\sigma))
therein) and that the coroots of
\widehat{G}
map trivially in
\widehat{Z}_{G}^{0}
(thus also
r_{p}(w)
therein). Similarly,
b(w)
maps to
1\in\widehat{Z}_{H}^{0}
, so
\zeta(b(w))=1
. Now we apply
\zeta
to (
7.4.6.1
) to see that

\zeta(\eta(w))=\zeta(\mathbf{a}(w))\rtimes w
, as desired.
∎

7.4.7
.

We introduce transfer factors in general by reducing to the case
G_{\mathrm{der}}=G_{\textup{sc}}
, in which case we can always assume that
{\mathcal{H}}={}^{L}H
, cf. §
7.2.4
and §
7.4.4
.
Let
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)\in E(G)
. Take a
z
-extension
1\rightarrow Z_{1}\rightarrow G_{1}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1
and define
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}=(H_{1},{}^{L}H_{1},s_{1},\eta_{1})\in E(G_{1})
as in §
7.2.8
.
Then we have
\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{1})\in\mathbb{C}
defined on
\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(F)_{(G_{1},H_{1})\text{-}{\rm reg}}
and
\gamma_{1}\in G_{1}(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
. Langlands–Shelstad define

\Delta(\cdot,\cdot):H_{1}(F)_{(G_{1},H_{1})\text{-}{\rm reg}}\times G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

as follows. Set
\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma)=0
unless
\gamma
is an image of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
(§
7.4.2
), that is, unless
\gamma
lifts to
\gamma_{1}\in G_{1}(F)
which is an image of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
, in which case
\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma):=\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{1})
. By (
7.4.5.1
),

\Delta(z\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma)=\lambda_{H_{1}}(z)\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma),\qquad z\in Z_{G_{1}}^{0}(F).

Notice that
Z_{1}\subset Z_{G_{1}}^{0}
. Lemma
7.4.6
implies that
\lambda_{H_{1}}|_{Z_{1}(F)}=\lambda_{1}
, where
\lambda_{1}
was given in §
7.2.10
. (This is also checked in
[
LS87
, p. 254]
.)

7.4.8
.

Keep on assuming that
F
is a local field. We state the Langlands–Shelstad transfer and the fundamental lemma for connected reductive groups
G_{1}
with
G_{1,\mathrm{der}}=G_{1,\textup{sc}}
. Let
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}=(H_{1},{}^{L}H_{1},s_{1},\eta_{1})\in E(G_{1})
.
If
G_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
are unramified, then
\eta_{1}
induces a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra map via the Satake transform:

(7.4.8.1)

\eta_{1}^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G_{1})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1}).

Proposition 7.4.9
.

For each
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}(G_{1})
, there exists
f_{1}^{H_{1}}\in{\mathcal{H}}(H_{1})
enjoying the following property: If
\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(F)_{(G_{1},H_{1})\text{-}{\rm reg}}
has no image in
G_{1}(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
then
SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f_{1}^{H_{1}})=0
. If
\gamma_{H_{1}}
has an image
\gamma_{1}\in G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
then

(7.4.9.1)

SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}}_{1})=\sum_{[x]\in{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma_{1}},G_{1};F)}e(I_{\gamma_{1,[x]}})\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{1,[x]})O_{\gamma_{1,[x]}}(f_{1}).

Moreover, the fundamental lemma (FL) holds true, i.e., if
G_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
are unramified and if
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G_{1})
then the above holds with
f^{H_{1}}_{1}=\eta_{1}^{*}f_{1}
.

Proof.

The second assertion (FL) follows from work of Ngô as well as Cluckers-Loeser, Hales, and Waldspurger
[
CL10
,
Hal95
,
Ngô10
,
Wal06
]
. The first assertion (the transfer conjecture) is implied by FL
[
Wal97
]
.
∎

7.4.10
.

Let us adapt Proposition
7.4.9
to the setting with fixed central characters.
Let
{\mathfrak{X}}
be a closed subgroup of
Z(F)
and
\chi:{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
a continuous character. We view
{\mathfrak{X}}
also as a closed subgroup of
Z_{H}(F)
via
Z\hookrightarrow Z_{H}
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}
the preimages of
{\mathfrak{X}}
under
H_{1}(F)\rightarrow H(F)
and
G_{1}(F)\rightarrow G(F)
, respectively, so that
{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}\cong{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}
canonically. Choose compatible Haar measures on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}
. Let
\chi_{1}
denote the character of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}
or
{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}
pulled back from
\chi:{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
Restricting
\lambda_{H_{1}}
as in §
7.4.7
, we obtain another character

\lambda_{H_{1}}|_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}}:{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

By slight abuse of notation, we will often denote the above still by
\lambda_{H_{1}}
(as we will not consider
\lambda_{H_{1}}
on a larger domain). In the special case when
{\mathfrak{X}}=\{1\}
, notice that
{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}={\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}=Z_{1}(F)
,
\chi_{1}=1
, and
\lambda_{H_{1}}=\lambda_{1}
.

If
F
is non-archimedean,
\chi:{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is said to be
unramified
if the character is trivial on the maximal compact subgroup of
{\mathfrak{X}}
. The same definition works with
{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}
in place of
{\mathfrak{X}}
. When
G
,
{\mathfrak{e}}
,
\chi
, and the
z
-extension
G_{1}
are unramified, we have
G_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
also unramified. In this case, the map
\eta_{1}^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G_{1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1})
from (
7.4.8.1
) is averaged to give a map

(7.4.10.1)

{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G,\chi^{-1})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1},\chi_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{H_{1}})

as follows. Let
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G_{1})
be any lift of
f
along the natural surjective map, where the first arrow is averaging against
\chi_{1}
:

{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G_{1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G_{1},\chi_{1}^{-1})\cong{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G,\chi^{-1}).

For
z\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}
, write
f_{1,z}(h):=f_{1}(zh)
.
Define a function
f^{H_{1}}
on
H_{1}(F)
by

f^{H_{1}}(h):=\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}}\chi_{1}(z)\eta_{1}^{*}f_{1,z}(h)dz=\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}}\chi_{1}(z)\lambda^{-1}_{H_{1}}(z)\eta_{1}^{*}f_{1}(zh)dz.

Then
f^{H_{1}}
belongs to
{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1},\chi_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{H_{1}})
and is independent of the choice of
f_{1}
. The resulting map (
7.4.10.1
) is again denoted by
\eta_{1}^{*}
as there is little danger of confusion.

Proposition 7.4.11
.

For each
\chi:{\mathfrak{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G,\chi^{-1})
, there exists

f^{H_{1}}\in{\mathcal{H}}(H_{1},\chi_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{H_{1}})

such that for every
\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(F)_{(G_{1},H_{1})\text{-}{\rm reg}}
, if
\gamma\in G(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
is an image of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
then

SO^{H_{1}(F)}_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})=\sum_{[x]\in{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)}e(I_{\gamma_{[x]}})\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{[x]})O^{G(F)}_{\gamma_{[x]}}(f).

If
\gamma_{H_{1}}
admits no image in
G(F)
then
SO^{H_{1}(F)}_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})=0
.

Moreover when
F
is non-archimedean, if
G_{1}
,
{\mathfrak{e}}
, and
\chi
are unramified, and if
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G,\chi^{-1})
, then
\chi_{1},\lambda_{H_{1}}|_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}}
are unramified and the above holds true with

f^{H_{1}}=\eta_{1}^{*}(f)
.

Proof.

Given
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(G,\chi^{-1})
, choose a lifting
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}(G_{1})
under the surjective composite map

{\mathcal{H}}(G_{1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}(G_{1},\chi_{1}^{-1})={\mathcal{H}}(G,\chi^{-1})
.
Let
f_{1}^{H_{1}}\in{\mathcal{H}}(H_{1})
be a transfer of
f_{1}
as in Proposition
7.4.9
. Define
f^{H_{1}}\in{\mathcal{H}}(H_{1},\chi_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{H_{1}})
by

f^{H_{1}}(\gamma_{H_{1}}):=\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}})}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}}f^{H_{1}}_{1}(z\gamma_{H_{1}})\chi_{1}(z)\lambda_{H_{1}}^{-1}(z)dz,\quad\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(F),

so that for
\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(F)_{\mathrm{ss}}
,

(7.4.11.1)

SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})=\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}})}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}}\chi_{1}(z)\lambda_{H_{1}}^{-1}(z)SO_{z_{1}\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}}_{1})dz.

If
\gamma_{H_{1}}
has no image in
G(F)
then it has no image in
G_{1}(F)
either, so
SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})
vanishes.
Otherwise, let
\gamma_{1}\in G_{1}(F)
be an image of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
. Then
z\gamma_{1}
is an image of
z\gamma_{H_{1}}
for each
z\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}
. Applying Proposition
7.4.9
to (
7.4.11.1
), we see that
SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})
equals

\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}}\frac{\chi_{1}(z)}{\mathrm{vol}({\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}})}\sum_{[x]\in{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma_{1}},G_{1};F)}e(I_{\gamma_{1,[x]}})\frac{\Delta(z\gamma_{H_{1}},z\gamma_{1,[x]})}{\lambda_{H_{1}}(z)}O_{z\gamma_{1,[x]}}(f_{1})dz,

where
Lemma
7.3.2
gives a bijection
{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{z\gamma_{1}},G_{1};F)={\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma_{1}},G_{1};F)\cong{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)
.
(We view
[x]
also as an element of
{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{z\gamma_{1}},G_{1};F)
or
{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)
.)
By definition and (
7.4.5.1
), we have

\Delta(z\gamma_{H_{1}},z\gamma_{1,[x]})=\Delta(z\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{[x]})=\lambda_{H_{1}}(z)\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{[x]}),\qquad z\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}.

By
[
Kot83
, Cor. (2)]

e(I_{\gamma_{1,[x]}})=e(I_{\gamma_{[x]}})
. All in all, as the sums run over
[x]\in{\mathfrak{D}}(I_{\gamma},G;F)
below,

\displaystyle SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{[x]}e(I_{\gamma_{[x]}})\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{[x]})\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{vol}({\mathfrak{X}}_{1})}\int_{{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}}\chi_{1}(z)O_{z\gamma_{1,[x]}}(f_{1})dz\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{[x]}e(I_{\gamma_{[x]}})\Delta(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{[x]})O_{\gamma_{[x]}}(f).

It remains to prove the last assertion when
G
,
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}
, and
\chi
are unramified. Let
z
be an element in the maximal compact subgroup of
{\mathfrak{X}}_{H_{1}}
. In the notation above, if
f_{1}
is replaced with a translate
f_{1,z}
then
f^{H_{1}}
is multiplied by
\lambda_{H_{1}}(z)
according to §
7.4.7
. On the other hand,
f_{1}
is unchanged if translated by
z
since
f_{1}
is in the unramified Hecke algebra. Combining the two facts, we see that the stable orbital integrals of
f^{H_{1}}
do not change values under multiplication by
\lambda_{H_{1}}(z)
. Therefore
\lambda_{H_{1}}
is unramified. The fact that we can take
f^{H_{1}}=\eta_{1}^{*}(f)
follows from the earlier part of the current proof, where we can pick
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(G_{1})
and choose
f_{1}^{H_{1}}
to be the image of
f_{1}
under (
7.4.8.1
).
∎

7.4.12
.

Here we work out a small generalization of some results in local twisted endoscopy by Morel and Kottwitz
[
Mor10
, §9, App. A]
to the setting where
{\mathcal{H}}
in the endoscopic datum cannot be taken to be an
L
-group.
We put ourselves in the setting of §
7.2.12
with
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Let
{\mathfrak{e}}=(H,{\mathcal{H}},s,\eta)\in E(G)
, which gives rise to
{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}=(H_{1},{}^{L}H_{1},s_{1},\eta_{1})\in E(G_{1})
as in Lemma
7.2.9
. We make an additional hypothesis that

s\in Z(\widehat{H})^{\Gamma_{p}}.

The group
G
is assumed quasi-split over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
so that
[
Mor10
, App. A]
applies.
If
G
and
H
are unramified (which we are not assuming) then we may and will choose
G_{1}
and
H_{1}
to be also unramified.

In twisted endoscopy, the norm map is defined by Kottwitz and Shelstad
[
KS99
]
for strongly regular elements and by Labesse
[
Lab04
]
for elliptic elements (which may not be strongly regular). The norm map in untwisted endoscopy is simply the transfer of conjugacy classes as in §
7.4.2
. In the special case of base change, Kottwitz
[
Kot82
]
defines the norm map for general elements. (These norm maps coincide when there are more than one definitions available in a given setting.) For our purpose, we define the norm map from
R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
to
H_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
to be the degree
n
base change norm from
R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
to
G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
followed by the transfer of semi-simple conjugacy classes (§
7.4.2
) from
G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
to
H_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. It is an exercise to check that this is consistent with the norm map by Kottwitz–Shelstad and Labesse.

Let
\delta_{1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})=R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. It has a degree
n
norm
\gamma_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We assume
\gamma_{0,1}
to be semi-simple. Then
\delta_{1}
corresponds to
[b_{1}]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})
according to Corollary
7.3.7
. So we write
\delta_{[b_{1}]}
for
\delta_{1}=\delta_{[b_{1}]}
. Recall
\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0,1},[b_{1}])=\kappa_{I_{0,1}}([b_{1}])
.
Suppose that
\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})_{\mathrm{ss}}
is a norm of
\delta_{1}
. Then
\gamma_{0,1}
is an image of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
.

When
\gamma_{H_{1}}
is strongly
G_{1}
-regular (equivalently when
\gamma_{0,1}
is strongly regular), Kottwitz
[
Mor10
, Cor. A.2.10]
proved that

(7.4.12.1)

\Delta_{0}(\gamma_{H_{1}},\delta_{[b_{1}]})=\Delta_{0}(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{0,1})\langle\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0,1},[b_{1}]),s_{1}\rangle,

where we have taken the sign correction of
[
KS12
, §5.6]
into account.
To make sense of the pairing, we view
s_{1}\in Z(\widehat{H}_{1})^{\Gamma_{p}}
as an element of
Z(\widehat{I}_{0,1})^{\Gamma_{p}}
via
Z(\widehat{H}_{1})^{\Gamma_{p}}\subset Z(\widehat{I}_{\gamma_{H_{1}}})^{\Gamma_{p}}=Z(\widehat{I}_{0,1})^{\Gamma_{p}}
, cf.
[
Mor10
, A.3.11]
.

When
\gamma_{H_{1}}
is
(G_{1},H_{1})
-regular but not strongly
G_{1}
-regular, we take (
7.4.12.1
) as the definition of
\Delta_{0}(\gamma_{H_{1}},\delta_{[b_{1}]})
, cf.
[
Mor10
, (A.3.11.1)]
.

Let
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
[b]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be the images of
\gamma_{0,1}
and
[b_{1}]
, respectively.
Then
[b]
gives rise to
\delta_{[b]}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
via Corollary
7.3.7
, and
\gamma_{0}
is a degree
n
norm of
\delta_{[b]}
. If the hypothesis on
Z_{1}
in Lemma
7.3.6
is satisfied, and if
[b_{1}]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
[b]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
correspond under the bijection of that lemma, then the
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
\delta_{[b_{1}]}
maps to that of
\delta_{[b]}
.
We defined
\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0},[b])
to be
\kappa_{I_{0}}([b])
, which equals the image of
\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0,1},[b_{1}])
. Thus we have

\langle\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0,1},[b_{1}]),s_{1}\rangle=\langle\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0},[b]),s\rangle.

In case
G_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}
are unramified, the twisted datum
\widetilde{\mathfrak{e}}_{1}=(H_{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{1},\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{1})
of §
7.2.14
is also unramified, and
\widetilde{\eta}_{1}

induces
\mathbb{C}
-algebra morphisms

{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R_{1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1})\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R)\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1},\lambda_{H_{1}})

as in the untwisted case, cf. §
7.4.8
and §
7.4.10
. By slight abuse of notation we call both maps
\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{*}
.
The following is a twisted analogue of Proposition
7.4.11
.

Proposition 7.4.13
.

Let
f\in{\mathcal{H}}(R(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
. Then there exists
f^{H_{1}}\in{\mathcal{H}}(H_{1},\lambda_{H_{1}})
such that the following holds:
Let
\gamma_{H_{1}}\in H_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})_{(G_{1},H_{1})\textup{-reg}}
. We have
SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})=0
if
\gamma_{H_{1}}
is not a norm from
R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. If
\gamma_{H_{1}}
is a norm of
\delta_{1}\in R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})=G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})
then whenever
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is an image of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
, we have

(7.4.13.1)

SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})=\Delta_{0}(\gamma_{H_{1}},\gamma_{0})\sum_{[b]\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})}e(I_{\delta_{[b]}})\langle\beta_{p}(\gamma_{0},[b]),s\rangle TO_{\delta_{[b]}}(f).

Moreover if
G_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}
are unramified, and if
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R)
then the above is true for
f^{H_{1}}=\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{*}(f)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1},\lambda_{H_{1}})
.

Remark 7.4.14
.

In the essential case when
G_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}
are unramified with
f
in the unramified Hecke algebra, if
\gamma_{H_{1}}
is restricted to be a strongly
G_{1}
-regular element, the proposition is a special case of the twisted fundamental lemma (TFL) for the full unramified Hecke algebra. When the residue characteristic
p
is large, TFL for the unit element is true thanks to Ngô, Waldspurger, Cluckers-Loeser, and others (
[
Ngô10
,
Wal08
]
with
[
Wal06
]
or
[
CL10
]
). For general elements of unramified Hecke algebras, including the case of small
p
, TFL was recently established by Lemaire, Moeglin, and Waldspurger
[
LMW18
]
and
[
LW17
]
. Note that TFL states an orbital integral identity only for strongly regular elements; in fact the twisted transfer factors are not defined for other elements in general. However we want an orbital integral identity for
(G_{1},H_{1})
-regular semi-simple elements which may not be strongly regular. The transfer factors for such elements are available in our setting, and Proposition
7.4.13
is proved for such elements in
[
Mor10
, Ch. 9, App. A]
, under the hypotheses that
G_{\mathrm{der}}=G_{\mathrm{sc}}
and that
{\mathcal{H}}={}^{L}H
in the endoscopic datum.
So the point of our proof below is to remove the hypotheses of
[
Mor10
]
.
The basic idea is to employ
z
-extensions, but there is a technical problem: no single
z
-extension
G_{1}
satisfies the assumption of Lemma
7.3.6
for all (sufficiently large)
n
. We choose an auxiliary
z
-extension as in Lemma
7.2.13
to get around the issue.

Proof of Proposition
7.4.13
.

We present a proof when
G_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}
are unramified assuming that
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R)
.
The general case will be taken care of in the last paragraph of this proof.

Put
\widetilde{Z}_{1}:=\Res_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(Z_{1})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}}
. Choose
f_{1}
to be any preimage of
f
under the averaging map
{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R_{1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R)
over
\ker(R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow R(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))=\widetilde{Z}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Suppose that
z\gamma_{H_{1}}
is not a norm from
R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
for any
z\in Z_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Then
[
Mor10
, Prop. 9.5.1, Prop. A.3.14]
tells us that
SO_{z\gamma_{H_{1}}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{*}(f_{1}))=0
. Hence

SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{*}(f))=\int_{Z_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\lambda^{-1}_{H_{1}}(z)SO_{z\gamma_{H_{1}}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{1}^{*}(f_{1}))dz=0.

Now assume the existence of
z\in Z_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
such that
\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}:=z\gamma_{H_{1}}
is a norm of some element
\delta^{\prime}_{1}\in R_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
Let
\gamma_{0,1}\in G_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be an image of
\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}
. (Such a
\gamma_{0,1}
exists since
G_{1}
is quasi-split and has simply connected derived subgroup, cf.
[
Kot82
, Thm. 4.4]
.) Write
\gamma_{H}\in H(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
\gamma_{0}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
for the respective images of
\gamma_{H_{1}}
and
\gamma_{0,1}
. Take central extensions
G^{\prime}_{1}
and
H^{\prime}_{1}
as in Lemma
7.2.13
and write
Z^{\prime}_{1}
for the common kernel of the surjections
G^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow G
and
H^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow H
. Thus
Z^{\prime}_{1}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Res_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m}
for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
F_{i}\supset\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}
.
Set

\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{1}:=\Res_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(Z^{\prime}_{1})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}\hookrightarrow F_{i}}\Res_{F_{i}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{G}_{m},

where the second product runs over the set of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-embeddings. Fix an embedding
\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}\hookrightarrow F_{i}
for each
i
and define
Y_{1}
to be the subtorus of
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{1}
whose components outside the set of fixed embeddings are trivial. The Frobenius automorphism
\sigma\in{\rm Gal}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
acts on
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{1}
by permuting the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-embeddings.

The norm map
N:\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow Z^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is obviously onto and restricts to an isomorphism

N:Y_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})Z^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

From
{\mathfrak{e}}
, we have
{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}_{1}=(H^{\prime}_{1},{}^{L}H^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}_{1},\eta^{\prime}_{1})\in E(G_{1})
as in Lemma
7.2.9
. Since
s\in Z(\widehat{H})^{\Gamma}
we have that
s^{\prime}_{1}\in Z(\widehat{H}^{\prime}_{1})^{\Gamma}
. One builds a twisted endoscopic datum
\widetilde{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}_{1}
for
(R^{\prime}_{1},\theta^{\prime}_{1})
as in §
7.2.12
and §
7.2.14
.

Choose
f^{\prime}_{1}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R^{\prime}_{1})
to be a preimage of
f
under the averaging surjection
{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R^{\prime}_{1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(R)
.
We have

(\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{1})^{*}(f^{\prime}_{1})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H^{\prime}_{1}),\qquad f^{H^{\prime}_{1}}:=(\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{1})^{*}(f)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H^{\prime}_{1},\lambda_{H^{\prime}_{1}}).

The inclusion
H_{1}\hookrightarrow H^{\prime}_{1}
induces an isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H_{1},\lambda_{H_{1}})\cong{\mathcal{H}}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}(H^{\prime}_{1},\lambda_{H^{\prime}_{1}})

since the character
\lambda_{H^{\prime}_{1}}
restricts to
\lambda_{H_{1}}
.
The functions
f^{H^{\prime}_{1}}
and
f^{H_{1}}
correspond under the isomorphism. Clearly

SO_{\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}}(f^{H^{\prime}_{1}})=SO_{\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}})=SO_{\gamma_{H_{1}}}(f^{H_{1}}).

By
[
Mor10
, Prop. 9.5.1, Prop. A.3.14]
,

(7.4.14.1)

SO_{\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}}((\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{1})^{*}(f^{\prime}_{1}))=\sum_{\delta^{\prime}_{1}}e(I_{\delta^{\prime}_{1}})\Delta_{0}(\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}},\delta^{\prime}_{1})TO_{\delta^{\prime}_{1}}(f^{\prime}_{1}),

where the sum runs over a set of representatives for the
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
G^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p^{n}})=R^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
whose norm is
\gamma_{0,1}
. (Here we view
\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}
and
\gamma_{0,1}
as elements of
H^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
G^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via
H_{1}\subset H^{\prime}_{1}
and
G_{1}\subset G^{\prime}_{1}
.) Let
\delta\in R(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denote the image of
\delta^{\prime}_{1}
, and
y\in\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
an arbitrary element. We collect the following facts.

•

the set of representatives
\delta^{\prime}_{1}
in the sum is in bijection with
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0,1},G^{\prime}_{1};\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, and also with
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\gamma_{0},G;\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(Lemma
7.3.6
and Corollary
7.3.7
),

•

e(I_{y\delta^{\prime}_{1}})=e(I_{\delta})
by
[
Kot83
, Cor. (2)]
,

•

N(y)\gamma_{0,1}
(resp.
N(y)\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}
) is a norm of
y\delta^{\prime}_{1}
in
G^{\prime}_{1}
(resp.
H^{\prime}_{1}
),

•

(
7.4.14.1
) holds with
N(y)\gamma_{0,1}
and
N(y)\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}
in place of
\gamma_{0,1}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}}
,

•

\Delta_{0}(N(y)\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}},y\delta^{\prime}_{1})=\lambda_{H^{\prime}_{1}}(N(y))\Delta_{0}(\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}},\delta^{\prime}_{1})
by
[
KS99
, p. 53]
,
47
47

47

Unlike the formula in
loc. cit.
, we do not put an inverse over
\lambda_{H^{\prime}_{1}}
since we are following the convention for
\Delta^{\prime}
in
[
KS12
]
, which inverts
\Delta_{III}
in
[
KS99
]
.

•

\Delta_{0}(\gamma^{\prime}_{H_{1}},\gamma_{0,1})
remains the same whether it is viewed with respect to the transfer between
H^{\prime}_{1}
and
G^{\prime}_{1}
or between
H_{1}
and
G_{1}
.
48
48

48

To see this, one reduces to the strongly regular case by
[
LS87
, §4.3]
and
[
